Executive Summary
Concentrating Solar Power (CSP) with thermal energy storage offers a unique and strategic opportunity
to support California’s clean energy transition. Unlike photovoltaic (PV) systems, CSP with thermal
storage can generate electricity after sunset and during periods of high demand, making it a valuable
complement to intermittent renewable resources. CSP also provides synchronous, inertia-contributing
generation, long-duration storage, and flexible dispatch—capabilities increasingly important as thermal
plants retire. This report summarizes the findings of the CSP Plant Optimization Study for the California
Power Market or “CalCSP study,” which evaluated the technical, economic, environmental, and policy
factors that influence the deployment of CSP technologies in California.
The CalCSP study was conducted to assess how CSP can contribute to California’s long-term
decarbonization goals while enhancing grid reliability, supporting local economic development, and
making efficient use of land and transmission resources. It draws on detailed modeling of CSP
performance and costs, site suitability analysis, policy reviews, and stakeholder engagement across
utilities, regulators, developers, and community organizations. The analysis focuses on mature molten-
salt tower technology and incorporates lessons learned from the global CSP fleet, distinguishing today’s
CSP from earlier first-of-a-kind projects in the U.S.
Key findings include:
 • CSP is well suited to provide dispatchable, long-duration renewable energy, helping to mitigate
 the “duck curve” challenge and reduce reliance on fossil fuels during evening and nighttime.
 • Molten salt tower technology, with low cost and efficient molten salt thermal energy storage, is
 currently the most commercially viable and economic CSP technology to help California address
 its energy needs. CSP compares favorably to batteries for storage durations beyond 8 hours.
 • Capacity expansion modeling indicates that CSP is becoming more competitive, especially
 where grid reliability and firm capacity are prioritized. When included in planning models,
 NREL’s Regional Planning Model selected 14 GW of new baseload CSP capacity in the WECC.
 • Many sites in and around California are well-suited for CSP development, particularly in high
 solar resource regions with proximity to existing or planned transmission infrastructure. If
 California relaxes environmental screens in lower-sensitivity desert areas, significant
 opportunity for GW of cost-effective siting exists. Otherwise, the most economic siting
 opportunities exist outside of California in Arizona and New Mexico, but only if transmission
 access is available.
 • Environmental assessments show that CSP can be deployed with minimal environmental
 impact including low emissions, minimal water use with dry-cooled configurations, and limited
 ecological impact when sited responsibly.
 • Stakeholder engagement revealed significant interest in a resource like CSP from grid
 operators, state agencies, and various load serving entities; though concerns remain about
 permitting complexity, capital and operating costs, and historical project underperformance.
 • Policy changes currently being implemented in California should improve CSP deployment
 prospects, including the Reliable and Clean Power Procurement Program, which integrates
 Resource Adequacy and RPS procurement and creates a more predictable framework for new
 clean-capacity resources with long-duration energy storage such as CSP.
Taken together, the findings support a more prominent role for CSP in California’s evolving clean energy
landscape. With strategic planning, targeted policy support, and continued cost improvements, CSP can
complement PV and batteries to deliver reliable, around-the-clock clean electricity—especially in areas
with high solar resource and constrained grid capacity.

1 Introduction
Since about 2010 when the price of utility scale photovoltaic (PV) power became lower than the cost of
power from concentrating solar power (CSP) plants, no new CSP projects have been developed in the
United States. Utilities were looking to satisfy state-driven renewable portfolio standards (RPS) that
required a minimum amount of electricity to come from renewable sources and therefore looking to
contract the lowest cost renewable power available, at any time of day. As a result, utilities have not
valued the dispatchability or firm capacity that could be provided by CSP plants with integrated thermal
energy storage (TES) if there was sufficient natural gas plant capacity available to supply power when it
was needed. As California increased the amount of daytime solar power on the grid, it began to
experience what became known as the CAISO Duck Curve, see Figure 4. This created new issues to make
sure the state had sufficient ramping capability to ramp up to meet the system net load in the evening
and to ramp down in the morning to offset the increase in daytime power coming from solar. RPS
standards have reduced natural gas usage but have not significantly reduced natural gas capacity, which
still needs to be available to meet the evening peak demand.
As states increase their RPS requirements to meet carbon reduction goals, there is a growing need for
flexible and dispatchable renewable energy generation to replace natural gas usage and natural gas
power plant capacity. As California moves to meet its 60% RPS target in 2030, it has already added over
15 GW of 4-hour lithium-Ion batteries. As California and other states work to decarbonize their power
sectors, longer duration energy storage and other clean energy resources are likely to be needed. States
will need to move beyond simplistic least cost RPS procurement practices to achieve a reliable, low cost,
carbon free power sector.
California possesses world-class solar resources in its southern deserts. CSP technologies are well-
matched to these regions, and their dispatchability makes them a valuable tool for firming PV
generation and reducing evening ramping needs. The integration of TES within CSP distinguishes it from
PV + BESS in terms of thermal inertia, long-duration storage potential, and synchronous generation.

 Figure 1. The 125 MW Crescent Dunes Project Utilizing Molten-Salt Central Receiver/tower technology
 with 10 hours of thermal energy storage.

This project focused on looking at how CSP could best be used to help California meet its carbon
reduction targets, 100% of retail electric sales by 2045, which are some of the most aggressive in the
country. California represents an important potential market for CSP, it has the highest direct normal
solar resource, and about 70% of the CSP capacity constructed in the U.S. to date has been in California.

 Page 8 of 77

1.1 California Energy Planning Dynamics and the Role for CSP + TES
Compared to the rest of the nation, California’s energy resource planning is among the most complex
and integrated. Much of the state’s energy planning centers around SB 100 (De León)1, which mandates
that 100% of California’s retail electricity be met with eligible renewable resources by 2045, with 60% by
2030. While the state has made significant progress towards these goals, with 57% of generation being
met with renewables in 2024, nighttime demand and load growth from building and transportation
electrification, climate change impacts, and digital infrastructure growth (e.g. data centers) pose
significant challenges.

SB 100 requires state agencies to work in tandem to ensure smooth planning and implementation, with
many findings and recommendations from one process acting as inputs for another (see Figure 2).
Previously, concentrating solar power has not been considered in SB 100 Joint Agency Planning,
“because of the higher costs relative to solar photovoltaic and wind energy,” (2021 SB 100 Joint Agency
Report). New planning mechanisms are changing the potential role for CSP in California’s energy
portfolio, especially when paired with thermal energy storage.

 Figure 2. California state agencies’ and load-serving entities’ energy and emission reduction planning
 processes are closely integrated with one another.
Under SB 100, the “Joint Agencies” (CARB, CPUC, and CEC) are required to periodically submit to the
California Legislature a joint policy report that details the state’s progress in achieving 100% clean
energy by 2045. All load-serving entities (LSEs), or any organization that provides electricity to end-use
customers, must also comply with SB 100. Depending on the type of LSE, these entities may be required
to prepare and submit materials for compliance with the CPUC. The leading three LSE models in
California include the following:

1
    S.B. 100, 2017–2018 Reg. Sess., ch. 312, 2018 Cal. Stat.
 Page 9 of 77

 ● Investor-owned utilities (IOUs): Private, for-profit utility companies, including PG&E, SCE, and
 SDG&E. IOUs are regulated by the CPUC.

 ● Community choice aggregators (CCAs): Entities formed by local governments (e.g. cities and
 counties) to purchase electricity on behalf of residents. CCAs are reliant on the incumbent IOU
 for transmission, distribution, and billing services. CCAs receive CPUC oversight for SB 100
 compliance but are subject to local governance.

 ● Publicly-owned utilities (POUs): Public, not-for-profit utilities including LADWP, SMUD, etc. POUs
 are not regulated by the CPUC; they are subject primarily to local governance and SB 100
 oversight from the California Energy Commission (CEC).

  Figure 3. The three leading LSE models, IOUs, POUs, and CCAs, make up the majority of California’s
 energy retail sales. Note that despite 75% of California’s retail load being met by IOUs, approximately
 25% of this generation comes from CCAs operating within IOU territory and infrastructure.
Under the CPUC’s Integrated Resource Planning (IRP) process, LSEs (with the exception of POUs) are
required to submit plans showing how they will meet emission reduction targets, reliability needs under
Resource Adequacy (RA), and clean energy goals under Renewable Portfolio Standards (RPS). The CPUC
sets standards within these processes and uses LSE responses to plan accordingly.

Reliability needs are addressed through Resource Adequacy (RA), which is California’s regulatory
framework that ensures there are sufficient resources (plus a planning reserve margin, or PRM) available
on the grid. The legacy RA model was created around gas plants, which are generally capable of meeting
demand for as long as needed, provided that they are permitted to operate at the required output levels
– subject to air quality regulations and emissions limits. This model focused on meeting demand for a
single peak-hour and failed to address the dynamic, real-time needs of the electricity grid, especially as
more variable resources (e.g. wind, PV solar) get interconnected. In recent years, RA has shifted to the
“slice-of-day” (SoD) model, which more accurately encapsulates these evolving conditions by looking at
grid needs hour-to-hour. As a result, the SoD model provides more incentive to resources capable of
providing power in the evening hours when reduced solar power is available, such as those utilizing
storage technologies.

                                                                                             Page 10 of 77

The Renewable Portfolio Standard (RPS) requires load-serving entities to show how they plan to meet a
minimum percentage of electricity sales with eligible renewable resources. The CPUC sets these
percentages in the IRP process and are consistent with SB 100 targets. This process ensures that not
only are utilities meeting their reliability needs under RA, but that the generation they procure to do so
is becoming increasingly met with renewable resources.

A newly-proposed program at the CPUC, the Reliable and Clean Power Procurement Program (RCPPP),
more closely integrates RA and RPS to improve procurement processes by providing LSEs a predictable,
longer-term framework. Previously, the Integrated Resource Planning (IRP) and procurement processes
operated on an “order-by-order” basis, which discouraged load-serving entities (LSEs) from proactively
securing resources capable of meeting future load growth and evolving grid needs. The RCPPP shifts
toward a technology-neutral approach—departing from earlier IRP modeling that tended to favor
specific resource types identified in Joint Agency Planning. Under this new structure, any qualifying
resource can earn credits toward both the Clean Energy Standard (CES)—indicating zero-emissions
capability—and the reliability requirement, which reflects the resource’s ability to deliver dependable
and flexible capacity.

Under the new RCPPP framework, concentrating solar power with thermal energy storage (CSP + TES) is
now eligible for dual-attribute value recognition, as it provides capacity that qualifies for both CES and
reliability requirement credits. This creates a new competitive advantage for CSP + TES, especially when
compared to standalone PV solar, which can only earn CES credits; standalone storage, which can, at
best, earn restricted reliability requirement credits, as it struggles to meet long-duration needs; and
even PV plus storage, again eligible for CES but limited for reliability credit attribution.

As previously mentioned, the SoD model looks to better address hour-by-hour needs, which is especially
important for the evening peak and overall nighttime demand. As increasing PV solar generation gets
interconnected to the grid, there is a decreasing net load (total demand minus demand met with
renewable generation) in the middle of the day. This phenomenon has been dubbed the “duck curve”
(see Figure 4) for its unique belly-shaped dip in the middle of the day.

 CSP
 CSP

                                                                                             Page 11 of 77

  Figure 4. California’s Duck Curve. As more solar generation comes online, there is a decreased need for
 renewable generation in the middle of the day; however, the evening peak (the “head” of the duck) and
 nighttime demand (extending to the “tail”) remain largely met with fossil fuel generation.
While battery storage technologies are becoming crucial for meeting the evening peak, their limited
duration struggles to meet demand throughout the night before the sun rises, and solar generation
becomes abundant again. Lowering the “head” and “tail” of the duck remains as one of California’s
biggest challenges in meeting SB 100 targets and poses significant advantages for renewable resources
with storage beyond 4 to 8 hours.

As California continues to tune its planning processes, Figure 4 depicts how CSP + TES that is capable of
providing clean, reliable capacity throughout the night, becomes an increasingly stronger candidate for
overcoming the remaining challenges while receiving improved market incentives.

2 Project Objectives and Work Plan
The CalCSP project was developed to explore the strategic role that Concentrating Solar Power (CSP)
with thermal energy storage can play in California’s evolving electricity system. The study sought to
address key questions regarding CSP’s technical capabilities, economic competitiveness, environmental
footprint, and potential deployment pathways under California’s clean energy policies.

2.1 Project Objectives
The primary objectives of the CalCSP study were to:
 1. Demonstrate how CSP can support California’s SB 100 carbon reduction goals
 California has set a goal of achieving 100% carbon free power generation by 2045 or sooner.
 California expects to require a massive expansion of photovoltaic (PV) power in the state. This
 will provide most of its power needs during the day, but it will also need new carbon free power
 at night and for periods of extended cloudy weather. Although battery technologies are one of
 the key technologies that will be required, additional dispatchable carbon neutral technologies
 will be necessary to get to the 100% goal. These could be combustion turbines fueled by carbon
 neutral (green) fuels or that sequester carbon from combustion, or new long duration energy
 storage technologies. CSP could also be part of the technology mix. This project looks at how the
 next generation of CSP plants could be best optimized to support this need. This project focuses
 on near-term commercial CSP technologies to evaluate which CSP technologies and
 configurations are most likely to provide the value for future market needs. The project will also
 compare CSP technologies to the likely alternative non-carbon dispatchable power configuration
 for nighttime generation, PV + battery energy storage systems (BESS).
 2. Determine where CSP projects should be sited
 This study evaluated regions where CSP plants could be developed to identify the most
 promising sites and determine whether CSP could serve as a significant resource for carbon-free
 power in California. The project sought to identify the best sites—those that could provide the
 necessary power, minimize cost and environmental impacts, account for factors that might limit
 CSP development, and offer the greatest economic benefits to the State of California. One key
 aspect was to identify sites that are likely to have transmission capacity available for new CSP
 plants.
 3. Identify the barriers to deployment of CSP

                                                                                             Page 12 of 77

 Traditional least-cost power procurement approaches used to contract new power generation in
 California (and the U.S.) do not typically realize the value of CSP plants with thermal energy
 storage (TES). This project will evaluate the barriers that impede CSP deployment and evaluate
 strategies that CSP developers and policymakers can use to support a rapid expansion of CSP
 capacity, while minimizing the cost of power to California.
 4. Inform stakeholders about the potential of CSP
 Many of the key stakeholders that are planning for how California will achieve its SB 100 goals,
 either do not have a good idea of the potential for CSP to support California’s energy needs, or
 they may have pre-existing perceptions about the technology that may not be up to date. As a
 result, although CSP is acknowledged as a relevant technology for SB 100, it has not been one of
 the primary technologies evaluated for meeting its targets. The project reached out to the key
 stakeholder groups involved in plotting the path to address the SB 100 goals to help provide
 information to help CSP technologies be fairly evaluated against other technology options.

2.2 Work Plan
 Task 1: CSP Stakeholder Advisory Group (SAG)
 An advisory group was formed, made up of key stakeholders from California agencies (CPUC,
 CEC, CAISO), California utilities, and other interested stakeholders, to provide input and
 feedback to the work effort and deliverables. The SAG members were given a survey at the
 beginning of the project to provide input and direction to the project. Portions of the SAG
 helped review the various outputs from the project to provide continued feedback and
 direction. In addition, the results from the study were shared with the SAG members to help
 build informed support for CSP in the key stakeholder entities participating.
 Task 2: CSP and Alternative Plant Conceptual Designs
 Conceptual designs were developed for CSP and alternative plant configurations to be
 evaluated. The power market needs were reviewed to define the technical needs for
 dispatchable generators. Then reference designs, performance models, cost models, and
 financial models were developed for each technology, using consistent assumptions across
 technologies where possible. The goal was to enable individual technologies to be optimized to
 meet the market objectives and then to be able to compare the cost and performance of
 technologies on a consistent basis.
 Task 3: California System Capacity Expansion Modeling
 NREL used its Regional Planning Model (RPM), a capacity expansion model, to evaluate the
 potential for CSP to support California’s SB 100 goals. The western interconnect (WECC) was
 modeled in RPM to determine which technologies would be deployed between 2025 and 2045.
 The detailed zonal capabilities in RPM were used in specific zones of interest for CSP
 development. Two SB 100 scenarios were developed and run in RPM, one without CSP and one
 with CSP included as an option. RPM was used to identify which configurations of CSP were
 most attractive, how CSP would be dispatched to meet system load, where CSP was most likely
 to be deployed, and what the sensitivity to the cost of CSP was.
 Task 4: CSP Siting Analysis
 A siting assessment was conducted to determine where CSP projects might be constructed. A
 process was used like the approach used by the California Energy Commission to identify the
 resource potential for utility scale photovoltaics and wind power. The CEC approach uses a
                                                                                            Page 13 of 77

Final Technical Report DE-EE0009809

 graphical information systems assessment to identify areas where resources can be sited by
 elimination of areas that cannot be used. CSP resource siting potential was determined for each
 of the California Independent System Operator’s (CAISO) transmission planning regions. The
 analysis was expanded to include other western states including Arizona, Nevada, New Mexico,
 Utah, Colorado, and Texas. An economic assessment of the potential cost of energy was
 performed for each potential site to help identify the resource potential cost curve for each
 CAISO transmission planning region and state.
 Task 5: CSP Implementation Analysis
 This task looked at different approaches that could be used to accelerate the adoption of CSP
 technology. Policies were reviewed that have been used in the past to support the development
 of CSP in the U.S. and that might be applied or adapted to support future CSP deployments. The
 goal of this task was to evaluate different pathways that encourage CSP deployment and
 minimize the cost of meeting state goals. The task looked at the potential to use public private
 partnerships to implement state or federal power park sites that are predeveloped with
 infrastructure, the cost reduction potential for building power parks (multiple projects at the
 same location), evaluated different financing options that might be used to reduce the cost of
 project financing, the impact of changing US tax policies was evaluated (the IRA and the OBBBA),
 the impact of different power purchase agreement (PPA) contract structures such as energy only
 vs. energy and capacity payments, or tolling structures.
 Task 6: Screening Analysis
 This task compared the different CSP technology options for both night dispatch operation
 profile and a baseload operation profile. All technologies were compared for a Mojave Desert
 site. The most competitive CSP technology identified was then used as the reference technology
 for evaluation of CSP technology for all sites.
 Task 7: Detailed CSP Assessment
 This task took the CSP technology in the Budget Period 1 screening study and conducted a more
 detailed technoeconomic assessment of the technology for meeting CA SB 100 goals. This
 included a more detailed cost model; an improved performance model optimization, to make
 sure it accurately reflects the operating performance profile delivered by the plant during
 transient conditions; a more detailed and realistic IPP financial model that more accurately
 reflects the project finances and cost of power. The siting analysis was updated, and resource
 potentials were calculated for each CAISO transmission planning region and southwestern state.
 Task 8: Dissemination of Information
 This task focused on the dissemination of the study results. A focused outreach was targeted to
 the CSP stakeholder advisory group members and their organizations through targeted
 meetings. In addition, a larger,, more generalized outreach was made through the development
 of specific materials and published reports documenting the key findings of the study. Finally, an
 industry webinar was given to provide the results to the public.

                                                                                           Page 14 of 77

3 CSP Technology Assessment
Concentrating Solar Power (CSP) technologies convert direct normal irradiance (DNI) from the sun into
thermal energy, which is used in a heat engine to generate electricity. An important distinguishing
feature for some CSP technologies is their ability to integrate thermal energy storage, which enables a
separation between when energy is collected and when it is used to produce power. As a result, CSP
plants with TES can provide dispatchable, on-demand power delivery—a key advantage over variable
renewable energy (VRE) sources like PV and wind. This section summarizes the current state of CSP
technology, focusing on commercially mature systems and near-term emerging technologies that could
be deployed in the next 5 to 10 years. Four CSP technologies were selected to evaluate. Parabolic trough
plants with indirect molten-salt TES and molten-salt central receiver/tower plants are viewed as
commercially mature technologies. For newer emerging technologies, the study evaluated a multi-tower
configuration that uses sodium as a heat transfer fluid in the tower receivers and an indirect molten-salt
TES, and a parabolic trough plant that uses molten-salt as the heat transfer fluid in the solar field,
allowing direct TES.

3.1 Parabolic Trough Systems with Indirect Thermal Energy Storage
Figure 5 shows the Solana Generating Station near Gila Bend, Arizona, a 250 MW parabolic trough CSP
plant with 6 hours of TES. Figure 6 shows the typical process flow for a parabolic trough plant with
indirect TES. These plants use fields of collectors with parabolic shaped mirrors to focus sunlight on a
receiver tube containing a heat transfer fluid (a synthetic oil) that is heated as it passes though the
collector field. This is often referred to as an “Oil Trough” plant. This plant includes a two-tank indirect
molten-salt TES system. Molten salt is heated with hot oil coming from the solar field in an oil to salt
heat exchanger. The storage is then discharged later to produce power at night or augment the solar
field during low insolation periods by running hot salt back through the heat exchanger to reheat the oil.
Hot HTF from the solar field or the TES is used to produce steam, in a series of heat exchangers known
as a steam generator, to run a conventional Rankine cycle steam turbine to produce electricity.
Approximately 80 parabolic trough plants have been constructed globally, approximately half include
TES. Parabolic trough technology is generally viewed as the most mature and lowest technical risk of the
CSP plant configurations. Because the TES is charged and discharged via a heat exchanger, it is often
referred to as indirect molten-salt TES. While lower in temperature, 735°F (391°C) and efficiency, trough
power plants are a mature technology with decades of operating history.

 Figure 5. 250 MW Solana Parabolic Trough Power Plant with 6 hours of Thermal Storage
 near Gila Bend, AZ

                                                                                              Page 15 of 77

 Figure 6. Parabolic Trough Power Plant with TES System Process Diagram

3.2 Molten Salt Power Towers/Central Receiver Systems
Figure 7 shows the Noor III power station located near Ouarzazate, Morocco, a 150 MW molten salt
(MS) tower plant with 7 hours of TES. Figure 8 shows the process diagram for a molten-salt tower plant.
It uses a central receiver mounted atop a tower to collect concentrated sunlight from a field of tracking
mirrors (heliostats). The receiver heats molten salt directly, which is stored in insulated salt storage
tanks. Hot salt can then be dispatched when needed, set to a series of heat exchangers (steam
generator), to produce steam to run a Rankine cycle steam turbine. Because it directly heats the salt up
to 1050°F (565°C), the technology achieves the highest operating temperature of any current technology
with TES. As a result, it achieves a higher power cycle efficiency and has a significantly lower TES cost
than parabolic trough technology. The main drawback of the MS tower plant is that sunlight is
concentrated on the receiver at the top of a 700-foot-tall tower. This is visible tens of miles away, which
limits the locations where it can be installed. Additionally, there are high flux zones between the mirrors
and receiver that are hazardous to avian species. MS tower technology has fewer plants in commercial
operation at this point, 13 at the end of 2024, and fewer years of operational experience. A significant
number of new MS tower plants have been constructed and commissioned in the last 5 to 10 years, and
dozens are currently under construction or development in China. MS tower technology has emerged as
the leading CSP technology being deployed in the market.

                                                                                             Page 16 of 77

 Figure 7. Noor III Molten Salt Power Tower near Ouarzazate, Morocco (source: ACWA)

 Figure 8. Molten Salt Central Receiver Power Tower System Process Diagram

3.3 Distributed tower systems
There are several distributed tower concepts currently under development. The molten-salt tower is
limited in size by optical performance of the heliostat field and the height of the tower. Current molten-
salt tower solar fields go up to about 600 MWt in size. This limits the power block to about 100 MWe for
a baseload configuration or potentially 150 MWe for a night dispatch configuration. Potentially the solar
plant’s thermal rating could be increased to 800 MWt or even more, but with diminishing returns as
distance from the TES system and power block grows with each additional tower. One scenario is to
build multiple MS tower solar fields that tie to a single power block. One project in China for example
combines three molten-salt tower solar fields to a single 350 MWe power block. The multi-tower
configuration is one way to get around the size limit of individual heliostat fields.
                                                                                            Page 17 of 77

Another multitower configuration being developed in Australia uses smaller modular polar distributed
towers with cavity receivers. This plant uses sodium as the heat transfer fluid in the tower receiver.
Figure 9 shows the demonstration plant that operated for several years demonstrating the operability of
the sodium multi-tower plant. Figure 10 shows the process diagram for the sodium multi-tower plant. It
includes an indirect molten-salt TES. A heat exchanger is used to transfer heat from the sodium to the
molten-salt TES. A separate molten-salt to steam generation system, similar to the MS tower plant, is
used to dispatch thermal energy to produce steam to run the Rankine cycle steam turbine. This
approach could use 50 or 100 modular fields to tie into a single power plant. The use of sodium as the
heat transfer fluid creates some design and safety considerations but adds significant flexibility to the
operation and control of the multi-tower solar field. A 30 MWe commercial demonstration project, with
6 modular towers, and 7 hours of TES is currently under development in Australia.

 Figure 9. Sodium Distributed Tower Demonstration Project Ford, Australia (Source: Vast)

  Figure 10. Distributed Tower using Sodium HTF and Molten-Salt TES Process Diagram (Source: Vast)

3.4 Parabolic trough systems with molten salt HTF
Conventional parabolic trough technology is limited by the upper operating temperature of synthetic
hydrocarbon based HTF to about 750°F (400°C). One idea that has been under development for the last
30 years is to use molten salt as the HTF in the parabolic trough solar field. Figure 11 shows the process
diagram for a parabolic trough plant that replaces the oil HTF with a molten salt HTF. This eliminates the
need for the oil-to-salt heat exchanger to charge or discharge the TES system. The molten-salt HTF
allows the solar field to operate at significantly higher operating temperatures of 1022°F (550°C),
compared to about 735°F (391°C) for the oil trough. This allows the power cycle to operate more
                                                                                            Page 18 of 77

efficiently (up to about 41.6% compared to 39.3%). But even more important, it significantly reduces the
cost of the TES and the HTF system, as the higher temperature provides about 2.5X greater energy
density. The main risk with molten salt is that it freezes at about 435°F. So, the entire HTF system and
solar field must be designed to recover from a potential salt freeze event. Molten salt HTF has been
tested at pilot scale with some success but never at full commercial scale. As a result, this configuration
represents significant technical risk. However, it has a siting advantage as it lacks the tall towers of the
MS tower plant.

 Figure 11. Parabolic Trough using Molten-salt HTF

3.5 CSP Technologies not Evaluated
Steam central receiver technology and dish engine CSP technologies were not included, due to their lack
of efficient and cost-effective thermal energy storage. The study did not consider linear Fresnel
technology because it has more limited commercial deployments, and there is not see any significant
advantage over conventional parabolic trough or MS tower technology. There are also next generation
CSP technologies currently under development. The U.S. DOE has been developing the CSP Gen3 solid,
liquid, and gas pathways. These technologies operate at higher temperatures above 700°C to enable the
use of more efficient supercritical CO₂ power cycles in place of conventional Rankine steam power
cycles. Although these offer several potential advantages and cost reductions, the technology, both
solar and power cycle, is still at an early prototype demonstration stage. These technologies seem
unlikely to achieve broad commercial deployment within the next ten years.

3.6 Lessons from the Concentrating Solar Power Best Practices Study
CSP technology is at a relatively early stage of technical maturity. Many early CSP projects have suffered
from reliability and maintenance issues related to the early-stage nature of the technology. In 2020, the
U.S. Department of Energy published the Concentrating Solar Power Best Practices Study, a
comprehensive assessment of past CSP project experiences, with the goal of identifying actionable

                                                                                              Page 19 of 77

Final Technical Report DE-EE0009809

guidance for future development.2 The study concluded that while CSP projects are inherently complex,
their success hinges more on project execution than on fundamental technical limitations. Key
recommendations included working with experienced development teams, establishing robust EPC
contracts and Owner’s Technical Specifications, and involving operations and maintenance (O&M)
personnel early in the design process. The study also emphasized the importance of engineering
systems—such as the steam generation system—for the full range of transient thermal and operational
behaviors they are likely to encounter in real-world conditions.
Although the study acknowledged a few remaining design-related challenges for parabolic trough and
molten-salt tower technologies, it determined that these could be addressed through appropriate
engineering and equipment selection. Based on this assessment, the authors expressed confidence that
future tower and trough CSP plants can be constructed on schedule and deliver reliable long-term
performance, if projects are well-managed and follow established best practices.

3.7 Technology Selection for Detailed Review: Molten Salt Central Receiver
Following a comparative assessment of CSP technology alternatives (described in Chapter 4), molten salt
central receiver (tower) systems were selected as the focus for detailed analysis in this study. While
parabolic trough systems are more established and technically mature, they operate at lower
temperatures and rely on indirect thermal energy storage (TES) using synthetic oil, resulting in higher
storage costs and lower efficiency. In contrast, molten salt tower systems enable higher operating
temperatures and more efficient power conversion, paired with a cost-effective two-tank direct storage
configuration. These attributes make them particularly well-suited for baseload and night dispatch
applications in a decarbonized grid. Although other emerging configurations—such as sodium-based
distributed towers or molten salt troughs—may offer similar or even lower costs, they remain less
commercially mature. Given the need to balance cost, maturity, and deployment potential, molten salt
towers were selected as the most viable CSP technology for evaluating market opportunities in
California and the broader Western U.S.

3.8 Molten salt central receiver technology assessment
Note: This section provides a high-level summary. For the detailed assessment, refer to the companion
document: Annex A – Technical Assessment of Availability Issues in Commercial Molten-Salt Central
Receiver Projects.
Molten salt central receiver plants with TES offer dispatchable, zero-carbon electricity and are
increasingly relevant to California’s long-duration energy storage needs. While the commercial value
proposition of CSP with TES remains strong—especially in high-DNI regions—first-generation molten salt
tower plants faced significant availability and reliability challenges. This section summarizes lessons
learned from those early deployments.
The U.S. pioneered molten salt tower technology through Solar Two, which demonstrated operational
viability in the late 1990s. Since then, the commercial scale-up began with Spain’s Gemasolar and the
110 MW Crescent Dunes plant in Nevada. These were followed by other large, international projects
including Noor III (Morocco), Cerro Dominador (Chile), DEWA IV (UAE), and Redstone (South Africa).

2
 Mehos, Mark, Hank Price, Robert Cable, David Kearney, Bruce Kelly, Gregory Kolb, and Frederick Morse. 2020.
Concentrating Solar Power Best Practices Study. Golden, CO: National Renewable Energy Laboratory. NREL/TP-
5500-75763. https://www.nrel.gov/docs/fy20osti/75763.pdf.
                                                                                                 Page 20 of 77

China now leads in global deployment of molten salt central receiver plants, with multiple plants in
operation, and several gigawatts under construction, driving industrial learning and cost reductions.
Two components emerged as the most critical technical barriers to availability:
 • Hot Salt Storage Tanks suffered from buckling, weld cracking, and premature creep-fatigue
 failure—largely due to inadequate design codes, insufficient floor thickness, and poor thermal
 control during commissioning. Improved tank designs now incorporate smaller diameters,
 thicker floors, better weld QA/QC, enhanced thermal monitoring, and foundation materials that
 mitigate salt leaks and NOx formation.
 • Salt-to-Steam Generators experienced thermal fatigue, corrosion, and tube-to-tubesheet
 failures due to flat-head designs and poor start-up control. Modern solutions emphasize all-
 welded heat exchangers, improved hydraulic control schemes, and strict thermal ramp rate
 limits.
Other reliability concerns addressed include:
 • Heliostats, which are now more mature as a result of improved design standards and initiatives
 like HelioCon, though challenges remain around optical calibration, wind loading, and closed-
 loop control.
 • Heat Trace Systems, which have caused outages due to poor installation and control. Best
 practices now call for redundancy, PID control, and better vendor coordination.
 • Salt Valve Selection and Pipe Supports, where improper designs have led to vibration, freezing,
 and erosion. Globe valves with bellows seals remain the most reliable solution for both flow
 control and isolation.
 • Receiver Panels and Molten Salt Pumps, by contrast, have generally performed well in
 commercial plants, with lessons from Solar Two informing improved materials, modularity, and
 maintenance strategies.
While early tower CSP plants fell short of their availability targets, newer plants are demonstrating
substantial improvements in annual generation. The gains are attributable to better system design,
O&M practices, and global knowledge sharing. The accompanying Annex A provides detailed analysis of
component-specific failure modes and engineering solutions and is intended to guide future CSP plant
design and deployment in California and beyond.

                                                                                            Page 21 of 77

4 Cost and Performance Modeling
Note: This section provides a high-level summary of the cost and performance modeling conducted. For
detailed analysis and assumptions, refer to Appendix B: California CSP Study Performance Modeling
Inputs and Assumptions.
Understanding the cost competitiveness and operational performance of Concentrating Solar Power
(CSP) with thermal energy storage is central to evaluating its role in California’s clean energy future. The
CalCSP modeling effort examined a range of CSP configurations under California-specific conditions to
estimate lifecycle costs, storage capabilities, dispatch profiles, and system value across various
deployment scenarios.

4.1 Modeling Framework
The cost and performance analysis was conducted using a combination of:
 • NREL’s System Advisor Model (SAM) for baseline techno-economic modeling
 • Custom dispatch modeling tools for hourly grid value and capacity assessment
 • Input assumptions drawn from recent CSP project data, vendor specifications, and DOE-funded
 R&D benchmarks

4.2 CSP Technology Screening Analysis
The initial modeling effort evaluated the existing commercial CSP technologies and new emerging CSP
technologies that might be commercial in the next 5 to 10 years. The goal was to determine which
technologies were likely to be most competitive for addressing California’s decarbonization needs. The
following CSP technologies were discussed in chapter 3 and were evaluated:
 • Standard oil heat transfer fluid (HTF) parabolic trough plants with indirect molten-salt TES. Cost
 estimates were based on internal cost numbers received from developers.
 • Molten-salt tower, central receiver plants with direct molten-salt TES. Cost models are based on
 data from international projects extrapolated to the U.S. market. The study evaluated two
 molten-salt configurations, an unconstrained land case with a 600 MWt molten salt receiver,
 and a square mile land constrained tower case.
 • Sodium HTF distributed tower technology, with indirect molten-salt TES. Preliminary cost and
 performance data for a single configuration were received. The project was unable to model the
 cost and performance of this technology. But it appears to have the potential to be lower cost
 than existing molten-salt tower technology. It represents an interesting option for future cost
 reduction.
 • Molten-salt HTF parabolic trough plants with direct molten-salt TES. An internal cost model was
 used for molten-salt trough technology that was developed as part of the DOE funded project
           DE-EE0008140.
4.2.1 Baseload Dispatch
A parametric analysis was conducted by varying the size of the solar field, thermal storage, and power
cycle to determine the minimum cost for any capacity factor for each of the four technology cases
evaluated (Oil trough, MS trough, 600 MWt MS tower, and square mile MS tower). The goal was to
evaluate higher capacity-factor, or baseload plant configurations. Figure 12 shows the real LCOE results
of the screening analysis for baseload plant configurations as a function of the capacity factor. The
molten-salt tower configurations were significantly lower cost than the oil based parabolic trough
technology. Although the MS trough configuration appeared to result in slightly lower costs than the
molten-salt tower technology, the study concluded that, given the uncertainty of the cost and
                                                                                              Page 22 of 77

Final Technical Report DE-EE0009809

performance assumptions and the pre-commercial status of the technology, this difference should not
be considered meaningful. Only a single point was available for the Vast distributed tower technology.
While the cost appears attractive, it is based on pre-commercial estimates and may also reflect early-
stage optimism.

 Figure 12. Baseload CSP Technology Comparison

generates electricity at night. The allowable generation hours are shown in Figure 13, and these are the
hours when a standalone PV plant is not generating electricity (based on a SAM PV model).

systems see an increase in LCOE compared to the baseload cases because of the larger storage sizes

4.3 Detailed technoeconomic assessment of CSP vs. PV + BESS
To answer the question of whether CSP plants are economically competitive in the marketplace, it is
important to make sure any comparison made uses similar power supply products. For this comparison,
the study compared CSP (MS tower technology) to PV + BESS configurations that provide similar output
profiles. Both baseload and night dispatch configurations were evaluated.
Figure 15 shows a comparison of the LCOE for baseload configurations, including PV + BESS, standalone
CSP, and hybrid CSP + PV configurations that all achieve a 75% annual capacity factor. Since PV and BESS
are expected to continue to decrease in cost over time, the LCOE based on both 2025 and 2030 cost
from the 2024 NREL Annual Technol Baseline Midline costs are presented. For CSP, the study evaluated
the LCOE for a single plant and for a 4-plant power park. The Hybrid CSP + PV configuration is a night
dispatch CSP plant configuration with PV added for daytime generation. The hybrid LCOEs are shown for
hybrid plants with a single CSP plant and a power park with 4 CSP plants. Note the analysis assumed 30%
ITC for all technologies.

                                                                                          Page 25 of 77

Final Technical Report DE-EE0009809

 Figure 15. LCOE Results for Baseload configurations with 75% Annual Capacity Factor

The analysis assumes the following system configurations:
 • PV + BESS: 100 MWAC, PV 380 MWDC, BESS 1800 MWhDC (useable capacity)
 • CSP Only: Receiver 600 MWt, Turbine 80 MWe (gross), TES 16 hrs
 • CSP + PV Hybrid: Receiver 600 MWt, Turbine 140 MW MWe (gross), TES 14 hrs, PV 280 MW DC

Figure 15 shows that the CSP plant is expected to be more cost competitive than the PV + BESS plant
providing the same baseload power output. Current CSP costs appear to be competitive with PV+BESS
costs in the 2030 timeframe. CSP power park configurations further reduce the LCOE of CSP technology
by 15%. The results show that hybridizing CSP with PV can further improve the economics of the
combined system. The detailed results are shown in Table 2.

5 Capacity Expansion Modelling for California
Note: This section provides a high-level summary of the capacity expansion modelling conducted by
NREL. For more details refer to Annex C - “Deployment Potential of Concentrating Solar Power
Technologies in California.”
In 2021, the California Energy Commission (CEC) released a joint agency report 3 that included a study of
potential capacity expansion pathways to achieving the carbon reduction goals established in SB 100
which excluded CSP as an option. However, the dispatchability of CSP’s power cycles and low cost of TES
compared to batteries would make CSP a cost-competitive option for complementing variable
generation and provision of ancillary services to the grid 4. This section details the study NREL conducted
to assess the deployment potential of CSP in future capacity planning to meet the revised California RPS.
The full details of the study are available in Annex C and a journal paper published in Sustainability.5

5.1 Resource planning model
NREL employed its Regional Planning Model (RPM), a utility-scale capacity expansion model developed
at NREL 6. The main advantage that RPM has over other capacity expansion models is that it can model
the region of interest at nodal fidelity rather than zonal. Figure 16 displays the Western Interconnection
as a nodal and zonal representation; our study includes the nodes inside the shape on the left-hand side,
connected to the zones outside the black shape on the right-hand side. This adds high-fidelity modeling
of transmission and distribution within California and incorporates connections to generators outside
the state while maintaining tractability.

5.2 RPM Analysis and Results
The goal of this study was to demonstrate the deployment potential of CSP technologies in California
when it is included in capacity expansion models under an SB 100 scenario. The main runs for this study
included two core scenarios: one in which CSP technologies are included in the model, and one in which
CSP technologies are not allowed to deploy. The NREL analysis allowed RPM to select CSP configurations
that had 100 MWe capacity plants with multiple options for solar multiple (1-3) and hours of TES (6-18).
Table 9 below shows the configurations available to RPM, which then selects the configuration that is
most competitive depending on grid demands.

Figure 17 shows the capacity difference between the CSP and the No CSP runs. The difference in
capacity deployment of and generation from CSP plants between the two scenarios demonstrates the
impact of including CSP technologies in the grid analysis. A positive value in the plot denotes a relative
increase in a technology’s deployment in the CSP run, and a negative value in the plot denotes a relative
decrease in a technology’s deployment in the CSP run, compared to the No CSP run. When CSP
technologies are included, RPM first builds about 2 GWe of installed CSP capacity in 2030. The amount
of installed CSP grows steadily after that, reaching a cumulative value of 9.1 GWe by 2045,
approximately 6% of the state’s generating capacity. Figure 17 shows that CSP mostly replaces wind,
stand-alone solar PV, and stand-alone battery storage capacity when it is included. The 9.1 GWe of CSP
capacity displaces about 30 GWe of other technologies. The CSP capacity is smaller because CSP has a
higher capacity factor and CSP capacity includes both energy and storage whereas energy and storage
are separate with PV/wind and batteries. CSP generates approximately 60 terawatt-hours-electric
(TWhe) of energy in 2045, representing approximately 15% of California’s electric generation.

Figure 18 shows the quantities of CSP installed in the different utility service territories in California.

selected some other configurations with smaller solar multiples for some of the locations outside of
California. Future analysis might look at candidate configurations with smaller increments of TES and
solar multiples, to see if different configurations are more optimized.

 Figure 18. CSP installed capacity in California under SB 100 by utility region using RPM

                                                                                              Page 32 of 77

A CSP plant with 12 hours of storage capacity acts like a load-following generator on the grid. This is
confirmed by Figure 19, which shows the aggregated charging and dispatch for all CSP installed capacity
in California under SB 100 in RPM for 2045. The CSP plants charge during the day at rates higher than
the plant’s installed capacity due to the high solar multiple in the deployed configuration. The CSP plants
operate at close to maximum capacity during the afternoon and evening hours for all representative
days. This is the time of day when generation from solar PV is falling while consumer demand is
increasing, leading to a severe ramp in non-variable generation requirements. The RPM results show
that CSP plays a substantial role in supporting the grid during this critical time of day. Except for the
peak day in Figure 19, CSP generation is suppressed during each representative day’s midday because of
the abundance of solar PV generation at that time.

 Figure 19. RPM CSP Power Dispatch Schedules in 2045
The RPM analysis modeled all WECC. Figure 20 shows the nodal locations for where CSP capacity was
added. Inside California, the circles are located at the substation nodes on the CAISO grid where capacity
is added. Outside of California, the circle is located at the geographic center of the balancing authority.
The size and color of the circles represents the MW capacity deployed at that node. Since California
imports approximately 20% of its energy on an annual basis, it is possible that some of the capacity
added outside of California is also supplying power to California.

                                                                                             Page 33 of 77

 Figure 20. NREL RPM Analysis Results: CSP Deployment Locations
Table 5 summarizes the deployment of CSP to the grid when it is included as an option and shows that
RPM selects deployment of more than 13 gigawatts of CSP capacity by 2045 across the Western
Interconnection; in this scenario, the technology generates about 15% of in-state electricity production
for California in 2045. Compared to the scenario without CSP, the new deployments displace wind, PV
and batteries, which is consistent with the technoeconomic analysis in Section 2 suggesting that CSP is a
more economical option to replace fossil-fueled, net-load-following plants with nighttime dispatch
capabilities. Dispatch results indicate that the deployed CSP plants, which mostly have a 2.0 solar
multiple and 12 hours of TES, operate like load-following plants that maximize output in the late
afternoon and early evening hours in the summertime when net load is highest. However, Table 5 shows
that the deployment of CSP is highly elastic to technology cost, highlighting the potential benefit of goals
such as the SunShot 2030 target from the U.S. Department of Energy to the CSP industry.
A sensitivity analysis was also performed in which the technology cost of CSP was increased by 10% and
by 20% to determine the elasticity of CSP installations to currently forecasted cost trends.

             % of Baseline                     100%                52%                27%

The results showed that outside of California, CSP deployments were well-distributed in the Western
Interconnection except for Arizona, which has a relatively permissive RPS target in 2045 of 8.8%, and
New Mexico, which has access to low-cost wind resources. In addition to the higher-than-expected CSP
deployment in the Western Interconnection, a surprising finding was the lack of installation of hydrogen
combustion turbines, a part of the future asset mix in the joint report which used the RESOLVE model.
Future work may include an investigation of more aggressive RPS targets, including removing the ability
of natural gas or other fossil-fueled sources to offset system losses. A future study could also leverage
the insights of RPM’s higher-fidelity information about nodal system deployment to determine future
transmission and distribution needs that would be coupled with a capacity expansion plan for California.

5.3 RPM Analysis Conclusions
When included in the analysis, CSP plays a significant role in California under SB 100.
 • New CSP capacity is added starting in 2030 and steady growth continues through 2045.
 • Up to ~9 GW of CSP is deployed by 2045 in California (6% of generation capacity), providing
 approximately 15% of the net generation for the state.
 • CSP mainly offsets wind, PV, and battery capacity additions and generation.
 • Additional 4.5 GW of CSP plants are deployed in the rest of the Western Interconnection (mostly
 NV).
 • Multiple high-capacity factor plant design (SM=3.0) with 12 hours of TES were selected.
 • CSP deployments are split between the CA central valley (40%) and desert regions (60%).
 • CSP plants generally run at 100% output at night and at reduced load during the day.
 • Results are sensitive to the capital cost of a CSP system, but gigawatts of deployments remain
 present in results for California under higher-cost scenarios.

                                                                                             Page 35 of 77

Final Technical Report DE-EE0009809

6 Siting and Land Use Analysis
Note: This section provides a high-level summary. For detailed interview and engagement materials,
refer to the companion document: Annex D – CSP Siting Assessment.
Siting is a critical factor in the successful deployment of Concentrating Solar Power (CSP) projects. CSP
systems require high direct normal irradiance (DNI), substantial contiguous land areas, and access to
high-voltage transmission—while also needing to avoid conflicts with environmental, cultural, and
community constraints. The CalCSP siting and land use analysis was designed to identify and
characterize the most promising areas for CSP development in California and nearby states based on a
systematic, GIS-based screening process.
This section provides an overview of the siting and land use analysis conducted in this effort. For
detailed site screening criteria, GIS layers, and results, refer to the Siting and Land Use Analysis Report. 12

6.1 CSP Siting Assessment
The CSP siting assessment conducted in this study generally followed an approach like the one used by
the CEC to identify land that would potentially be suitable for siting photovoltaic plants. Although the
approach is similar, it has been adapted to account for the unique siting requirements of a CSP plant,
specifically a molten-salt tower power plant. The molten-salt tower represents the current state of the
art in CSP power plant technology. The molten-salt tower offers the most flexibility in designing a plant
to meet the unique power needs of the California market and is likely the most cost competitive. The tall
central receiver also adds significant considerations for siting of such plants.
The intent of this effort is to identify the resource potential for large amounts of CSP for California. So,
the study was not looking for the best site for a single plant, but rather areas where CSP plants could be
developed. CSP projects have significant economies of scale, so larger projects and the ability to
construct multiple projects sequentially in a single location are desirable to help drive costs down. The
siting analysis initially focused on California specifically but was expanded to include Arizona, Nevada,
and New Mexico, as a significant portion of California’s power is imported from surrounding states, and
these states also offer significant opportunity for siting of CSP plants.
The siting analysis is broken into a number of steps: gridding of land, initial screening of land that is not
suitable for CSP, screening of land for land use or environmental considerations, and screening of land
for the minimum size of CSP plants.
6.1.1 Gridding of Land
To classify land into parcels that can be developed for CSP, the study needed to break the land down
into individual parcels that can be independently evaluated. The study created a 0.5-mile by 0.5-mile
grid that covers the entire land area in U.S. states of interest. Each 0.5-mile by 0.5-mile parcel is then
evaluated for its suitability for siting of CSP plants.
6.1.2 Initial Screening of Unsuitable Land
Next, land area is screened out that is clearly not suitable for CSP project development. Any gridded
pixel of land is screened out for the following situations:
 • Federal/State Lands not considered developable: Department of Defense, National Parks,
 Forrest Service, Fish and Wildlife Service, The Nevada Test Site, and State Parks.

12
  Henry Price, Keith Boyle, and Devon Price, CSP Siting Assessment for the Southwestern U.S., Solar Dynamics,
October 2025, https://doi.org/10.2172/3000086.
                                                                                                   Page 36 of 77

Final Technical Report DE-EE0009809

 • General land exclusions: established bodies of water and land with slopes greater than 3%.
 • Infrastructure exclusions: major roads with a 100-m buffer, natural gas pipelines with a 50 m
 buffer, Railroads with a 250 m buffer, and transmission lines greater than 100 kV with a 100 m
 buffer.
 • CSP specific filters for molten-salt tower plants: Given that CSP plants are more of an industrial
 facility due to the power plant and that the tower, receiver, and mirrors are more visually
 impactful, 5-mile buffers were added around CSP plants for Airports and population centers, any
 grid that contained housing and other buildings were excluded, and any grid that included
 Department of Defense military aviation training routes (MTRs) were excluded.
6.1.3 Environmental and land use exclusions
Environmental and land use restrictions are then applied. Screens from the BLM 2012 PEIS ROD13 and
California 2016 DRECP14 were used to conduct screening of federal lands to exclude land from solar
development for environmental and land use reasons. When the original set of BLM land screens were
used, there are almost no BLM lands available for CSP (or solar) development that are outside the
existing BLM solar enterprise zones. For this project, it was important to reassess the land screenings
used in the original BLM PEIS and DRECP assessments to evaluate potential new opportunities for CSP
development. When the original land use screens were reviewed, it was noted that some of the land use
screen categories were obvious that they were land that should be screened out, for example,
wilderness areas, wild and scenic rivers, national trails, national monuments, etc. Others like the Fringe
Toed Lizard and Sage-Grouse Habitats are very specific habitat areas that should be screened. But other
screens, such as the Mojave Ground Squirrel, include very large areas, including some of the land that
otherwise appears best suited for CSP development. In this assessment two cases were evaluated, Case
A which included all the original BLM PEIS/DRECP screens, and Case B which included a reduced set of
environmental and land use screens.
Screens removed for Case B
 • GHI < 6.5 kW/m2-day – GHI is not relevant to CSP. Solar resource level is accounted in the
 economic analysis of sites later in the analysis.
 • Special recreational management areas – This is a general broad category that covers much of
 the desert areas. This should be considered later after sites are identified to see if there are real
 conflicts.

13
 BLM, The BLM 2012 Solar Programmatic Environmental Impact Statement (PEIS) Record of Decision (ROD)
established a process for permitting utility scale (> 20 MW) solar energy projects on BLM-administered lands in six
southwestern states: Arizona, California, Colorado, Nevada, New Mexico, and Utah. The Solar PEIS ROD process
facilitated the permitting of solar energy development projects on public lands in a more efficient, standardized,
and environmentally responsible manner. Link to PEIS website.

 BLM, Solar Energy Permitting and Program Resources. The 2016 Desert Renewable Energy Conservation Plan

Species Act (ESA), California Endangered Species Act (CESA), Natural Community Conservation Planning Act

Owens Valley. Link to DRECP website.
                                                                                                     Page 37 of 77

Final Technical Report DE-EE0009809

 • Mojave ground squirrel (MGS) habitat – This layer excludes all BLM lands in the western Mojave
 Desert in some of the best areas for siting CSP. The range of the MGS is known to be shrinking.
 The exclusions should focus on high value habitat known to host the MGS.
 • Flat tailed horned lizard habitat - This layer excludes most BLM land near the Imperial Valley
 that could be good for siting CSP. The range of the FTHL is known to be shrinking. Any sites
 identified in the current screen should be reassessed to determine if they are high value habitat
 for the FTHL.
 • FWS Critical Habitat for Fauna – This is an overlay of other exclusions with a few additional
 unclassified desert areas.
 • Areas of Critical Environmental Concern – This is an overlay of other exclusions and picks up
 many remaining otherwise unclassified desert areas.

in red are the additional areas included in Case B of this study with the reduced environmental and land

 screens are used. These locations are potential sites for molten-salt towers. The blue lines outline the

6.2 Economic Assessment of Potential CSP Sites
To assess the economic potential of different sites, a simplified levelized cost of energy model was
developed that could use the GIS data to estimate the cost of a molten-salt tower constructed at each
location. To calculate the LCOEs, GIS data is exported for locations of interest, run through a Python
program script to generate the LCOEs for various scenarios, then the LCOEs are uploaded to the GIS
system so that the results can be analyzed. This section describes the LCOE methodology used.
6.2.1 Levelized Cost of Energy (LCOE)
We used the LCOE calculation from the NREL 2024 ATB to compare the cost of energy produced at
different sites.15 The LCOE can account for differences in CAPEX or OPEX, different performance, or
different financial assumptions that may apply at individual sites. A real LCOE was calculated based on
the economic assumptions used in the 2024 ATB.
Reference Plant Configuration: The capital cost, O&M cost, and the performance were taken from the
Solar Dynamics reference baseload molten-salt tower plant design for a location in Arizona.

 Table 6. Reference molten salt tower configuration
 Molten salt tower reference baseload plant configuration
 Turbine size 100 MW gross
 Receiver size 600 MWt
 TES 14 hours
                      CAPEX                              $7,000 /kW
 Fixed O&M $150 /kW-yr
 Variable O&M $0.003 /kWh

Economic differences between the alternative sites were applied associated with the following criteria:
 • Solar resource at the site
 • Regional cost differences
 • Single plant vs. power parks

15
  NREL 2023 Electricity ATB, Equations and Variables in the ATB,
https://atb.nrel.gov/electricity/2023/equations_&_variables

                                                                                                 Page 39 of 77

 • Remoteness of site for labor and transportation.
 • Distance to existing transmission substation or high voltage transmission line
 • Transmission wheeling charges (sending power from out of state to CA)
 • Seismic loading
 • Energy communities (10% ITC adder)
 • Development cost differences by state and land type
The following assumptions were included:
 • Real LCOE (2021 USD)
 • 4 plant CSP power park
 • 30-40% ITC (10% Adder for qualifying energy communities)
 • Wheeling costs are added to sites outside of CAISO.
Figure 22 below provides a graphical representation of CSP siting potential in California and the
neighboring states from which power could be wheeled. The size of each circle is indicative of the
capacity potential of each contiguous site while the color provides an indication of the costs in USD per
MWh. As these numbers include many broad assumptions, such as federal incentives which are subject
to change, the results should be considered more for relative comparison as opposed to absolute values.

  Figure 22. Results of screening including relative costs and capacity potential for each contiguous site

The high level conclusions of the site screening and economic evaluation can be summarized as follows:
 • Tens of gigawatts of potentially economically attractive CSP resource potential exists at sites in
 and around California. Much of this lies near existing transmission corridors.
                                                                                              Page 40 of 77

 • The western Mojave Desert appears to offer the most economic opportunities for siting CSP in
 California. Few other desert areas in California look as economically attractive.
 • Most of the best sites identified in the western Mojave Desert are on land that was previously
 screened for the Mojave Ground Squirrel habitat. The range of the Mojave Ground Squirrel has
 been shrinking in recent years and many of the sites identified may no longer be an issue.
 • Although significant siting opportunities exist in the CA central valley, the cost of CSP power
 from these sites appears unlikely to be attractive.
 • Several locations outside of California (Arizona, Nevada, New Mexico) appear to offer attractive
 sites for CSP development to supply power to California if transmission capacity exists or can be
 expanded.

7 Public Perceptions of CSP
Note: This section provides a high-level summary. For detailed interview and engagement materials,
refer to the companion document: Annex E: Final Report for CSP Tower Public Opinion
Community acceptance is increasingly recognized as a decisive factor in the successful deployment of
large-scale renewable energy projects, including Concentrating Solar Power (CSP). Opposition rooted in
concerns about land use, property values, environmental impacts, and lack of community benefits has
delayed or halted many projects nationwide. This study examined public opinion toward CSP tower
projects and developed lessons for effective community engagement.
Fieldwork was carried out in Nevada, California, and Arizona, focusing on communities near existing CSP
plants (Ivanpah, CA; Crescent Dunes, NV) and potential development areas (Barstow, CA; Silver Lakes,
CA; Kingman, AZ). Methods included one-on-one interviews, on-site surveys, and informal community
engagement at local events. In total, hundreds of residents participated, providing insights into local
attitudes and expectations.
Key Findings:
 • General support for solar energy: Across surveyed communities, a majority held favorable views
 of solar power and expressed support for future CSP development, though enthusiasm varied by
 location.
 • Mixed perceptions of aesthetics: While many found tower structures unobjectionable, some
 expressed concern about visual impacts, particularly in recreational or scenic areas.
 • Importance of local benefits: Respondents consistently emphasized the need for projects to
 hire locally, purchase U.S.-made materials, and provide tangible community benefits such as
 workforce training.
 • Trust and transparency matter: In Tonopah, the loss of communication during Crescent Dunes’
 operational challenges created lasting distrust. Across sites, residents viewed transparency,
 continuous communication, and visible developer presence as essential.
 • Site-specific concerns: Water use in Kingman, dust and wildfire risk in Barstow, and utility
 relationships in Southern California emerged as critical issues shaping local acceptance.
 • Community considerations: Many surveyed communities highlighted needs for jobs, healthcare,
 and infrastructure, underscoring the importance of community benefit agreements.
Lessons Learned:
 • Engagement should begin early, before major project decisions are finalized, and continue
 throughout the full project lifecycle.

                                                                                            Page 41 of 77

 • In-person, face-to-face interaction remains the most effective method of building trust and
 capturing meaningful feedback.
 • Engagement strategies must be tailored to local demographics, values, and political contexts,
 with messaging adapted through testing and iteration.
 • Incentives, logistics, and survey design all significantly influence participation and
 representativeness of responses.
Recommendations:
Future CSP and renewable energy developers should:
 • Adopt a proactive, transparent engagement strategy – “early, often, and throughout” – to
 establish trust and credibility.
 • Develop community benefit agreements that reflect local priorities, ensure prevailing wages,
 and support workforce development.
 • Forge partnerships with local organizations such as schools, economic development offices, and
 labor groups to strengthen ties and credibility.
 • Maintain visible and consistent communication to prevent misinformation and foster long-term
 support.
Conclusion:
Public opinion research confirms that communities generally support CSP development when projects
are transparent, inclusive, and deliver meaningful local benefits. The success of CSP in California and
beyond will depend not only on technical and economic performance but equally on sustained, trust-
based engagement with host communities.

                                                                                            Page 42 of 77

8 Stakeholder Engagement
One of the goals of the CalCSP Study is to inform stakeholders about the potential of CSP. Many of the
key stakeholders that are planning for how California will achieve its SB 100 goals, either do not have a
good idea of the potential for CSP to support California’s energy needs, or they may have pre-existing
perceptions about the technology that may not be up to date. As a result, although CSP is acknowledged
as a potentially relevant technology for SB 100, it has not been one of the primary technologies
evaluated for meeting its targets. This project wanted to reach out to the key stakeholder groups
involved in plotting the path to address the SB 100 goals and to provide information to help CSP
technologies to be fairly evaluated against other technological options.

8.1 Stakeholder Advisory Group (SAG)
To accomplish this goal, Morse Associates (MAI) and the Center for Energy Efficiency and Renewable
Technologies (CEERT) formed an advisory group made up of key stakeholders from California agencies
(CPUC, CEC, CAISO), California utilities, and other interested stakeholders, to provide input and feedback
to the work effort and deliverables. The group referred to as the stakeholder advisory group provided
input to the study at several points during the project: at the beginning of the study, at the end of the
first phase, and again at the end of the project. The SAG members were given a survey at the beginning
of the project to provide input and direction to the project.
SAG membership: MAI and CEERT recruited participants for the SAG. In total, participants from 22
different organizations participated in the SAG, not including the project team.
 • California government agencies: CEC, CPUC, CAISO, DWR
 • Utilities: PG&E, LADWP, SMUD, San Jose CCA
 • Other CA stakeholders: Environmental, Unions, RE advocates/associations.
 • Federal: BLM, DOE
 • CSP Industry stakeholders: developer/project owner, CSP developer/engineer, CSP
 developer/technology provider.
Initial SAG Survey: An initial survey was sent to all the SAG members prior to the first SAG meeting to
attempt to get a baseline of attitudes and knowledge about CSP. The following summarizes some of the
key results from the initial SAG survey.

Proposals (RFPs). SMUD would like projects near their service territory (Sacramento) but would
entertain projects in the desert if they were confident in the deliverability of the energy.
