9 Policy Foundations and Case Studies in CSP Development
The goal of the deployment study was to evaluate pathways to meet state carbon reduction goals and
minimize the cost of CSP deployment. To help accomplish this, the team looked at the global efforts to
deploy CSP and attempted to learn from what has worked well and what has not. The team looked at
case studies from Ivanpah and Crescent Dunes, two CSP projects in the U.S. whose operational
successes and failures have been very public and have implications on future CSP developments.
Feedback from California stakeholders on their concerns about CSP were reviewed. This study discusses
other macro market factors that are likely to affect CSP deployment. Finally, the results provide a case
study for how CSP might be deployed cost effectively for California.

9.1 Lessons from global CSP markets
In general, CSP projects were developed when the economic conditions supported a viable and
financeable project. Because of the high CAPEX of CSP projects, they have required long-term power
purchase agreements or feed-in-tariffs of 20 to 35 years from a credit worthy off-taker. Projects have
relied on grants, tax incentives, low interest financing, or above market tariffs to help subsidize the
generally higher, above market price of CSP.
9.1.1 U.S. First CSP Wave: Energy Security (1984 – 1991).
The first commercial utility-scale CSP projects were the nine Luz SEGS parabolic trough projects,
developed in California between 1984 and 1991. The federal government’s Public Utility Regulatory
Policy Act or “PURPA” legislation created the new independent power producer market in the U.S.
PURPA, enacted in 1978 in response to the 1970s oil embargo, was designed to promote energy
independence by encouraging the development of domestic, non-utility power generation. While
PURPA is often associated with early renewable energy deployment, its primary aim was to diversify the
U.S. energy mix—not for environmental reasons, but to reduce reliance on imported fuels—paving the
way for new technologies like CSP to emerge. To avoid utilities needing to construct new coal-based
generation, California’s implemented standard offer contracts to PURPA qualifying facilities which
included geothermal, wind, cogeneration, and solar plants. These were 30-year power purchase
contracts, with avoided cost energy payments and fixed capacity payments. The federal government and
California offered investment tax credits, and California offered property tax exemptions for solar.
Lotker [17], describes the environment that existed that enabled the SEGS projects to be built and the
reasons for the eventual collapse of the market for new projects. In short, the absence of stable tax
policy and the decline in avoided costs—driven by an influx of low-cost natural gas from Texas and
Wyoming—increased project risk and undermined the economics of new CSP development.
The nine SEGS projects totaling 354 MW, which were constructed over about an eight-year period,
enabled a significant maturing of parabolic trough CSP power plant technology. This included both
parabolic trough solar field technology, but also the maturing of the power plant process configuration.
The plants increased in size from 14 to 80 MW, demonstrating the economies of scale. The SEGS
projects were also constructed with multiple projects at the same site, demonstrating the cost
advantage of power parks. The SEGS plants were also hybridized with natural gas backup, enabling them
to achieve high on-peak capacity factors, while burning a small amount of gas. Although it was another
15 years before the next large-scale commercial CSP project was completed in the U.S., the lessons

17
  Lotker, M. "Barriers to commercialization of large-scale solar electricity: Lessons learned from the LUZ
experience." , Oct. 1991. https://doi.org/10.2172/10108287.
                                                                                                      Page 47 of 77

learned from the SEGS projects were the foundation for the next waves of projects in the US, Spain, and
MENA region (2005-2015).
9.1.2 Global Environment Facility (GEF)
In the late 1990s, the GEF offered three $50M grants to subsidize integrated solar combined cycle
systems (ISCCS) in Egypt, Morocco, and Mexico. These projects used a parabolic trough solar field to
supply heat to generate supplemental steam to the bottoming cycle (steam turbine) of a natural gas
fired combined cycle power plant. Unfortunately, the ISCCS configuration has practical constraints which
limit the solar output to be a small percentage of the total output of the plant. At the time, the ISCCS
configuration was viewed as the most cost-effective solar power option available. The intent by the GEF
was to help spur a global CSP industry. Only two other ISCCS projects have been constructed. As the
solar power from an ISCCS plant competes directly with daytime PV power, this concept is generally not
of interest today, since a PV plant is much simpler to implement than adding a trough field and steam
generator to a combined cycle power plant. The Morocco ISCCS plant may have played a role in the later
formation of MASEN and development of CSP in Morocco.
9.1.3 U.S. Second CSP Wave: Renewable Portfolio Standards (2005 – 2015)
The second wave of projects in the U.S. were driven primarily by state renewable portfolio standards.
States required utilities to provide a percentage of their power from renewable or in some cases solar
sources. This was a competitively bid environment. During the period from 2000 to about 2010, CSP was
the least cost solar option. Many CSP projects were awarded PPAs. Unfortunately, delays in extension of
the ITC and the financial crisis of 2008 caused delays in the financing of many projects. The conversion of
the ITC from a tax credit to a grant and the availability of federal loan guarantees and low interest
federal financing enabled a number of projects to advance.
In total, ten CSP power plants went online between 2007 and 2015. Plants ranged in size from 70 to 250
MW. Six parabolic trough plants and four tower plants were constructed. Three of the projects
constructed multiple units at one site: Mojave (2), Genesis (2), and Ivanpah (3). Plant steam turbine size
was generally increased to at least 125 MW with power blocks as large as 250 MW in order to take
advantage of the economy of scale. Several additional CSP projects received PPAs but were unable to
achieve financial close. After 2010, PV prices had decreased to the point that CSP projects were no
longer the least cost alternative. The RPS policies generally tended to focus on the least cost and did not
value the attributes of thermal energy storage or other potential benefits that CSP projects might offer.
None of the seven CSP projects constructed in California included TES. Hotter climates whose peak
demand continued into the evening hours, such as Arizona and Nevada, did favor CSP plants with TES.
9.1.4 Spanish Feed-in Tariff (FIT) for CSP (2007-2013)
In 2007, the Spanish government implemented a FIT providing a very attractive tariff for CSP projects.
The FIT initially planned for only 200 MW, but eventually expanded to allow over 2300 MW of CSP
projects to be constructed. The FIT provided a very attractive fixed energy payment for any project that
was able to register. Projects were limited to 50 MW in size. Eventually, 49 CSP projects of 10 MW or
larger were constructed. Of these, 44 were 50 MW parabolic trough projects.
The FIT was a stable policy that enabled a large and robust Spanish CSP industry to develop. The Spanish
CSP industry led much of the later CSP development in the U.S. and global expansion. The FIT enabled
the first parabolic trough projects using indirect molten-salt thermal energy storage to be constructed.
In total 21 trough projects with TES were constructed. Standardized designs evolved that the industry as
a whole copied. The FIT also enabled the first commercial central receiver tower projects to be
constructed, both steam and molten-salt.

                                                                                             Page 48 of 77

The main issue with the FIT was that the tariff did not decrease over time, so it did not adequately
encourage efficiency and cost reduction. The nature of the FIT was very simple and as a result, many
Spanish EPCs were not fully prepared to deal with the more complex nature and financing structures
utilized in other markets. The Spanish government retroactively reduced the FIT after 2013, causing
financial distress to many of the projects constructed. However, most of the Spanish projects built under
the FIT continue operating at a high level of performance after up to 15 years in service.
9.1.5 Morocco
Morocco formed the Moroccan Agency for Sustainable Energy (MASEN) in 2010 to develop renewables
utilizing public private partnerships. MASEN developed several sites around Morocco for renewable
projects. They selected a high solar resource site between the Atlas mountains and the Sahara Desert,
near the town of Ouarzazate, for the initial development of CSP in Morocco. MASEN developed and
permitted the site and developed the water and transmission infrastructure. MASEN supported the
international financing and provided equity investment in the projects. MASEN also provided the PPA for
the projects. Three CSP project tenders were released for competitive bidding. Initially a 160 MW trough
with about 3 hours of TES was commissioned in 2016. A second solicitation for a 200 MW trough plant
with 6 hours of TES and a 150 MW molten-salt tower with 7 hours of TES were commissioned in 2018.
MASEN’s direct involvement is believed by industry to have resulted in higher quality projects. The
competitive bidding likely resulted in lower cost PPAs for Morocco. Given the low penetration of
renewable power in Morocco, Masen has ended its CSP development and is developing lower cost PV
and wind power. Masen is including about 1-hour of battery energy storage with its new solar projects.
9.1.6 South Africa (2012-2019)
South Africa launched the Renewable Energy Independent Power Producer Procurement Programme
(REIPPPP) in 2011 to competitively procure renewables (wind, PV, CSP, biomass, etc.). CSP was procured
both inside regular REIPPPP bid windows (BW1–BW4) and via a dedicated CSP window (commonly
referenced as BW3.5 or a CSP-dedicated window) to attract dispatchable, storage-capable plants.
Overall, 7 CSP projects were constructed with an output of 600 MW. Projects had between 2 and 12
hours of thermal energy storage. South Africa has a short evening peak that later projects were designed
to meet. The auction process resulted in a drop in pricing from 2.69 Rand/kWh to 1.7 Rand/kWh, a 37%
reduction. During this period the Rand experienced significant currency devaluation, so the actual
reduction in pricing in US dollars was significantly greater. Additionally, the Redstone project, the only
molten-salt tower project constructed, was significantly delayed due to business and PPA contracting
issues. A new flat tariff of 1.223 Rand/kWh was negotiated for the project, resulting in a current price of
about 0.7 US cents per kWh.
9.1.7 DEWA Concentrating Solar Power (CSP) Projects in Dubai
DEWA’s flagship CSP initiative is the fourth phase of the Mohammed bin Rashid Al Maktoum Solar Park
in Dubai —a 950 MW hybrid plant combining 600 MW of parabolic trough, 100 MW molten-salt central
receiver tower, and a 250 MW photovoltaic plant. The project includes 5,907 MWhe of molten-salt
thermal energy storage. The plant uses a hybrid CSP and PV model to achieve 24-hour generation, using
PV generation during the day and CSP to cover the evening peak and generation through the night. The
project was developed under an Independent Power Producer (IPP) model by Noor Energy 1. The
project shareholders are DEWA (51%, the local utility), ACWA Power (25%, a private developer), and Silk
Road Fund (24%, the Chinese government). The project located in a mediocre solar resource achieved a
record low PPA price of 7 US cents per kWh for the CSP plants, helped in part by a 35-year PPA. DEWA
developed the site and infrastructure.
9.1.8 CSP Development in China

                                                                                             Page 49 of 77

China’s growing investment in concentrating solar power (CSP) is notable given that it already
dominates global supply chains for solar PV and lithium-ion batteries—technologies widely viewed as
the backbone of future renewable power systems. Despite this leadership, China continues to pursue
CSP with multi-hour molten-salt thermal storage at large scale, signaling a strategic judgment that
dispatchable solar generation and high-temperature thermal storage are essential complements to
variable PV and batteries. China’s energy planners appear to view CSP as providing capabilities—such as
long-duration storage, nighttime solar output, and rotational inertia—that are difficult to supply with
PV-battery systems alone, and the country is positioning itself to build a globally competitive CSP
industry around these strategic advantages.
The Chinese government launched its CSP demonstration program in 2016 as a key milestone in the
government’s drive to build a fully domestic, globally competitive CSP industry. Under this initiative, 20
commercial-scale projects across various technologies—parabolic trough, solar tower, and Fresnel—
were selected to showcase home-grown solutions and strengthen China’s solar thermal value chain. By
January 2021, eight of these demonstration plants, totaling 500 MW, were complete and three more
were under construction, signaling rapid progress in domestic deployment and technology maturation.
China’s second demonstration round comprising 11 new CSP plants with integrated thermal energy
storage to be designed, financed, and completed by 2024. Each project must be developed by a
consortium anchored by large state-owned energy companies, ensuring access to low-cost financing and
solid equity backing to meet tight two-year construction deadlines. These projects generally integrated a
100 MW CSP plant with TES with up to 900 MW of PV or wind.
China’s current CSP policy is built around its national Concentrated Solar Power Demonstration Program,
which underwrites selected commercial-scale plants with central government support. Projects
admitted to the program receive guaranteed 20-year power-purchase agreements, priority grid
interconnection and land allocation, and access to a national feed-in-tariff that bridges the cost gap
between solar thermal generation and wholesale electricity prices. This framework explicitly rewards
systems that incorporate multi-hour molten-salt storage, ensuring dispatchable output well beyond
daylight hours.
Beyond direct subsidies, CSP is now woven into provincial renewable-energy quotas and China’s broader
renewable-energy law. Large state-owned banks extend concessional loans, while major state-owned
energy companies spearhead project consortia to meet tight construction deadlines. Together, these
measures underpin a roadmap to exceed 4 GW of CSP capacity by 2030, compared to 1.3 GW currently
operating the U.S and 2.3 GW operating in Spain. The CSP development in China fosters a fully domestic
solar-thermal value chain, and position Chinese firms to export turnkey CSP solutions. The program’s
standout successes have come in molten-salt tower systems, where the Chinese claim that multiple
units are operating reliably and meeting performance targets.
By the end of 2024, China had 838 MW of CSP projects in operation and 3,300 MW under construction,
according to the 2024 CSP Blue Book. Additionally, another 4,800 MW of CSP projects were under
development in China.

9.2 CSP Case Studies
When California stakeholders think of CSP, they often think of the Ivanpah CSP plant in California. When
next generation CSP plants that will use molten-salt central receiver/tower technology are discussed,
the experience from Crescent Dunes plant in Tonopah, Nevada is important. This report provides two
case studies of experience and lessons learned from these two plants to aid in discussion for next
generation CSP plants.

                                                                                             Page 50 of 77

9.2.1 Ivanpah Solar Electric Generating Station
For many California stakeholders, the Ivanpah plant is what comes to mind when CSP is mentioned. The
Ivanpah plant is very visible, located next to the major interstate highway running between Los Angeles
and Las Vegas. As a result, it has received a lot of visibility in the news and press.
Ivanpah is a 386 MW CSP project with three power plants (126, 133, and 133 MW) in California, located
just north of Interstate 15 near the Nevada state line. The first unit came online in 2014 and became the
largest CSP facility in the world when the third unit was placed in service. The project was developed by
BrightSource Energy, with much of the same management team from the Luz Industries team that built
the SEGS parabolic trough projects in the 1980’s. The project was developed following the California
energy crisis of the early 2000s. The design focused on meeting California’s noon to 6pm summer
afternoon peak demand. As a result, there was limited value adding thermal energy storage to the
project. BrightSource selected a reheat steam central receiver/tower CSP technology design for
Ivanpah, because this was viewed as the most efficient CSP plant configuration available, and was
significantly cheaper than photovoltaic technology at the time. Molten-salt central receiver technology
was not yet commercially available. The plant was initially going to be hybridized with natural gas much
like the SEGS parabolic trough plants to assure high on-peak capacity factors. The plant design later
removed the hybrid capability but still required natural gas for start-up of the receiver and steam
turbine, as well as for managing operation during cloud transients. Natural gas represents a meaningful
fraction of the energy input to the plant on an annual basis, according to publicly available data from the
Energy Information Administration (EIA)18.

18
  EIA, “Electricity Data Browser – Plant 57074,” accessed July 24, 2025,
https://www.eia.gov/electricity/data/browser/#/plant/57074
                                                                                             Page 51 of 77

Figure 24. The 386 MW Ivanpah Solar Energy Generating System project in California, with three reheat
 steam central receiver plants in California.
The Ivanpah project experienced several challenges during its development. It was one of the first large
solar developments to be constructed on federal BLM land. This on top of the complex California
permitting requirements added challenges and cost to the project. It appears that the developers did
not conduct sufficient environmental due diligence on the site, as it had a significant population of
desert tortoises present. Construction of the project was halted for a period until the US Fish and
Wildlife Service determined that the project would not have a detrimental effect on the tortoise
population in the area. A separate tortoise reserve was set up to house displaced tortoises. The project
decided to minimize impact on the site compared to other solar technologies and leave the natural site
topography and vegetation in place in the heliostat field. This added challenges during operation of the
plant as it can draw in wildlife where it may be put in harm’s way.
The Ivanpah towers were the first scale-up of central receiver tower technology beyond 20 MW. The
project was a successful demonstration of the feasibility of large 100 MW+ central receiver heliostat
fields and receivers.
Ivanpah was the first CSP central receiver/tower project to use a reheat steam receiver and steam
turbine. Although the plant has one of the more efficient power cycles during steady state operation,
the temporal imbalance between superheated and reheated steam paths in the receiver and steam
turbine cause significant operational challenges during plant start-up and cloud transients. This has
resulted in lower output from the plant. In the later Ashalim steam tower project in Israel, the reheat
cycle was abandoned for simpler superheated steam tower/receiver configuration. The Ashalim plant
has operated much closer to design performance levels. The major disadvantage of steam tower

                                                                                            Page 52 of 77

Final Technical Report DE-EE0009809

technology is the lack of a practical form of thermal energy storage. Thus, it must compete directly with
photovoltaic technology, rendering the technology of little value to the future market.
The plant has underperformed its original performance projections, operating somewhere in the 80 to
90% performance level. The performance has generally improved year after year, with a few upsets
along the way. As previously mentioned, the reheat steam cycle was sensitive to cloud transients and
start-ups. The original performance forecasts were based on a solar measurement near the plant site.
The actual plant site is on the side of a mountain and sees more afternoon clouds and a reduced solar
resource that was initially measured. Being a first of a kind plant, the performance forecasts were likely
more aggressive than they should have been. But in general, the plant has been operating at a relatively
good level of performance.
When the plant initially started operating, there were concerns over avian mortality. The position of the
heliostats needs to always be controlled to prevent accidents where multiple heliostats concentrate
light in unintended locations. As a result, the heliostat field is designed to actively aim the heliostats in a
safe direction during all hours when the sun is up and the heliostat is not used to aim light at the central
receiver. This is referred to as standby operation. The initial standby operating strategy created several
high flux zones above the heliostat field that were not visible to avian species. As a result, there was
initially a relatively high level of birds being injured by high solar flux. The plant modified the standby
operation of the heliostats and eliminated the high flux hazard of standby operation. There remain high
solar flux zones near the receivers at the top of the towers during operation. The plants have
implemented various deterrence mechanisms to keep birds away from the tower during operation.
These efforts have helped significantly reduce avian mortality at the plant. The plant has implemented a
rigorous monitoring program to detect avian mortalities. The results indicate that the plant has reduced
avian mortality to a level not viewed as detrimental to local or migratory bird populations. (See Annex F
for more information)
9.2.2 Crescent Dunes
Crescent Dunes Solar Energy Project is a 125 MW (110 MW net) molten-salt central receiver/tower plant
with 10 hours of thermal energy storage. The plant was the first of a kind scale-up of molten salt
technology after the 20 MW Gemasolar plant in Spain. The plant is located near Tonopah, Nevada, on
BLM land. The plant site is remote, midway between Las Vegas and Reno. The plant went into
commercial operation in 2015. The plant had a 25-year PPA with NV Energy, the utility for Las Vegas and
southern Nevada, and was designed to supply power from noon to 10 pm. The developer was Solar
Reserve, who also supplied the molten-salt receiver, and ACS Cobra who was the EPC. Both were equity
investors in the project.

                                                                                                 Page 53 of 77

Figure 25. The 125 MW Crescent Dunes Project Utilizing Molten-Salt Central Receiver/tower technology
 with 10 hours of thermal energy storage.
The plant has significantly underperformed the original performance projections, mainly due to issues
with the hot salt storage tank and the steam generation system.
 • Leaks in the hot salt storage tank – This is the key issue causing the low availability of the plant,
 resulting in four extended outages, one lasting over 2 years while the tank was replaced. A
 significant effort has gone into understanding the failure mechanisms and how to appropriately
 design hot salt storage tanks. The scale up of the hot salt tank was one of the main contributing
 factors to the failures. The failure did not occur in the Gemasolar plant that has a hot tank
 approximately one quarter of the capacity of the Crescent Dunes hot tank but did occur in some
 projects with tanks of similar size to Crescent Dunes. The floor of the original hot salt tank was
 not designed appropriately for the temperature gradients the tank experienced during
 commissioning and operation. More attention also needed to be paid to welding procedures
 and quality control during construction of the tank. Finally, more attention needed to be paid to
 controlling temperature gradients during commissioning and operation. This issue can be
 eliminated in future plants with improved design, construction, commissioning, and operation of
 the tank.
 • Leaks in the steam generator – This has been the second most important factor contributing to
 the underperformance of the plant. There are two issues that were identified. First, the plant
 water chemistry needs to be maintained during commissioning and operation. Poor water
 chemistry caused corrosion in the evaporators resulting in tube leaks. Second, the steam
 generation system needs to be designed to make sure the heat exchangers are operated within
 acceptable temperature gradients during daily start-ups. High temperature gradients can cause
 leaks between the tube sheets and the tubes. These issues can be eliminated with better
 monitoring of water quality and improved process design to eliminate start-up temperature
 transients.
The plant experienced some additional issues with the process design and sizing of equipment, but none
that are significant from a technological perspective. Many of these issues were identified and discussed
in the NREL CSP Best Practices report. More attention could have been paid to heliostat optical quality,
                                                                                              Page 54 of 77

Final Technical Report DE-EE0009809

process controls, and system automation. The industry has made advancements in these areas since the
plant was constructed.
As a result of the low availability and performance of the plant, the plant experienced contractual
difficulties. The original PPA was cancelled in 2019 due to under performance, causing the project to file
for bankruptcy. The project was restructured with the EPC taking over ownership and operation and
negotiating a buyout of the debt. The new owners continued to repair and maintain the plant in an
effort to reach profitable operation. The plant restarted operating in 2021 under a short term PPA at
market tariffs. Although the plant is currently operating in a derated condition, due to pending repairs of
one of its steam generators, it has otherwise been operating very reliably. It recently operated for over
50 days continuously, producing electricity 24 hours per day, and demonstrating daily solar operations
to recharge storage.
The plant was the first of a kind scale-up of molten salt tower technology, and helped identify several
important technology issues in the design used at Crescent Dunes. These lessons are being shared with
industry and are being addressed in the new generation of molten-salt tower plants currently being
deployed around the world. While Crescent Dunes may never perform at the original performance levels
expected, newer molten-salt tower plants around the world are performing as expected.
For further details about the technical challenges facing Crescent Dunes and proposed solutions, please
refer to “Assessment of, and Potential Solutions, for Availability Problems in Commercial Central
Receiver Projects” as attached in Annex A.

9.3 California Stakeholder Issues and Concerns
The project gave the members of the stakeholder advisory group a survey to gain an understanding of
individuals knowledge and concerns about CSP. From the responses, it was generally clear that there
was a lot of misinformation about CSP and many concerns were based on dated information.
9.3.1 SAG Siting Concerns for CSP
The following were the major concerns identified by the SAG for siting of CSP projects:
 • Access to transmission.
 The CAISO transmission queue is overloaded with projects that have a low probability of
 occurring. Efforts are being made to clean up the queue and remove these projects. But the
 interconnection process is very delayed for any new projects. But CSP needs to be included in
 CA transmission planning processes. This is unlikely to occur until real CSP projects are
 announced or the state identifies CSP as an important resource, much as it has done for
 offshore wind. Many potentially attractive CSP sites lie along existing HV transmission corridors,
 making them easier to access transmission.
 • Availability of suitable land for CSP plants.
 This study identified tens of GW of siting potential in California and surrounding states. Even a
 fraction of this area could provide significant resource potential for California. Many of the best
 locations in California are in the Mojave Desert and on BLM land that has been screened for
 environmental reasons. A more careful examination would find that much of the land could be
 suitable for development if proper care is taken.
 • The quantity of water used by CSP plants.
 Although CSP is sometimes perceived as water-intensive, real-world data shows that its water
 use is far lower than the agricultural uses it often replaces. For example, the land underlying the
                                                                                                 Page 55 of 77

 Solana Generating Station previously required about 30,000 acre-feet per year for alfalfa
 production across roughly 3,000 acres, whereas Solana’s wet-cooled 2,000-acre CSP facility uses
 under 3,000 acre-feet per year—about 15% of the former agricultural demand. Modern plants in
 desert climates almost universally use dry cooling, reducing water needs even further to about
 2% of an equivalently sized alfalfa farm and approaching the water use of utility-scale PV
 (primarily for mirror or panel washing). Given that CSP facilities generate orders of magnitude
 more revenue per acre than traditional agriculture while using a fraction of the water, CSP
 represents a high-value, low-water land use in arid regions.
 • The environmental impact of CSP plants.
 This concern appears to be largely driven by publicity from the Ivanpah project. The Ivanpah
 project was located on pristine desert land within line of sight of a major interstate highway.
 Many other projects were constructed on more suitable sites and received much less attention.
 Improved site selection, as had been attempted in this study, should significantly reduce this
 concern.
 • The effect of concentrated solar radiation on avian species.
 Publicity from the Ivanpah project has likely driven public concern about avian impacts at
 central-receiver CSP plants, but decades of monitoring at Solar One, Ivanpah, and Crescent
 Dunes show that overall mortality rates are low and comparable to those observed at large wind
 farms. The industry has made significant progress in monitoring and mitigation, including
 improved stand-by aiming strategies that limit off-receiver flux, deterrent measures, and
 systematic third-party surveys; biologists conclude that the three Ivanpah towers are not
 adversely affecting local or migratory bird populations. Ongoing research by Sandia National
 Laboratories and NREL—including automated avian detection systems and refined operational
 protocols—continues to further reduce potential impacts, and CSP plants pose minimal risk to
 bats due to their daytime flux profile. Additional details and supporting data are provided in
 Annex F, “Avian and Bat Health Concerns at Central Receiver CSP Plants.”
 • Reflected light affecting general aviation.
 When Ivanpah first went into operation there were several complaints by commercial airline
 and private pilots about the brightness of the towers. Ivanpah was the first commercially
 operated central receiver plant in the U.S. It is only about 40 miles from the Las Vegas airport
 and almost directly in line with the runways. As part of this study, the team reached out to the
 Las Vegas airport to see if the towers were still a concern. The response was that they were no
 longer receiving complaints about the towers once they were included on FAA maps and pilots
 were made aware of glare issue. Nonetheless, care needs to be taken when siting central
 receiver plants in terms of the distance and direction from existing airports.
9.3.2 SAG Technology Concerns for CSP
 • The cost of energy from a CSP plants is too high.
 The perception that CSP is too expensive often stems from comparisons between the cost of
 legacy CSP projects and today’s PV prices. However, this study demonstrates that CSP can be
 more cost-effective than PV paired with battery energy storage (PV + BESS) for baseload or
 night-time dispatch profiles—applications where long-duration, firm capacity is essential. Cost
 reductions can be achieved by co-locating multiple CSP units at a single site, enabling shared
 infrastructure and economies of scale. Further cost declines are possible through the
 development of multi-project CSP power parks, which would also support supply chains,
                                                                                            Page 56 of 77

 workforce development, and streamlined permitting. Additionally, public-private partnerships
 could play a critical role in lowering the financing costs and accelerating deployment of CSP in
 California.
 • Underperformance of CSP plants.
 Some of the CSP plants developed in the U.S. were first-of-a-kind either in terms of technology
 or size scale-up. Many issues related to first-of-a-kind designs or scale-up issues (Ivanpah,
 Crescent Dunes, Solana), have been resolved in subsequent projects, but that information does
 not get shared. So, the projects often received negative publicity, resulting in potentially
 negative perceptions about the technology. Additionally, as the technologies have matured,
 performance forecasting has also matured. Many newer plants are operating with improved
 availability and performance.
 • There is a lack of developers and CSP industry in the U.S.
 The history of CSP development has shown that when a viable market opportunity emerges for
 CSP, the CSP industry arrives to support the need. The RPS has been the primary driver for new
 renewable power generation in the US over the last 15 years. This typically only values the
 energy value of electricity. As California moves towards 100% carbon-free generation, the
 capacity value of CSP can be recognized and monetized. The new RCPPP in California will enable
 CSP to compete for both resource adequacy (capacity) and the RPS (carbon-free energy).
 • The cost of operation and maintenance is too high for CSP.
 The O&M costs of CSP are high compared to PV, but potentially cheaper than PV + BESS for
 baseload or night peaker configurations, when the cost of battery replacement is included. As
 reliability and automation improve for CSP, and the scale of the projects increase, O&M costs
 are reduced.
 • The time it takes to develop and construct a CSP plant is too long.
 CSP projects are more complicated projects and tend to have longer development and
 construction timelines. As the industry matures, it is moving to standardize designs which can
 help accelerate the time to COD. California would include CSP as one of its long lead time
 resources similar to geothermal and offshore wind. This could enable the state to actively
 support proposals for CSP and could actively support the creation of a public private partnership
 model.

9.4 Macro Market factors affecting CSP Development
The Trump administration has implemented a number of policies that are likely to have significant
implications for future development of CSP and other renewables in the U.S. These include changes to
the investment and production tax credits, new rules aimed at foreign entities of concern (FEOC), tariffs
on imported materials and equipment, and changes in land use policy and permitting.
9.4.1 Investment Tax Credits
The investment and production tax credits are being phased out rapidly for solar energy. Solar projects
must start construction by July 4, 2026, to be eligible for the Solar ITC or PTC. Given the long
development and financing timeline for CSP, it is highly unlikely that any new projects could meet this
milestone. The economic analysis in this project assumed the prior 30% ITC conditions and the 10%
bonus for energy communities where appropriate.

                                                                                               Page 57 of 77

The energy storage tax credits remain. It is possible that CSP projects with TES could benefit from the
energy storage tax credit. A preliminary analysis seems to indicate that the thermal storage system and
potentially the steam generation system that is used to discharge storage could qualify for the ITC. The
heliostat field, receiver and tower are likely to be considered part of the solar collection system, and the
eligibility of the steam power plant is unclear for inclusion under the storage ITC. For baseload and night
dispatch molten-salt tower plants, it appears that 25 to 30% of the direct costs might qualify for the
storage ITC. A more detailed breakdown may be needed to evaluate which indirect costs could be
included.
Figure 26 below shows the real levelized cost of energy (2021 USD) for the CSP configurations reported
above for different investment tax credit assumptions. For the baseload configuration, the single plant
LCOE increases from 9.3 ȼ/kWh with a 30% ITC to 11.9 ȼ/kWh with no ITC, but drops to 11.5 ȼ/kWh with
the 100% bonus depreciation. If the thermal energy storage system and the steam generator system
qualify for the storage ITC and all indirect costs are factored equally across the direct costs, then the
LCOE with 30% storage ITC drops to 10.8 ȼ/kWh. In summary, the LCOE increases by 16 to 24%
depending on whether the storage ITC can be used.

 Figure 26. Effect of Tax Policy on CSP LCOE
9.4.2 New FEOC Rules
It is not yet clear what implications the new foreign entity of concern (FEOC) rules will have on CSP
projects. Most imported equipment has historically come from non-FEOC countries. Unfortunately, the
massive buildup of CSP in China in recent years has developed a very mature and low cost CSP supply
chain in that country. The FEOC rules and tariffs may prevent US projects from realizing the benefit of
low-cost supply from Chinese vendors.
9.4.3 Import Tariffs
The new import tariffs could have a significant impact on CSP plant costs. Most of the major equipment
is currently imported from Europe or Asia. The tariffs could encourage more domestic supply, however,
most of the equipment is imported because it is no longer cost effective to manufacture domestically.
9.4.4 Land use policies

                                                                                              Page 58 of 77

Final Technical Report DE-EE0009809

Much of the land identified in the siting study conducted in this report is federal land. Any new land use
policies and permitting requirement changes will factor in the ability to develop those lands for projects.
Any uncertainty or changes in policy could result in delays in development.

9.5 Opportunities to support future CSP deployment
This section outlines the opportunities to support the deployment of CSP.
 • Generally, new state mandates to supply up to 100% of their power generation from carbon-
 free resources are the main driver for creating a new opportunity for CSP in the US.
 o California’s SB 100 is an important driver that will end up influencing not only California,
 but also the surrounding states that supply approximately 20% of California’s power.
 o Nevada, New Mexico, and Colorado all have 100% RPS or CES.
 o Although Arizona doesn’t have a state-based 100% RPS or CES mandate, individual
 utilities, such as the public owned Salt River Project do.
 • California needs to take a new look at how CSP could be used to support its SB 100 goals.
 California is considering offshore wind resources, and it should also consider “onshore CSP”
 resources. To do this, CSP should be included in California’s Planning processes.
 o CSP should be included in California’s RESOLVE modeling
 o CSP resources should be included or at least considered in the California transmission
 planning process.
 o CSP should be included in California annual integrated resource planning (IRP) efforts.
 The new RCPPP integrates the RPS/CES with Resource Adequacy which is what CSP has
 needed to value both its green energy and green capacity benefits. CSP should be
 modeled and explicitly included as a technology option.
 • Public Private Partnerships (PPP): Several countries have successfully implemented PPPs to
 support and accelerate the development of CSP. California already has a program and state
 agencies in place that could be used to implement a PPP to support development of CSP
 resources.
 o The CPUC has created its long lead time (LLT) procurement program to augment and
 accelerate development of geothermal and offshore wind resources. CSP meets the
 criteria developed for defining LLT resources.
 o California has tasked the Department of Water Resources to procure LLT resources with
 support from the California Energy Commission.
 • The LLT program could be used to:
 o Identify and select the best sites for CSP development.
 o Once a site is selected, it could develop and permit the site, and provide the necessary
 infrastructure including interconnection, substation, and transmission to the site, water
 supply, roads, fencing, and security.
 o Projects would be competitive bid to provide best value.
 o Long-term PPAs of 30-years or longer would be provided that are structured to value
 both RA (capacity) and RECs (carbon-free energy).
 o Provide access to lower cost public financing and/or share in the equity ownership in
 the projects.
 o As scale is important for driving down costs and gaining the maximum economic benefit
 to local communities, projects should be structured to be multi-phased projects that are
 sequentially constructed in a larger power park setting.
 o Projects could be hybridized with PV to provide cost reduction and to support baseload
 renewable power.
                                                                                                Page 59 of 77

Final Technical Report DE-EE0009809

 • Data Centers – Data centers are generating the first significant increase in baseload power
 demand seen in the US in decades. Data center expansion is limited by available energy supply
 resources. Data centers are looking at any and all power supply options, but they are under
 pressure to minimize the increase in carbon emissions.
 o One opportunity for CSP development could be to partner with data center developers
 to provide co-located baseload and dispatchable renewable energy.
 o A hybrid CSP + PV project could provide baseload power supply to a data center.
 o If the project is also hybridized with natural gas, the CSP plant could be used to provide
 power even during prolonged, multi-day cloudy periods.

10 Future CSP Plant Concept
A conceptual multi-unit CSP plant (power park) was modeled at a site in Arizona which has transmission
access to California. To achieve economies of scale, promote learning, and reduce costs on the order of
20%, a hybrid four-plant molten-salt tower power park with PV was developed. PV provides daytime
generation and also supplies the parasitic loads of the CSP plant in the daytime. The plant can deliver
400 MW power 24/7 at 80%+ annual capacity factor on solar input alone. The capacity factor can
approach 100% by augmenting the CSP plant operation with natural gas or RNG. Dumped PV energy or
low-priced grid power can also be used to charge TES with an electric heater. For data center
applications, demand side management (such as throttling computing power for AI model training) can
be used to minimize combustion fuel needs.
The case study below is designed to meet the needs of a major utility company in Arizona. The 100 MW
(net) solar dispatch molten salt tower is designed to complement the production of other resources by
delivering electricity when intermittent renewables are unable to deliver to their system. The large
thermal energy storage system (15 hours / 1500 MWh) combined with the relatively low capacity factor
of 48% allows the plant to collect and store energy when the sun is shining, deferring the delivery of
electricity until it is dispatched when needed by Automatic Generation Control (AGC). This flexibility is
most valuable to a utility portfolio that is changing over time, as the production profile of the plant can
adapt to the changing needs of future years.
The proposed operating strategy has been designed to maximize the electricity delivery to the priority
hours as defined by the utility’s “heat map” in their 2025 All-Source RFP19, with all remaining energy
being delivered at a reduced uniform load until the priority hours of the following day. By running at
minimum load through days will poor solar resource, the performance model indicates that the turbine
can remain online for the vast majority of the year, requiring as little as three shut-down and start-up
cycles per year (neglecting equipment availability). Table 8 below shows the capacity factor by hour /
month, overlaid on the utility’s heat map, where the darker colors indicate higher demand for energy.
Note that all of the higher priority hours are late afternoon and evening in the summertime.

As evidenced by Table 8 above, the annual capacity factor during the highest priority hours is 98%. The
capacity factor during the secondary priority hours is 90%. While this pertains to Arizona rather than
California, the underlying “duck curve” challenges are similar in both states.

 Figure 27. Single CSP Square mile Tower

To illustrate the flexibility of the plant, the graph below shows an example of 4 operating days in which 2
consecutive days in the middle are disrupted by cloudy weather. Despite the challenging conditions, the
operator has flexibility in dispatching the stored energy, with 3 different dispatch scenarios depicted.
Note that the highest valued hours are not particularly well-aligned with the solar resource, thus the
need for dispatchable energy storage.

                                                                                                      Page 61 of 77

 Figure 28. Operating scenarios for 4 days of operation summertime operation

Under “Operations Scenario 1,” the plant operates in a baseload mode, delivering a constant 55 MW to
the grid, regardless of the nighttime hours and cloudy weather days. Note that the “Storage State of
Charge” takes an overall downward trend through the cloudy days, utilizing stored energy when the sun
isn’t shining, but recovering energy with the return of the final sunny day. While adding value to the
utility off-taker as a dependable baseload resource, this operating mode pays no regard to the time
value of energy, leaving those high-value hours to be addressed by other resources.

“Operations Scenario 2” dispatches energy during the high value hours and continues delivering at
maximum load until the energy storage is depleted. This has historically been the assumed operating
mode for CSP plants as it maximizes the energy delivered from the plant on an annual basis. However,
the daily start-ups and associated temperature cycling of the power block take a toll on the equipment,
potentially increasing maintenance requirements and reducing the overall life expectancy. Further, it
gives little regard to the real-time energy needs of the utility off-taker, which are likely to vary day to
day, and also evolve over the course of years with changing customer demand.

                                                                                              Page 62 of 77

Finally, “Operations Scenario 3” combines the best aspects of the prior two scenarios, likely providing
the most value to the utility off-taker. The plant dispatches at maximum load during priority hours, stays
online at a reduced sustainable load during non-priority hours, then ramps up again during the priority
hours of the following day. This operating mode has benefits for both the operator and the off-taker.
Keeping the turbine running 24/7 reduces the temperature cycling and associated wear and tear. For
the utility off-taker, it offers spinning reserves which can be called upon to ramp up or down. The
synchronous generator also offers mechanical inertia which contributes to grid stability. With this
operating mode, the performance model indicates that the turbine can remain online for the vast
majority of the year, requiring as little as three shut-down and start-up cycles per year (aside from
equipment availability).
To achieve a higher capacity factor, the 100 MW (net) molten salt tower plant can be paired with a 100
MW (net) single-axis tracking photovoltaic plant. The PV plant delivers electricity when the sun is shining
and the tower complements production by delivering at night and during cloudy periods. This
configuration combines the low cost of PV with the high flexibility of the tower, resulting in an
attractively priced 100 MW system which delivers an annual capacity factor of approximately 75%.
The modeled PV plant has a few unique features. First, it is oriented on an east-west axis, resulting in
less seasonal variation compared to the more traditional north-south axis. Second, the PV plant is also
tied to an electric heater that can be used to charge the thermal energy storage of the CSP plant. This
feature is useful as it can capture overproduction from the PV plant (which is intentionally over-sized)
including energy which may otherwise be curtailed due to grid saturation from other PV plants. The
electric heaters also allow the possibility to charge from the grid (noting that this offer and the
performance model have assumed no grid charging). Finally, the over-sized PV plant also provides the
off-line load of the molten salt tower, energy necessary to pump the salt to the top of the tower and
into the thermal energy storage system. These unique features contribute to the overall high capacity
factor of the hybrid system.
This performance model has also been designed to maximize the electricity delivery to the high-priority
hours with all remaining energy being delivered overnight. The plant still has some flexibility to deliver
alternative profiles, but to a lesser extent due to the higher capacity factor. Table 9 below shows the
capacity factor by hour / month, overlaid on the utility’s heat map.

 Table 11. Combined Capacity Factor (%) by Hour / Month of PV + CSP tower

Finally, deploying multiple plants at the same location results in significant economies of scale. A
configuration consisting of 4 x 100 MW (net) molten salt towers + 400 MW (net) PV is depicted and
discussed further below.

 Figure 29. 4 X 100MW towers + 400MW PV → 400MW baseload generation

                                                                                           Page 65 of 77

The supply chain for molten salt tower plants is less mature than PV and wind, a contributing factor to
the higher cost of these plants, particularly for singular projects. The construction of four co-located
towers would allow manufacturers to invest in the necessary infrastructure, ideally in the U.S.
The other significant savings associated with four towers is the O&M costs. Unlike PV and wind, the
power block of CSP plants and the solar field requires meaningful staff 24/7. The power block must be
manned when the facility is delivering electricity, and mirror washing is conducted at nighttime on a
continuous basis.

                                                                                            Page 66 of 77

11 Conclusions and Next Steps
California’s transition to a 100% clean electricity system requires more than low-cost renewable
energy—it demands a balanced portfolio of resources capable of providing firm capacity and flexible
dispatch. The CalCSP Study demonstrates that modern molten-salt tower CSP, when paired with multi-
hour thermal energy storage, can play a meaningful and cost-competitive role in meeting these needs.
Through technoeconomic modeling, GIS-based siting analysis, capacity-expansion modeling, and
stakeholder and public engagement, the study provides a comprehensive view of CSP’s potential
contribution to California’s decarbonization strategy.

11.1 Key Findings
CSP provides unique firm and flexible capabilities not supplied by PV and batteries alone.
CSP with TES delivers high-value evening and nighttime output, synchronous generation, and
operational flexibility that supports reliability during steep ramping periods.
CSP is cost-competitive when evaluated for the products that California needs most.
Where baseload power or nighttime dispatch are needed, molten-salt tower CSP competes favorably
with PV + BESS systems of equivalent service, especially beyond 8–10 hours of storage. Multi-unit CSP
power parks can further reduce LCOE by 15% to 20%.
Significant siting potential exists in California and the Southwest
Tens of gigawatts of high-quality CSP resources are located in the western Mojave Desert and across
Arizona, Nevada, and New Mexico, often near existing transmission corridors. This resource is
comparable in size to that of offshore wind but more accessible.
Capacity-expansion shows CSP plays a major role when considered as an option in modeling tools
NREL’s RPM modeling finds approximately 9 GW of CSP deployed in California by 2045 under SB 100
planning assumptions, providing ~15% of in-state generation and displacing over 30 GW of PV, wind,
and batteries.
Stakeholders are receptive—if barriers are addressed.
Utilities, agencies, and community stakeholders recognize the potential value of CSP but cite permitting
complexity, transmission constraints, cost viability, and past project performance as concerns. Updated
designs and improved availability data address many of these issues.

11.2 Implications for California’s SB 100 Goals
The study indicates that achieving 24/7 clean electricity—especially under high electrification and
climate-driven load growth—requires a portfolio that includes long-duration, dispatchable renewable
resources. CSP power plants could:
 • reduce reliance on natural gas during evening and nighttime hours
 • provide firm renewable capacity aligned with RA and Slice-of-Day needs
 • offer rotational inertia and grid stability
 • support baseload supply for emerging large-load sectors, including data centers, and
 • reduce curtailment of solar by dispatching CSP into the evening
The capabilities of CSP closely match the reliability features needed in a future grid with high solar
penetration and complement geothermal, offshore wind, and long-duration batteries.

                                                                                              Page 67 of 77

11.3 Recommended Actions for Developers and Industry
CSP developers and technology providers should:
 • Be “at the table” in California to promote the value of CSP
 • Develop standardized reference designs reflecting best practices from global deployments
 • Plan multi-unit CSP parks to capture scale economies
 • Develop hybrid CSP + PV designs to optimize cost and grid value
 • Expand domestic supply chain capacity for tanks, valves, heat exchangers, and heliostats
 • Enhance O&M automation, system controls, and quality assurance
 • Engage early with communities and labor partners to build trust and maximize local benefits

11.4 Recommended Actions for California
Include CSP explicitly in California’s planning models.
 • Incorporate CSP technologies and cost options into the CPUC IRP process.
 • Integrate CSP into CEC SB 100 Joint Agency modeling.
 • Work with CAISO and NREL/E3 to ensure CSP is represented in RESOLVE.
Align procurement frameworks to recognize CSP’s value proposition.
 • Ensure the RCPPP fully values both clean energy and clean capacity provided by CSP.
 • Allow CSP to compete for RA needs under Slice-of-Day rules.
 • Consider multi-decade procurement structures (≥30 years), similar to offshore wind.
Integrate CSP into transmission planning.
 • Evaluate CSP-suitable regions (e.g., western Mojave; NV/AZ/NM interfaces) within the 20-year
 transmission outlook.
 • Consider coordinated transmission buildouts for multi-project CSP parks.
Streamline permitting and environmental review.
 • Create joint CEQA/NEPA templates for CSP.
 • Clarify siting guidance for BLM lands and high-DNI corridors.
 • Resolve outdated wildlife screens and update BLM/DRECP layers using current habitat data.
Initiate a CSP Long-Lead-Time procurement track.
 • Use the LLT process to select, prepare, and anchor a first CSP development zone with shared
 infrastructure.

11.5 Federal/State Public-Private Partnership Opportunities
Public-Private Partnerships have been used to effectively deploy CSP projects internationally. A joint
state–federal-private CSP Power Park model—similar to Morocco’s MASEN approach—could sharply
reduce cost and risk while accelerating learning. The partnership would:
 • Identify and select the best sites for CSP development.
 • Once a site is selected, it could develop and permit the site and provide the necessary
 infrastructure including interconnection substation and transmission to the site, water supply,
 roads, fencing, and security.
 • Projects would be competitively bid to be sited there to provide best value.
 • Long-term PPAs of 30-years or longer would be provided that are structured to value both RA
 (capacity) and RECs (carbon-free energy).

                                                                                            Page 68 of 77

 • Provide access to lower cost public financing and/or share in the equity ownership in the
 projects.
 • As scale is important for driving down costs and gaining the maximum economic benefit to local
 communities, projects should be structured to be multi-phased developments that are
 sequentially constructed in a larger power park setting.
 • Projects could be hybridized with PV to provide cost reduction and to provide a baseload
 renewable power resource.

11.6 Recommended Pathway for California to Reap the Benefits of CSP
A PPP project should:
 • Use commercially mature molten-salt tower technology
 • Include 10–16 hours of TES
 • Use dry cooling which is now the standard in arid regions
 • Be sited in a high solar resource area near existing HV transmission
 • Include strong community benefits, domestic content, and labor standards
 • Optionally include PV and electric heaters for hybrid operation
 • Be procured under a multi-decade tariff through the LLT or RCPPP pathway
This approach would demonstrate reliability, cost, and availability, build confidence in the technology,
and establish a template for multi-project expansion.

11.7 Final Remarks
As California deepens its commitment to 24/7 clean energy, the limitations of short-duration storage
and variable renewables become more pronounced. CSP with thermal energy storage offers a proven,
dispatchable, carbon-free alternative capable of meeting these emerging reliability needs. The results of
the CalCSP Study indicate that CSP is technically ready, economically viable, and strategically aligned
with California’s long-term decarbonization pathway. With thoughtful planning and coordinated policy
support, CSP can play a meaningful role in strengthening the Western grid and advancing California’s
clean energy leadership.

 Title: Technical Assessment of Page: 2 of 17 Doc. No.: DOE-SD-09809 - 1

Introduction
Tower concentrating solar power (CSP) technologies represent the second generation of CSP
technology following the first-generation oil-based parabolic trough systems. The tower CSP plants are
a point-focused configuration that involves a field of two-axis tracking heliostats (sun-tracking mirror
assemblies) that reflect light onto a central tower receiver. This setup with a nitrate molten salt
working fluid allows for higher temperature operation of the receiver and direct thermal energy
storage (TES). The ability of CSP to couple with TES is its main advantage over photovoltaic panels,
which are significantly cheaper if storage is not considered – the TES system allows CSP to provide
baseload or dispatchable clean energy. Therefore, tower CSP with long-duration (8+ hours) of TES
represents the biggest value proposition for this technology.
Molten-salt tower technology was developed initially by the US Department of Energy (DOE), Sandia
National Laboratories, and California and southwestern utilities. A molten salt power tower CSP plant
with TES was first demonstrated in the US at the 10 MW Solar Two pilot plant with 3 hours of TES was
successfully operated for 3 years by Southern California Edison [1]. Following Solar Two, the first
commercial plant went online in Spain in 2011; the 20 MW Gemasolar plant with 15 hours of TES is still
operating successfully today [2]. The next major plant of this type was the 110 MW Crescent Dunes
plant in Nevada with 10 hours of TES; Crescent Dunes represented approximately 5x scale up from
Gemasolar (over 10x scale up from Solar Two) and was the first of a kind project for the technology at
this scale. Ivanpah is another first-of-a-kind tower CSP plant in the US with over 100 MW production,
but it generates superheated steam directly in its central receiver to run the power plant and lacks TES
(and therefore molten salt components), thus it is not considered directly comparable; the heliostat
field is the main component of Ivanpah that can be compared to Crescent Dunes and other CSP tower
plants with storage. Based on this history, tower CSP (and CSP in general) has a strong history of
development and manufacturing occurring in the US.
Following Crescent Dunes several CSP tower plants with molten salt TES and 100 MW capacity or
higher have been built around the world including: Morocco, United Arab Emirates, Chile, South Africa,
and China [3], [4].
