Molten salt CSP plants with 50 MW or higher capacity are summarized in Table 1.; as more of these
plants are built and commissioned around the world companies such as ACWA Power and Cosin Solar
are gaining significant industry experience. ACWA power is responsible for 3 of these projects
worldwide, the latest being the Redstone project in South Africa which was first connected to the grid
in September 2024. Cosin Solar is the first company to surpass 1 GW of installed capacity, mostly due
to projects based in China. In the past decade China has built out more CSP tower capabilities than any
country, with 470 MW installed as of December 2024 and 2,920 MW under construction and planned
to start operation within the next few years. The increased deployment of this technology around the
world has increased the commercial maturity of several components and spurred international
collaboration for further technology development and standardization.
In 2020 NREL released the CSP Best Practices Study based on feedback from stakeholders in the
industry, the report published details of issues identified at operating CSP plants along with the
approaches taken to address those issues and/or alternative potential solutions [3]. Since this report,
additional progress has been made towards understanding and addressing some of the remaining
critical issues. For molten-salt tower plants, the key issues experienced at the first plants were related
to the hot salt storage tank and the salt to steam heat exchangers known as the steam generator. In
the following sections, these and other reliability concerns in the first molten-salt tower plants are
reviewed, and important advancements since the CSP Best Practices Study are discussed in detail.

 Title: Technical Assessment of Page: 4 of 17 Doc. No.: DOE-SD-09809 - 1

Technical Discussion
1 Hot Salt Tank
1.1 Function and Design
The hot tank receives salt from the receiver and supplies salt to the steam generator for power
generation. The tank operates at atmospheric pressure due to the very low vapor pressure of the
molten salt storage media, and is similar, but not identical, to tanks used for oil storage. As such, a
common design code is API Standard 650, Welded Steel Tanks for Oil Storage; however, the nominal
hot tank design temperature is 565 °C, while the maximum design temperature in API Standard 650 is
260°C. To account for higher temperature operation and estimate the fatigue damage, the industry has
typically referenced the ASME Boiler and Pressure Vessel Code, specifically the procedures in Section
VIII Division 2, Alternate Rules for the Construction of Pressure Vessels for fatigue damage, and data
from Section II, Materials regarding allowable stresses. The state-of-the-art design is a vertical tank
with a flat bottom and a self-supporting domed roof; the shell is manufactured completely from
stainless steel 347H. The wall is welded from several courses, rings made of steel plate, that are
thickest at the bottom and thinner at the top, while the floor includes a thicker perimeter section
directly underneath the wall and a thinner central section welded from large rectangular plates; the
floor also tends to be sloped for drainage with the center sitting higher than the perimeter. The tanks
generally operate with relatively consistent minimum and maximum operational levels around 1 – 10
m, driven by the minimum required pump head and geotechnical considerations. The first large scale
tanks (capacity ≥ 1 GWh) designed and built following these recommendations have failed within the
first few years of operation. Recent investigation into these failures determined that there were many
similarities occurring at different projects around the world, making it clear that the design
methodology used to date was inadequate [6]. Common failures including buckling of the floor leading
to a very short lifetime, and cracking initiated in the floor and lower wall welds are discussed in more
detail in the following sections.
1.2 Failures, Sources, and Solutions
Based on recent analysis of this component, several design and operational changes are proposed to
improve the reliability of the tank:
 • The stresses due to friction are inversely proportional to the thickness of the floor. By doubling
 the floor thickness stresses are reduced by about 50 percent and the resistance to buckling is
 quadrupled.
 • The friction stresses in the floor are 1) proportional to the square root of the tank diameter,
 and 2) proportional to the depth of the salt inventory. As such, the availability of the storage
 system can be improved by selecting two 50-percent, or perhaps three 33-percent, capacity
 tanks.
 • The stresses due to radial temperature gradients can be reduced by designing inlet distribution
 systems that ensure mixing across the entire floor area and controlling the receiver outlet
 temperature to limit the temperature difference between the inlet and bulk salt temperatures.

 Title: Technical Assessment of Page: 5 of 17 Doc. No.: DOE-SD-09809 - 1

 • The stresses due to friction can be reduced by controlling the receiver outlet temperature such
 that the temperature rise in the inventory is limited to 3 to 5 °C if the tank is more than 50
 percent full
 • The stresses in the wall-to-floor weld joint can be reduced by the following:
 o Accurately controlling the foundation grading at the perimeter of the tank. This ensures
 that the annular plate beneath the wall is properly supported and does not place
 bending loads on the welded connection.
 o Providing a rigid support (refractory bricks, steel plate) beneath the wall to prevent
 cyclic loadings, due to changes in the inventory level, from displacing the foundation
 material at the outer edge of the annular plate.
 • The tank floor is susceptible to buckling during the commissioning. Recommendations to avoid
 buckling during pre-heating and initial filling of the tank include thermal gradients in the floor
 should be minimized during these operations, avoid adding or removing salt when high thermal
 gradients are present in the floor, avoid large changes in bulk temperature when filling to the
 maximum level.
 • The tank shell material (both base material and weld filler) should be carefully reviewed
 considering the welding procedure and operation in the creep regime. At minimum a new weld
 filler, such as E16.8.2 is recommended as opposed to the matching E347 filler generally used to
 date [7].
 • The reliability of the floor can be improved by adopting more rigorous QA/QC weld inspection
 procedures, including, perhaps, some of those from the nuclear industry.
 • An array of perhaps 40 to 50 thermocouples can be installed on the floor to detect the
 progression of radial and tangential temperature gradients. If the gradients reach pre-
 determined levels of potentially damaging stress, then the operators can take corrective
 measures.
Implementing most of these changes will result in a more expensive tank; however, improved design,
fabrication procedures, and process control will significantly extend the tank lifetime. Different aspects
of the tank failure and the motivation behind the proposed design changes are discussed in more detail
below.
1.2.1 Buckling in the Floor
1.2.1.1 Failure
In at least 4 of the commercial tanks in service, buckles have developed in the floor. The presence of
buckles does not result in an immediate failure of the tank. However, the top of the buckle has
plastically deformed, and the buckled region can ‘flex’ as the tank is operationally cycled. Ultimately
the creep-fatigue lifetime of the buckled regions is compromised and appears to be on the order of
only a couple of years.
1.2.1.2 Source
The floor is a large, flat thin sheet, and as such, it has a low resistance to buckling when loaded in
compression. Compressive forces can develop due to friction and radial temperature gradients on the
 Title: Technical Assessment of Page: 6 of 17 Doc. No.: DOE-SD-09809 - 1

tank floor. Damaging conditions can occur during tank preheating prior to filling, and during daily
operation depending on the specific process conditions.
1.2.1.3 Solution
Based on engineering reviews of operating tanks, the radial temperature gradients seen during
preheating could be better controlled by the use of multiple nozzles for distributing the combustion
gases used to initially heat the tank during commissioning of the tanks. The current industry standard is
to use a single nozzle.
Radial temperature gradients during normal operation can be mitigated by improvements to the salt
distribution system and/or the control system to limit the difference in temperature between the salt
entering the tank and the temperature of the inventory in the tank.
Furthermore, the critical buckling stress of the floor is inversely proportional to the tank diameter to
floor thickness ratio, i.e., smaller diameter tanks with thicker floors are more difficult to buckle.
Depending on the specific process the tank is being designed for, some combination of a smaller tank,
thicker floor, redesigned salt and/or air distribution systems, and improved process control will
prevent the buckling failure.
1.2.2 Weld Failures
1.2.2.1 Failure
In at least one commercial project, leaks have developed in the floor at weld locations. In another
commercial tank, voids have been detected in the welds in the floor. In at least 3 commercial tanks,
cracks have developed in the bottom course of the wall.
Normally, if a crack develops in a weld, the failed region can be removed by grinding, and repairs
conducted by re-welding. However, type 347H stainless steel, discussed below, is very difficult to re-
weld due to the segregation of alloying elements. The segregation leads to local areas of low strength,
strain localization, and a failure process known as hot reheat cracking [7].
1.2.2.2 Source
All of the commercial tanks built to date use Type 347H stainless steel as the material. This material
was selected for its increased resistance to intergranular stress corrosion cracking compared to 304H.
However, under the right conditions 347H is susceptible to stress relaxation cracking (SRC), and
evidence of this failure has been observed in at least one commercial tank and is suspected in other
projects based on the welding procedure and operating conditions (but not confirmed with additional
metallurgical testing). SRC is accelerated by high temperatures and high stresses. The high stresses can
be the result of residual welding stresses or plastic deformations (i.e., buckled regions).
The tank floor is fabricated by welding an array of large rectangular plates, they are welded from one
side with backing plates to help form a suitable root pass. Incomplete weld fusion between adjacent
plates can produce small voids between the bottom of the root pass and the top of the backing plate.
The voids can act as stress concentration points. If there are deficiencies in the floor welds, then
alternating stress can lead to low cycle fatigue failures.

 Title: Technical Assessment of Page: 7 of 17 Doc. No.: DOE-SD-09809 - 1

1.2.2.3 Solution
NREL and partners are investigating the SRC failure in detail [7], [8], and are currently exploring
alternative materials and weld fillers for these tanks. Some alternative materials have been proposed;
however, the data base on fatigue and creep properties of the materials are perhaps not as extensive
as for Types 347H or 304H, which may add some uncertainty to the lifetime estimates of the tank if
these materials are selected. Post-weld heat treatment for 347H is another potential solution, but this
is expected to be extremely challenging due to the large flat-bottom tank geometry and relatively
stringent temperature profiles to be maintained.
For the floor welds, alternative fabrication procedures are being explored. This will also be influenced
by any changes in thickness to the floor that may occur to mitigate other failure mechanisms in the
tank. At a minimum, some improvement in weld quality control and filler material will be necessary. A
more extreme solution proposed is to elevate the floor above the foundation during fabrication; this
allows welding to be conducted from both sides of the floor, eliminating the need for backing plates
and simplifying the inspection process. Based on the specific tank design and process conditions
different solutions may be appropriate.
1.2.3 Design Standards
1.2.3.1 Failure
Commercial tanks are based on the following codes and standards: API Standard 650; ASME Section II;
and ASME Section VIII Division 2. However, none of these consider time-dependent properties, i.e.,
creep. The elevated temperature of the CSP tower molten salt tanks and that the combination of
residual and operational stresses are high enough to initiate damage due to creep. Finite element
studies that account for the residual stresses from welding estimate creep-to-rupture times of only
several years [6].
1.2.3.2 Solution
The tank design should account for both creep and fatigue damage mechanisms and include residual
stresses to the extent they can be estimated. Due to the high uncertainty associated with estimating
the residual and operational stresses in the tank, it is recommended to design this component to a
lifetime of at least 1.5 – 2 times the expected plant lifetime to minimize the potential for failure during
plant operation. Potential codes for tank design to satisfy these criteria include ASME BPVC Section III
Division 5, and API 579-1 / ASME FFS-1. Including the effects of creep can reduce the allowable stress
by a factor of 3 compared to only fatigue calculations as in ASME BPVC Section VIII Div. 2, and
accounting for the residual stresses from welding further reduces the allowable operational stress.
However, these failure mechanisms must be considered for the tank to last the lifetime of the plant.
1.3 Conclusion
The experience from more recent molten salt tower plants indicates that plants using smaller tanks
and/or thicker floors, using improved QC during construction, and improved control during operation
are not experiencing the failures seen in the first largescale hot salt tanks.

 Title: Technical Assessment of Page: 8 of 17 Doc. No.: DOE-SD-09809 - 1

2 Salt Steam Generator
2.1 Function and Design
The steam generator converts the thermal energy in a flow of hot salt into a combination of flows of
live steam and reheat steam at the temperature and the pressures required by the Rankine cycle. The
steam generator system (SGS) consists of 3 heat exchangers responsible for sensible heat transfer
(preheater, superheater, and reheater), 1 heat exchanger responsible for latent heat transfer
(evaporator), and a steam drum for collection.
2.2 Fabrication
The 4 heat exchangers are typically straight shell designs, with fixed heads, flat tubesheets, and U-tube
bundles. The high pressure water/steam are on the tube sides, and the low-pressure salt is on the
shell sides. The most common approach for connecting the tubes to the tubesheets is to 1) strength
weld the ends of the tubes to the tubesheets, and 2) plastically deform (expand) the tubes into the
holes in the tubesheets.
Most of the sealing capability between the tube side fluid and the shell side fluid is provided by the
expansion operation. This results, in effect, a friction connection between the tube and the tubesheet.
To ensure that the friction connections between the tube and the tubesheet do not relax due to cyclic
thermal stresses, the allowable rate of temperature change for the heat exchangers is typically limited
to 10°C/min. The heat exchanger vendor will also typically define an allowable number of thermal
fatigue cycles. A representative value is (3 start/stop cycles per day) * (330 operating days per year) *
30 years = 30,000 cycles.
2.3 Operation
The SGS is generally operated as follows:
 - During overnight hold periods, cold salt is circulated through the heat exchanger train for freeze
 protection.
 - During startup, the hot salt pump is started, and hot salt is blended with cold salt at a mixing
 station upstream of the superheater / reheater. The flow rate of hot salt is controlled such that
 the rate of temperature change is limited to 10 °C/min.
 - During normal operation, the flow of cold salt is stopped, and only hot salt enters the steam
 generator train.
 - During shutdown, the startup process is essentially reversed.
2.4 Damage Mechanisms
The Electric Power Research Institute (EPRI) has performing an extensive investigation on the damage
mechanism for Rankine Cycle components in CSP plants, including all components in the steam
generator system (SGS) [9]. In addition, the heat exchangers making up the SGS are discussed in detail
in the CSP Best Practices report [3]. Based on these studies the following issues have been identified
leading to premature failures in the steam generator components:

 Title: Technical Assessment of Page: 9 of 17 Doc. No.: DOE-SD-09809 - 1

 - Thermal fatigue: poor process control during start-up and shut-down procedures can lead to
 temperature transients beyond the vendor specified limits, drastically reducing the fatigue
 lifetime.
 - Leakage between the tube and tubesheets: in flat tubesheet designs, the difference in thermal
 response between the thin-wall tubes and the thick tubesheet can result in flexing of the
 tubesheet, causing failures and leakage at the connection between the tube and tubesheet.
 - Pitting Corrosion: has been an issue at certain CSP plants; however, the mechanisms leading to
 this failure are well understood and attributed to improper feedwater chemistry.
 - Creep: expected to be the dominant failure mechanism for high temperature components such
 as the superheater/reheater; however, no failures of this type have been reported to date.
 - Flow-accelerated corrosion: expected to be a failure mechanism for low-temperature
 components of the SGS such as the economizer/pre-heater; however, no failures have been
 reported to date.
Although there can be more than one steam generator in a CSP plant, the two systems may not be
completely independent. At one plant of interest, each steam train (i.e. series of heat exchangers for
steam generation) can be started independently, but for both steam trains to run they must be started
simultaneously; similarly, if both trains are started simultaneously, they must be stopped
simultaneously. This design feature results in an added availability penalty if 1 steam train must be
taken offline due to the mechanisms listed above.
2.5 Solutions
Different solutions need to be adopted to address the different failure mechanisms in the steam
generator system components.
 - Thermal fatigue: this damage mechanism can impact most of the components in the steam
 generation system. The temperature gradients and thermal transients in the various heat
 exchangers can be improved through revisions to the process design such as a split-range
 control valve to improve the hydraulic stability of the system during start-up. It is
 recommended to follow the approach to system control that was successfully demonstrated at
 the Solar Two project [1].
 - Design improvements for creep and fatigue damage: an all-welded design for the shell-and-
 tube heat exchangers, particularly in the evaporator, is expected to improve the designs
 resilience to thermal transients thus reducing the thermal stresses generated during start-
 up/shut-down and the operating stresses for creep damage. The all-welded designs are
 estimated to be 20 to 30% more expensive than the typically used expanded tube design but
 could significantly improve the steam generator system availability based on experience from
 operating plants.
 - Corrosion mechanisms: material selection and feedwater chemistry are the critical aspects for
 both pitting and flow-accelerated corrosion. EPRI has been investigating these issues for CSP
 plants and is expected to release a “best practices” guideline document for CSP operators [9].

 Title: Technical Assessment of Page: 10 of 17 Doc. No.: DOE-SD-09809 - 1

3 Other Issues
Several issues have been identified since the first implementations of the molten salt tower CSP plant
at commercial scale. The previous sections discussed the reliability issues that are still of biggest
concern today; in contrast this section discusses issues that have caused significant downtime at
multiple plants but have since been improved upon.
3.1 Heliostats
The solar field is made up of many heliostats (10,000+ in large projects), each reflecting the sun onto
the receiver. Therefore, heliostats are a major factor impacting the cost and performance of tower CSP
technology. Since the first large-scale tower plants were constructed, the commercial experience with
manufacturing and deployment of heliostats has increased significantly. For this component,
experience is not limited to molten salt plants with storage, but all types of CSP tower plants.
Heliostats have reached commercial maturity under a restricted group of major global stakeholders,
such as BrightSource who supplied the heliostats for the three Ivanpah plants and several other large
CSP installations since then. In addition to the strong commercial track record built over the past
decade, the US government launched the Heliostat Consortium (HelioCon) in 2021 with the goal of
further developing the technology towards achieving better cost and reliability goals [10]. From a
technical point of view the optical accuracy of a heliostat, in terms of drive pointing error and mirror
slope error, is one of the critical design criteria for the heliostat vendor because the optical accuracy
has significant implications for the central receiver design and overall efficiency of the plant.
Performance issues are mainly attributed to various opto-mechanical errors and lack of metrology
techniques to verify reflector performance in-situ. Heliostat design efforts are focused on achieving
high optical accuracy that can be maintained throughout the lifetime of the plant at lower cost.
In general, the reliability of heliostats has been good with issues more related to reduced performance
and poor control. The key failure mechanisms are highlighted below [3], [10]:
 - Optical Performance: heliostat optical errors lead to increased spillage losses that result in the
 plant not being able to achieve performance targets.
 - Wind Load Design: wind loads have not been appropriately considered in heliostat design to
 date. Critical load cases for the structure are not well defined, and there is a lack of knowledge
 regarding how wind loads impact tracking errors and optical performance.
 - Heliostat Control System: open-loop control with manual calibration procedures is standard in
 most CSP applications. This control scheme has been shown to work well in many cases but has
 contributed to consistent under-performance in some projects. Closed-loop control schemes
 are under development but may not be required with industry adoption of best practices from
 the projects with better heliostat control strategies.
 - Hail Damage: Although hail has generally not been a significant issue at most plants, it is
 becoming more of a concern in the U.S. where hail damaged a few percent of the mirrors at the
 Ivanpah plant.
The main issue for heliostats has been the lack of industry standards and a lack of validated tools to
characterize the optical performance of heliostats when they are manufactured and when they are
 Title: Technical Assessment of Page: 11 of 17 Doc. No.: DOE-SD-09809 - 1

installed in the solar field. As mentioned previously, HelioCon and other international efforts are
working to develop industry standards for heliostats and the tools needed to validate the optical
performance of heliostats. Additionally, HelioCon is working to better understand static and dynamic
wind loading experienced by heliostats, and the influence of wind on optical performance.
3.2 Heat Trace
Failures of the heat trace cables, junctions and transformers for heat tracing can cause significant plant
downtime, particularly in the downcomer piping. Heat trace cables are installed underneath fixed
piping insulation making it a labor intensive repair. The cause of these failures were attributed to the
following:
 - Improper installation:
 o Thermocouple locations not to specification resulting in inadequate response.
 o Insulation for heat trace element tails (welded or soldered junctions) not to specification
 leading to failures of the junction.
 o Incorrect cable length installed on heat trace circuits can result in excess heat in certain
 areas leading to overheating and pipe rupture in extreme cases, or insufficient heat in
 other areas leading to overloading of the cables and/or sections of frozen salt.
 o No back-up circuits were installed, increasing downtime when failures did occur.
 - Improper design:
 o Loads on transformers not properly balanced, leading to high loads on certain units and
 persistent transformer failures.
 o Interlocks to prevent overheating required on certain circuits, e.g. if flow is required or
 pipe must be filled. A single damaging operation without the correct interlocks can be
 sufficient to permanently damage the heat trace cables.
 o Design in switch or control system command for start-up operation of heaters. For high
 temperature heat trace, vendors generally recommend starting up heating at 50%
 power in order to prevent damage to cables during initial heating.
 o On/off control is easy to implement but can result in excessive on/off cycles of heat
 trace cables depending on application, PID control is preferred to extend cable lifetime.
In summary, downtime due to failures of the heat trace system can be reduced significantly with
proper installation and quality control. Backup circuits should be installed and damaged circuits must
be identified and replaced before installation. Furthermore, coordination between vendors is crucial to
ensure transformers and cables are sized, installed, and controlled appropriately [3].
3.3 Downcomer Pipe Supports
Excessive vibrations in the downcomer and other salt piping has been a significant issue at a minimum
of two CSP tower plants. A common approach for salt pipe supports is a ring of calcium silicate around
the pipe to reduce heat losses by avoiding metal on metal contact from the pipe to the support; this
ring is held in place by an external metal clamp that is supported. Excessive pipe vibrations observed in
plants with this design are attributed to a couple of issues:

 Title: Technical Assessment of Page: 12 of 17 Doc. No.: DOE-SD-09809 - 1

 - The calcium silicate ring was found to have a limited lifetime due to daily expansion/contraction
 and pipe vibrations, often cracking after about 1 year of operation. Now it is recognized as best
 practice to install a metal support web and to compensate for the additional heat losses with
 additional heat trace cable as needed.
 - Vibrations due to specific flow control strategies are used at different plants, the approach
 demonstrated at Solar Two used an outlet vessel with a level control signal and throttle valves
 located at the base of the downcomer. This approach has been adopted in several commercial
 plants. An alternative approach demonstrated at Sandia involves several orifice plates in series
 and was adopted at one commercial project, referred to as ‘cascade flow’; this approach
 removed the need for the control valve, outlet vessel, and an additional overflow drain line
 subject to frequent freezing at Solar Two, but also introduced significantly more pipe vibrations.
 - Insufficient piping supports both around the pipe and along the length. With the calcium ring
 design, the vibrations can be reduced by supporting the metal ring from multiple locations
 around the pipe instead of just one side. For all designs the vibrations during different
 operating modes must be properly accounted for, e.g., with cascade flow described above a full
 dynamic analysis of the system must be performed to evaluate pipe vibrations through
 different modes of operation.
These issues were covered in detail in the CSP Best Practices Report published by NREL [3]: the outlet
vessel approach has been implemented at a greater number of operating commercial plants and can
work reliably given appropriate: outlet vessel design for the full range of transient conditions, heat
tracing of the drain line, and valve selection. The cascade flow approach can potentially be a
simplification to the system but introduces additional system vibrations, but this method requires
further refinement and demonstration before being considered commercially mature.
3.4 Molten Salt Valves
Valve selection is critical for reliable molten salt operation, this is another topic covered in detail in [3],
with the main points summarized here. Reliability of molten salt valves has been a persistent challenge
since the Solar Two demonstration project, where initially ball valves were specified for isolation and
globe valves for process control. While the globe valves were generally successful (more details below),
the ball valves tended to ‘bind’ in molten salt service due to corrosion between the ball and the sealing
rings. This experience has led to triple-offset butterfly valves being adopted for isolation. However,
there are still questions regarding the best stem material for salt service with this design. In addition to
leaks and binding, improper design of the process control valves can lead to system instabilities,
excessive pipe vibration, and erosion. Some of the more critical best practices for reliable and smooth
molten salt valve operation are highlighted below
 - A globe valve with bellows seals and butt-weld connections is the only valve recommended
 both for process control and isolation, all other types of valves have a high risk of leaks
 developing. The main reliability concern with this type of valve is freezing of salt in the bonnet
 resulting in leaks, which can be prevented with heat tracing; therefore, the heat trace is design
 and implementation is also critical for successful valve operation.

 Title: Technical Assessment of Page: 13 of 17 Doc. No.: DOE-SD-09809 - 1

 - For the downcomer throttle valves, hydraulic actuators are preferred to help prevent
 oscillations in the plug position. Regardless of the type of actuator used, the force produced by
 the actuator should have a margin of 50 – 100% over the calculated force to prevent
 oscillations at small valve openings.
 - In general, due to the high specific gravity of nitrate salts and subsequent high kinetic energy in
 the flow, special consideration must be given to the sizing of the valves and actuators
 considering the full range of dynamics conditions. Inadequate sizing of the valve or actuator can
 result in vibrations, erosion, and unstable flow control.
The full list of best practices for molten salt valves can be found in [3]. Globe valves with bellows seals
and positioners are expensive to implement for all control and isolation valves, providing plant
designers motivation to search for alternatives valve configurations. However, to date, no alternatives
have demonstrated the same reliability.
3.5 NOx Generation
In two commercial projects, cracks in the floor have allowed 1,000 to 1,200 tons of salt to leak into the
foundation. This has led to increased heat losses from the tank which reduces the electric output of
the project, and formation of hazardous NOx gases due to the reaction between the salt and certain
foundation materials. Several design changes have been demonstrated to address this issue:
 - Other projects have adopted a refractory brick with refractory sand directly underneath the
 tank and there is a selection of refractory materials that do not react with the salt. This
 refractory material can be dense or porous and there may be different layers of different
 refractories that make up the thermal foundation before reaching the concrete layer. Although
 there is no chemical reaction, leaking salt can still saturate the refractory foundation material
 and increase heat losses from the tank.
 - At least one plant has implemented a salt leak detection system that routes any leaks from the
 tank shell to a system of drain channels embedded in the foundation; this system effectively
 prevents salt from diffusing into the thermal foundation and provides prompt notification in the
 event of a leak. This system has successfully detected multiple leaks since it was installed.
 - A continuous metal sheet could be installed between the bottom of the sand layer and the top
 of the foundation insulation. Should a leak occur, salt will flow to the edge of the foundation
 and avoid exposing the insulating foundation materials to liquid salt.
The experience with the first generation of commercial scale molten salt storage tanks has
unfortunately included several loss of containment events (see Section 1). Given the elevated risk of
leaks in these storage tanks compared to oil storage tanks, the compatibility of the foundation
material(s) with the salt storage media must be verified in the design phase.

 Title: Technical Assessment of Page: 14 of 17 Doc. No.: DOE-SD-09809 - 1

4 Reliable Major Components
Although the first tower CSP Plants have been plagued with significant reliability issues that have
severely affected the availability, some major components have exceeded expectations. This section
discusses aspects of molten salt tower CSP plants that have been generally successful.
4.1 Receiver
The central receiver accepts solar radiation from the solar field and transfers it to the molten salt
working fluid. The prototype design from Solar Two has been generally adopted in commercial plants:
molten salt flows through several panels of alloy tubes arranged in a cylindrical configuration at the top
of the tower. The central receiver, together with the salt tanks, constitutes a single point failure in
many commercial tower projects and can therefore cause significant availability issues. Unlike the hot
salt tank, the receiver has been generally reliable in commercial projects to date. Although the receiver
system from Solar Two was plagued with availability issues, commercial projects have been able to
mostly learn from that experience and achieve higher availability. One of the issues at Solar Two was
the amount of time required to replace panels when an issue occurred. At least one commercial
project has demonstrated the capability to replace individual tubes instead of a full panel, significantly
reducing downtime if only a few tubes need to be replaced. It is estimated it could take 1-2 weeks to
replace a full panel in a commercial project, but this operation has not been performed to data to the
authors’ knowledge. Other issues include freezing of receiver tubes and vent lines that have not been
as frequent after the experience at Solar Two. However, at least one commercial plant did freeze a
receiver panel due to an insulation defect which caused significant downtime, but did not require
replacement of the panel [3].
Another concern is regarding the fatigue life of the tubes: the receiver tubes are designed for a 30 year
lifetime, but this relies on a specific flux profile from the solar field which may not be met due to issues
with the heliostats (see Section 3.1). Poor performance of the heliostats increased spillage losses which
in turn reduces the power output. To increase the power output, operators will adjust the aimpoints
of the heliostats. There are concerns that this adjustment puts higher flux on certain tubes of the
receiver and is reducing the low cycle fatigue life below the 30-year design point. However, to date no
fatigue failures have occurred in commercial projects, until more data is available for this damage
mechanism it is advised to design the receiver conservatively to account for uncertainty in the incident
flux profile [3].
4.2 Molten Salt Pumps
The salt pumps are generally long-shafted turbine pumps mounted above the tanks (on a separate
structure) and inserted through the roof of the tank. This design eliminates the need for a separate
pump sump tank and a control valve for each tank. The pumps shafts can be 12 m or longer; while
there was concern that this length would be problematic, in practice the pumps worked well and have
proven more reliable than control valves for molten salt applications. The issues that have arisen are
mainly due to the installation and alignment of the pumps, the support structure must be designed
with a healthy safety factor to not allow any misalignments, and the installation must follow the
vendor specifications which may include a rate (in m/h) at which the pump can be inserted into and
 Title: Technical Assessment of Page: 15 of 17 Doc. No.: DOE-SD-09809 - 1

removed from the tank. Best practices for these molten salt pumps for tower CSP projects specifically
are well documented in [3].

Conclusions
This document provides a summary of the issues encountered in the first commercial molten salt
tower plants. In retrospect, some items that were perceived as key design challenges, such as the salt
pumps and central receiver, worked well, while other aspects of the first plants were plagued with
significant reliability issues. Based on lessons learned discussed in previous sections, there is high
confidence future CSP tower plants can be significantly improved, and this is already occurring in the
next round of plants built worldwide. While 110 MW Crescent Dunes plant, the first commercial plant
of its kind, achieved a maximum annual net electricity generation of 195.8 GWh in 2018; in
comparison, the 100 MW Cerro Dominador plant produced 235.7 GWh in 2022. Meanwhile in China,
the Qinghai SUPCON Delingha 50 MW plant has generated over 140 GWh for the past 3 years, and the
Shouhang Hi-Tech Dunhuang 100 MW plant has generated 235 GWh or more the past 2 years. Given
that China has a substantially lower solar resource, these numbers indicate the availability and thus
production of these plants is increasing, and it will continue to do so as further experienced is gained.
Although tower CSP technology is behind other renewable energy sources in terms of commercial
maturity, progress is being made to address the performance and reliability issues that have impacted
the first commercial projects. As the industry grows worldwide, it is imperative that any new tower CSP
project leverage the lessons learned in the last decade of commercial installations to avoid repeating
costly errors.

 Title: Performance Model Inputs and Assumptions Page 2 of 16 Doc. No.: DOE-SD-09809-3

1 Introduction
This document records the methodology, inputs, and assumptions used to model the annual performance of the
solar technologies analyzed within the CalCSP project.
2 Overall Process
A unique Performance Model (PM) was used for every technology of interest which includes:
 • Molten Salt (MS) Tower
 • Modular MS Tower
 • MS Trough
 • Oil Trough
 • PV + Battery Energy Storage System (BESS)
The PM’s were connected to cost models that predicted the total installed cost of each technology. This allowed
calculation of normalized metrics such as Levelized Cost of Electricity (LCOE) which allowed comparison of
technologies.
2.1 Allowable Dispatch Periods
For each PM, the periods of the day when net generation to the grid was allowed must be specified. Three
different scenarios were explored:
 • Baseload
 o PM allowed to generate any period of the day.
 • Outside PV generation
 o PM allowed to generate outside of PV generation period (defined below).
 • Outside PV+4-hr BESS generation
 o PM allowed to generate outside of PV+4-hr BESS generation period (defined below).

2.1.1 PV and PV+BESS Generation Periods
First, a System Advisory Model (SAM) model of a PV + BESS plant was created. This is a DC-coupled system as
shown in Figure 1.

 Figure 1 PV+BESS configuration
The AC capacity of the AC/DC inverter array is fixed at 101.1MWe (closest to 100MWe with discrete number of
central inverters).
The user specifies:
 • Site location and weather file
 • BESS hrs (including 0)

 Title: Performance Model Inputs and Assumptions Page 3 of 16 Doc. No.: DOE-SD-09809-3

This PV or PV+BESS system is allowed to dispatch in any hour of the day.
The DC capacity of the PV array is first found through an iterative loop where DC capacity is varied until the
annual clipped energy from the PV array equals a user specified value. Five percent clipped energy was used in
this study.
Once the PV DC capacity is found, the annual performance is analyzed to determine when PV+BESS does not
generate. This is done by looking at a 12x24 dispatch chart. This is driven by two user parameters:
 • capacity_threshold = 50% (screening analysis) 20% (detailed assessment)
 • count_threshold = 10 (screening analysis) 20 (detailed assessment)
For each timestep, the script counts the number of occurrences that generation is below (capacity_threshold X
AC Capacity) and places this count into the 12x24 table. If the count is equal or greater than count_threshold
then this hour is defined as PV+BESS not generating. These non-generating PV+BESS hours are then marked as
allowable generation hours for the subsequent solar technologies of interest. Figure 2 presents the allowable
dispatch hours for a PV only and PV+4hr BESS system at Barstow, CA.

3 Molten Salt Tower
Figure 3 presents the overall modeling framework for the MS Tower technology where each aspect is defined
below.

 Figure 3 MS Tower overall modeling framework
3.1 Collection System Optimization
A standalone SolarPilot model was used to generate the solar collection system (heliostat field + receiver +
tower). This allowed for more control over the optimization process than SAM’s integrated SolarPilot which has
limited user parameters. The heliostat design was fixed using:
Heliostat Definition
 • Brightsource LH2-4 design (installed at Ashalim project)
 • 20.8 m2
      •   5.2 m x 4.03 m
 • 4 mirror facets
 • Focused at slant range
 • On-axis canting at slant range
 • 1.5 mrad slope error in x and y axis
Two overall collection systems were considered:
 • 600 MWt design duty receiver with no land constraint
 • Square mile land boundary
Design DNI
The design DNI is used to determine the number of heliostats needed to satisfy the receiver’s design capacity.
Design DNI can be seen as a design parameter that alters the ratio of solar field capacity / receiver capacity. It
allows the designer to under or oversize the field where the optimum will result in a small amount of dumping
(on the order of 5%) when simulated using an annual performance model and actual weather conditions. Thus,
the optimum design DNI is not known ahead of time, and three design DNI values of 900, 950, and 1000 W/m2

 Title: Performance Model Inputs and Assumptions Page 5 of 16 Doc. No.: DOE-SD-09809-3

were used to generate three unique collection systems. Each collection system resulted in a different solar field
size, and the optimum is found after running the PM.
Square mile land boundary
With a fixed land boundary, the optimum receiver size is not known. A range of receiver duties were modeled
(400, 450, 500, 550 MWt). For each receiver duty, the three design DNI values above were used resulting in a
total of 12 collection systems (4 receiver duty x 3 design DNI). The optimum collection system was found after
running the PM.

3.3 Cost Model
An Excel based cost model is used to calculate the total installed cost. The MS tower cost model was developed
by Aelius under contract with the CalCSP project. The cost model scales commercial costs from the Redstone
project to the configuration under consideration.

4.2 Cost Model
The oil-trough utilizes NREL’s oil-trough cost model as its basis. The main changes include:
 • updated solar field cost to Sunbeam costs as published in “2020_NREL_Manufacturing Cost Analysis of
 Advanced Parabolic Trough Collector"
 • Updated O&M cost model
 • Increased material cost factor on PB to 1.22. This was done during SMUD study project to match the
 model’s PB cost to estimate from Black and Veatch.
 • Set interest during construction in 0, this is captured in Financial model.
 • Added a land area input, used this for land cost of project plant.
 • Added electrical parasitics cost
 • Updated labor cost factor to 1.89 for LA
 • Added unit land cost to inputs, and connected to land cost calculation in Cap$ tab.

The MS-trough PM was developed under the SMART project (DE-EE0008140). It is an Excel GUI that connects
Excel based design models to the Physical Trough model from SAM using the SAM API.

model.

5.2 Cost Model
The MS-trough utilizes the MS tower cost model with the following changes to reflect the different solar
collection system:
 • Removed heliostats, tower, receiver, and riser/downcomer piping
 • Added parabolic trough solar field and field piping using bottoms-up cost model developed in SMART
 o Includes heat tracing on solar field piping
 o Uses same collector cost as oil-trough model
Note: The MS storage mass is calculated based on delta between cold and hot operating temperatures. Thus,
no changes to the cost model were needed other than inputting correct operating temperatures.

6 PV + BESS
The PV + BESS model uses SAM’s PM and its overall cost structure.
6.1 Performance Model
SAM Version 2021.12.02 is used for the PV+BESS PM. The PV module and inverter were updated to be more
reflective of current state-of-the-art products being installed at utility scale projects.
