4 Economic Assessment of Potential CSP Sites
In order to assess the economic potential of different sites, we developed a simplified levelized
cost of energy model that could use the GIS data to estimate the cost of a molten-salt tower
constructed at each location. To calculate the LCOEs, GIS data is exported for locations of
interest, run through a Python program script to generate the LCOEs for varioius scenarios,
then the LCOEs are uploaded to the GIS system so that the results can be analyzed. This section
describes the LCOE methodology used.

4.1 Levelized Cost of Energy (LCOE):
We used the LCOE calculation from the NREL 2024 ATB to compare the cost of energy
producted at different sites.15 The LCOE can account for differences in CAPEX or OPEX, different
performance, or different financial assumptions that may apply at individual sites. We

15
  NREL 2023 Electricity ATB, Equations and Variables in the ATB,
https://atb.nrel.gov/electricity/2023/equations_&_variables

 Title: CSP Siting Assessment for the Page: 32 of 48 Doc. No.: DOE-SD-09809 - 3

calculated a real LCOE based on the economic assumptions used in the 2024 ATB, using the
LCOE equation below:

 𝐹𝐶𝑅 × 𝐶𝐴𝑃𝐸𝑋 + 𝐹𝑂𝑀
 𝐿𝐶𝑂𝐸 = + 𝑉𝑂𝑀 + 𝐹𝑈𝐸𝐿 − 𝑃𝑇𝐶
                                  𝐶𝐹 × 8,760(ℎ𝑜𝑢𝑟𝑠/𝑦𝑟)

The NREL LCOE calculation allows either the production tax credit (PTC) or the investment tax
credit to be accounted for. For CSP systems, given the high capital cost of current systems,
using the ITC provides a better incentive to the project. The NREL LCOE calculation shows the
PTC directly in the LCOE formula. The ITC, on the other hand, is accounted for in the fixed
charge rate (FCR).
 𝐼𝑇𝐶
                                      [1 − 𝑇𝑅 × 𝑃𝑉𝐷 × (1 − 2 )] − 𝐼𝑇𝐶
 𝐹𝐶𝑅 = 𝐶𝑅𝐹 ×
 (1 − 𝑇𝑅)

Reference Plant Configuration: The capital cost, O&M cost, and the performance were taken
from the Solar Dynamics reference baseload molten-salt tower plant design for a location in
Arizona.
Table 2 Molten-salt Tower Reference Baseload Plant Configuration

 Turbine size 100 MW gross
 Receiver size 600 MW net
 TES 14 hours
             CAPEX                              $7,000 /kW
             FOM                                $150 /kW-yr
             VOM                                $0.003 /kWh

The LCOE calculation is corrected for the following locational factors:
 • Solar resources at the site:
 • Regional cost differences:
 • Single plant vs. power parks
 • Remoteness of site for labor and transportation.
 • Distance to existing transmission substation or high voltage transmission line
 • Transmission wheeling charges (sending power from out of state to CA)
 • Seismic loading
 • Energy communities (10% ITC adder)

Solar Resource: DNI data used in the analysis comes from the NREL NSRDB 2k dataset for 2019-
2022. 16 This dataset only relies on four years of data. We decided that a more accurate spatial

16
 NREL NSRDB. https://nsrdb.nrel.gov/data-viewer

 Title: CSP Siting Assessment for the Page: 33 of 48 Doc. No.: DOE-SD-09809 - 3

distribution of the DNI was important rather than using the longer term 4km x 4km TMY
dataset that has 25 years of data from 1998 to 2022.

The performance for a particular site is then calculated using the curve fit of annual net
generation for locations in the region of interest shown in Figure 22. For tower plants, there is a
second order influence of the latitude of the performance, thus the DNI is the primary influence
on the plant performance. However, from the figure below it appears that a linear fit as a
function of annual DNI is a reasonable first order approximation. Ambient temperature can also
have a significant influence on plant performance. The analysis did not account for ambient
temperature effects.

Figure 22 Generation as a function of DNI for baseload reference MS tower plant.

 Title: CSP Siting Assessment for the Page: 34 of 48 Doc. No.: DOE-SD-09809 - 3

Regional Cost Factors: In order to account for regional cost differences, we used NREL’s ReEDS
Regional Capital Cost Factors for CSP. The cost factors were scaled such that Tucson Arizona the
site of our reference plant cost estimate, had a cost factor of 1.000.

Figure 23 NREL ReEDS Capital Cost Factors for CSP

Power Park: Cost reduction factors were used to scale costs for multiple plants constructed at
the same site. Separate scaling factors were used for CAPEX and OPEX costs. The scaling factors
were developed as part of the Solar Dynamics Dispatchable Solar Power Plant project (DE-

 Title: CSP Siting Assessment for the Page: 35 of 48 Doc. No.: DOE-SD-09809 - 3

7/11/2025 Solar Dynamics LLC
EE0007579).17 The scaling cost reduction was limited to a maximum of 10 plants in the analysis.
The results are shown in Figure 24 assuming a maximum of 4 plants at any given location.

Figure 24 Power Park - Multi-Plant Cost Reduction Factor

This factor becomes a very important driver in the cost analysis and identification of preferred
sites. A more careful assessment of the potential benefits of power parks is justified. The
manner in which cost reduction is achieved is important. Projects must be constructed in a
coordinated manner to achieve the maximum potential benefit. Building individual projects in
an uncoordinated manner may not achieve any cost savings. A more careful assessment of the
cost implications of development of CSP plants in power parks is warranted. For the analysis,
we only assumed half the savings for the OPEX factor.
Remoteness of site: It has been seen from existing plants that the remoteness of the site can
have a significant impact on the cost of construction and operation of a plant. We have
estimated this impact by determining the distance to the nearest large urban population center
greater than 50,000 residents. Table 25 was used to estimate this impact based on our
judgement. A more careful assessment of the cost implications of site remoteness is warranted.

Table 3 Site Remoteness Cost Correction Factor

 Population
 proximity Cost
 Case miles Factor
 1 < 10 1.00
 2 < 20 1.01
 3 < 30 1.03
 4 < 45 1.05
 5 < 60 1.07
 6 < 90 1.10
 7 > 90 1.15

17
 Dispatchable Solar Power Plant Project,
https://www.osti.gov/biblio/1418902-dispatchable-solar-power-plant-project

 Title: CSP Siting Assessment for the Page: 36 of 48 Doc. No.: DOE-SD-09809 - 3

Transmission interconnection costs: The analysis looked at the cost of building a transmission
line from each location to the nearest substation (230kV or higher) or building a transmission
line to the nearest transmission line and adding a new substation.

 - Interconnection cost: $10 M per plant, each plant.
 - Cost to construct a new substation: $30 M.
 - Transmission line costs: $3 M per mile.

In this analysis, we assumed the transmission line would be shared by 4 plants. An
improvement might be to calculate the cost for the actual number of plants at a site, limiting it
to the maximum line capacity. Also, the transmission line length assumes a direct route to the
substation. Some allowance should be added to allow for a less than direct TL route. In general,
a more careful assessment of the cost implications of transmission access and interconnection
is warranted.

Transmission Wheeling Charges: We assumed a $6/MWh adder for wheeling power into
California from a non-CAISO interconnection point. This is a reasonable first order estimate for
firm delivery wheeling charges for power coming from Arizona or Nevada into California.

Seismic Loading: A GIS seismic dataset was used that showed the probability that ground
motion will reach a maximum peak horizontal ground acceleration (the fastest measured
change in speed, for a particle at ground level that is moving horizontally due to an earthquake)
with a 10% probability of exceedance in 50 years. Maximum vertical accelerations of
approximately 1g are seen in some regions in California. A quadratic curve fit was used to
estimate the cost increase as a function of seismic loads. The general assumption made was
that civil costs would double for the maximum seismic loads. A more careful assessment of the
cost implications of seismic loads for the tower and other structures is warranted. Table 4
shows the cost factor assumed for different seismic design loads.

Table 4 Seismic Loading Cost Correction Factor
 Case Seizemic Range Ave Cost Factor
             1    1-5                 2.5      1.002
             2    5-10                7.5      1.005
             3    10-15              12.5      1.009
             4    15-25               20       1.014
             5    25-40               33       1.023
             6    40-60               50       1.035
             7    >60                 80       1.055

Energy Communities: The Inflation Reduction Act included a 10% adder to the ITC for projects
constructed in qualifying Energy Communities. A location qualifies as an energy community if it
is a:

 - Brownfield site,

 Title: CSP Siting Assessment for the Page: 37 of 48 Doc. No.: DOE-SD-09809 - 3

7/11/2025 Solar Dynamics LLC
 - Metropolitan statistical area or non-metropolitan statistical area (MSA or non-MSA)
 with 0.17% or greater direct employment or 25% or greater local tax revenues related
 to the extraction, processing, transport, or storage of coal, oil, or natural gas at any
 time after Dec. 31, 2009, and an unemployment rate below the national average for the
 previous year, or

 - Census tract which has a coal mine or coal power plant that closed after Dec. 31, 1999,
 or a census tract directly adjoining a census tract with a coal mine or coal power plant
 that has closed.

DOE has created a website that identifies qualifying energy communities for MSA/non-MSA
FFE, and coal mine and coal plant closures. 18 Figure 25 shows a view of the energy
communities for California and the surrounding states. We have integrated this data into the
GIS data and accounted for the Energy Community 10% ITC adder where it is appropriate.

Note, when the analysis was conducted, the ITC was available for CSP plants. The ITC was
recently cancelled for solar technology for projects starting construction after July 4, 2026. It
appears that portions of a CSP plant may still qualify for the energy storage ITC.

18
  Energy Community Tax Credit Bonus, Website:
https://arcgis.netl.doe.gov/portal/apps/experiencebuilder/experience/?id=a2ce47d4721a477a8701bd0e08495e1d

7/11/2025 Solar Dynamics LLC

Figure 25 Energy Community - Coal plant closures and Qualifying Fossil Fuel Employment MSA/Non-MSAs

Other cost factors: Development, permitting, and land costs vary depending on the state where
projects are being sited and depending on the type of land that is being used (federal, private,
state, or tribal). These factors can also affect the project timelines as well. To account for these
effects, we used the following adjustments to CAPEX.

 CA 1.03 Incremental CAPEX factor for development in California
 BLM 1.02 Incremental CAPEX factor for developments on BLM land
 BIA 1.05 Incremental CAPEX factor for developments on tribal lands
 BLM + CA 1.05 Incremental CAPEX factor for developments on BLM land in CA

It should be noted that no learning curve cost reduction has been included in this analysis,
other than multi-plant builds at a single site. Costs represent current technology costs in 2021
USD.

4.2 LCOE Results
Figure 26 shows the LCOE results for molten-salt tower project in the western U.S. (California,
Arizona, Nevada, Utah, New Mexico, Colorado, and western Texas). The dots on the figure show
the location of the sites considered for CSP. The size of the dot defines the resource potential
for the site, in terms of megawatts of baseload CSP plants that can be sited in that location. The
color of the dot indicates the estimated levelized cost of electricity at that location, green
indicating the lowest LCOEs, and red indicating the highest LCOEs. Sites with more siting
potential are assumed to benefit from cost savings associated with building solar power parks
(two or more plants developed at the same site). Each LCOE color grouping shown in the key in
Figure 26, each represents 10% of the CSP siting potential identified. The LCOEs are based on
our reference baseload CSP plant configuration and assume a 30% ITC and 10% energy
community bonus where appropriate.

Figure 26 Assessment of MS Tower CSP Resource Potential and Cost [Real LCOEs 2021 dollars]

The results show significant low-cost siting potential in Arizona, New Mexico, and Texas. Costs
are generally higher in California, Nevada, Utah, and Colorado, although some lower cost sites
are present in each state.

 Title: CSP Siting Assessment for the Page: 40 of 48 Doc. No.: DOE-SD-09809 - 3

7/11/2025 Solar Dynamics LLC
Figure 27 zooms into the sites in and around California that could supply CSP power to
California. The LCOE figures in this chart include transmission wheeling charges to the price of
electricity for sites outside of the California Independent System Operator (CAISO) boundaries
(the non-CAISO portions of Nevada and Arizona, Utah, and New Mexico). The blue lines
represent the CAISO transmission planning regions in the RESOLVE model. The most economic
CSP sites in California appear to be in the Kramer CAISO transmission region. Sites in the Central
Valley, Riverside, and Imperial CAISO regions do not appear to be as economically attractive. A
number of sites in Arizona, New Mexico, and Nevada appear to be more attractive than most
sites in California.

7/11/2025 Solar Dynamics LLC
Figure 28 shows the LCOE as a function of siting potential by state. Again New Mexico and
Texas stand out as offering the lowest cost sites and have a large amount of land that is
available for siting of CSP plants. Arizona comes in third in terms of low-cost siting potential and
has a large amount of land available of siting of CSP plants. Note that a large fraction of the
Arizona siting potential is on tribal lands, that might not be available in practice. Interestingly,
Colorado comes in ahead of Nevada and California, likely due to the higher assumed CAPEX in
Nevada and California. Although California has some of the highest direct normal solar
resources in the U.S., much of the land is protected from development or screened out for
other reasons, so the land available with the best resource potential is limited. High CAPEX
costs in California mean that the cost of power is higher than most of the other states in the US
southwest. Although Nevada has a lot of siting potential, because of its proximity to California
and the remoteness of the sites, the cost of power appears significantly higher than other
southwestern states.

Figure 28 CSP LCOE Siting Potential by State

 Title: CSP Siting Assessment for the Page: 42 of 48 Doc. No.: DOE-SD-09809 - 3

7/11/2025 Solar Dynamics LLC
Figure 29 shows the LCOE as a function of siting potential by CAISO RESOLVE Model
transmission planning regions and for the adjoining states. The chart includes a transmission
wheeling charge cost adder for sites outside of CAISO. Sites in the Kramer RESOLVE region
provide the lowest cost sites within CAISO’s boundaries for CSP. All other regions within
California have appreciably higher LCOEs. Some low-cost potential siting exists in the CAISO
Eldorado region in southern Nevada, and the CAISO southern Arizona regions. Interestingly, the
New Mexico and the non-CAISO portions of Arizona appear to offer significant low cost CSP
siting potential if transmission for sending power to California is available. Although sites within
California are relatively limited at the lower cost ranges, substantial CSP siting capacity appears
to be possible in surrounding states (tens of gigawatts) if transmission is available or can be
built to transport power to California. We did not include Colorado and Texas for consideration
for supplying power to California. Texas is largely in a different interconnect (ERCOT), and the
CSP sites in Colorado are further from California than many better sites in Arizona and New
Mexico.

Figure 29 CSP LCOE Siting Potential for California by CAISO RESOLVE Region

 Title: CSP Siting Assessment for the Page: 43 of 48 Doc. No.: DOE-SD-09809 - 3

7/11/2025 Solar Dynamics LLC
4.3 CSP for Powering Data Centers

Power demand for new data centers is resulting in significant growth in demand for power for
the first time in decades. The power demand for data centers is mostly a baseload power
profile. As data centers' power demand is largely responsible for the growth in new power
demand, there is pressure to make sure that data centers are powered with carbon free
generation. Due to low-cost thermal energy storage, molten-salt tower CSP technology is well
suited for supplying either baseload or nighttime generation for data centers. Thus, CSP as a
stand-alone baseload plant or a hybrid configuration with PV for supplying power during the
day and CSP for supplying power at night, could be an attractive option for supplying carbon
free baseload power to data centers. Data centers require high reliability of power supply and
are likely to need either grid power as back up, dedicated natural gas backup capability, or
both. CSP plants can be hybridized with natural gas, using natural gas to run the plant on days
when solar input is reduced or not available due to cloudy weather.

NREL developed a map of the United States showing the location of data centers and the fiber
optic network backbone. 19 Data centers were initially located near key population centers, but
over time data centers are being located along the existing fiber optic network in areas where
there is an abundance of cheap power. The fiber optic backbone typically runs near major US
highways and interstates. We have extended the CSP siting analysis to look at areas where CSP
plants can be sited near the existing fiber optic network. We used the US highways and
interstates to define the path of the fiber optic network. We then looked at sites within a
particular distance of the US highways and interstates. Figure 30 shows the overlay of CSP
Siting potential within 20 miles of the existing fiber optic network. Similarly, Figure 31 shows an
overlay of the MS Tower CSP Resource Potential and Cost [Real LCOEs 2021 dollars] for sites
within 20 miles of the existing fiber optic network.

A number of good CSP sites exist withing 20 miles of the existing fiber optic network in New
Mexico, Texas, Arizona, Nevada, and California.

19
  Roberts, B 2025, Data Center Infrastructure in the United States, 2025 (Map),
https://www.nrel.gov/docs/gen/fy25/94502.jpg

5 Conclusion

The CalCSP study looked at whether CSP could be a potential low-cost solution to help
California meet its SB100 goal of 100% carbon free retail generation by 2045. There is a
question whether there are sufficient sites available for CSP plants. To answer this question,
this report documents a resource potential screening methodology for CSP like the one the CEC
has used for determining the resource potential for utility scale PV generation in California. We
adopted the CEC approach and modified it to include siting considerations unique for CSP
molten-salt tower plants. We found that the BLM environmental and land use screens used by
the CEC effectively screened nearly all the high solar resource regions in the California desert
areas. We were a little less restrictive and allowed some desert regions to be included in the
screening analysis. We also expanded the screening analysis to include all the southwestern
states including Arizona, Nevada, New Mexico, Utah, Colorado, and the western half of Texas.
After identifying areas potentially suitable for development of molten-salt CSP plants, we
estimated the LCOE at each of the sites to economically rank the potential sites.

Using the original, very restrictive BLM environmental and land use screens used by the CEC,
there is only about 1 GW of CSP siting potential in California in prime solar locations. However,
by relaxing some of the environmental and land use screens, this figure increases to about 10
GW of prime solar resource areas with siting potential within the CAISO boundaries. Most of
the expanded CSP siting potential identified was in the Western Mojave Desert in the Kramer
RESOLVE transmission planning region. This area had been previously screened for the Mojave
Ground Squirrel, but recent sampling has not found the MSG present in a number of the areas
identified for potential CSP development.

The analysis also found that there appears to be economically attractive siting potential for tens
of gigawatts of CSP in Arizona and New Mexico if adequate transmission is available or
constructed to deliver the power to California.

 Annex E
 Final Report for CSP Tower Public Opinion

 Final Report for CSP Tower Public Opinion
 and Education Project

                                 DOE Project DE-0009809
 CSP Plant Optimization Study for the California Power Market

                                      October 11, 2024

Submitted to: Hank Price
 Principal Investigator

 Hank.Price@SolarDynLlc.com

Report by: Allison Lenthall
 Lenthall Advisors LLC
 allison@lenthalladvisors.com

Context
As seen in the headlines, and documented by several Lawrence Berkeley National Lab reports,
globally and nationally, communities are delaying or canceling large-scale renewable energy
development projects due to lack of community acceptance. 1 Concerns include using land
intended for food production instead for "fuel," lowering property values, developing land in rural
areas which disturbs pristine ecosystems and views. Other reasons cited include perceived
health risks due to toxicity of materials, the perceived environmental and wildlife risks, as well as
divisive opinions caused by partisan politics.

In the past, developers had not pursued community engagement until the project was far
underway for various reasons, such as not having a firm grasp on the financials and lack of
clarity on whether or how much they needed to engage. It has been shared anecdotally,
whether true or not, that developers have offered money directly to the county supervisor who
was responsible for siting, thus leaving much of the rest of the community to feel as though they
didn’t receive any benefits, or worse, that this was a form of bribery. There hasn’t been much
trust or collaboration between the developer and the host community. From a diversity, equity,
inclusion, and accessibility (DEIA) perspective, those who are socially, culturally, and
economically diverse most likely feel left out of the conversation and the benefits.

In order to break the barriers of community opposition, and encourage host communities to
support large-scale renewable projects, there must be a sincere, meaningful relationship with
the project throughout all the project stages (development/construction/O&M), with in-person
contact, and mutually satisfying benefits that reach all of those directly and indirectly affected.
Interactions must begin early in the project planning process and must be transparent, including
the explanations of advantages and risks, as well as all of the associated benefits at the state,
local, and individual levels.

Project objectives
As part of a project for the U.S. Department of Energy on the use of concentrating solar power
(CSP) in California, Solar Dynamics decided to include the public’s opinion of the technology
and an exploration of the community engagement that is needed to support such development.

The initial objectives for the California and Nevada public perception activities were twofold. The
primary objective was to gather public opinion and feedback from the communities living near
solar power tower plants, Ivanpah and Crescent Dunes, and to distill lessons learned from the
community engagement conducted before, during, and after the plants were developed to
inform future development. This included outreach to nearby airports. The other objective was to
gauge public opinion about large-scale solar, specifically CSP towers, to start educating the
public on the benefits and to begin building relationships with communities of interest, initially
targeting the Kingman, Arizona area. The objectives shifted after the first exploratory trip to
Ivanpah and Kingman, however, as it became apparent that it would be challenging to gather
public opinion from the community surrounding Ivanpah that could be useful for other
community profiles, and the company wasn’t ready to address the concerns in Kingman.
Another area was chosen, therefore, to represent those where future development is possible.

The recently published Lawrence Berkeley National Lab Perceptions of Large-Scale Solar
Project Neighbors Study2 exemplified public perception polling based on social science and
served as the foundation for the survey questions taken to the field. The intent was to ensure
that people knew their input was valued and that the time they spent was valuable for the
participant as well. It has been noted in the literature that in-person interaction has greater
benefits than activities online or via mail, as well as limits the expense.

Public opinion from communities with CSP towers
Ivanpah Solar Electric Generating System (Ivanpah, CA, San Bernardino County)
After preliminary research, I visited Ivanpah to further understand community concerns and
inform engagement strategies.

Ivanpah consists of three 450-foot-tall CSP towers, covering 3,500 acres (about 5 square miles)
in California, roughly 9 miles from Primm, Nevada. Operational since the end of 2013, the 392
MW plant can be seen from about 25 miles away, depending on terrain. It’s also listed as an
attraction on TripAdvisor.

The glow from one of the towers was visible from around 22 miles away.

The closest population, approximately 1,100 people, are resort/casino employees residing about
4.5 miles away in the Primm Valley resort area—a demographic not easily comparable to other
U.S. communities. Most interactions with the plant come from
passersby, plant employees, or casino staff, making their
opinions biased or challenging to collect.

An anecdotal account from a gas station employee indicated
general indifference to the towers. She directed me to
Primm’s grocery store, the Market at the Desert Oasis, within
a subsidized housing complex for about 650 resort
employees. The resort owns the market, and I couldn’t get
permission to survey there.

Another engagement opportunity, the Primm Valley Golf Club, located 1.5 miles from Ivanpah
between I-15 and the plant, provides a clear view of the towers from the parking lot and several
holes.

The General Manager emailed, “We have very little if no interaction with the solar plant. They do
stop in for lunch, which we certainly appreciate. Outside of that, we have no opinion either way
regarding their operation. I can say the same for our customers.” A waitress echoed this
sentiment.
Conversely, a golfer expressed that the towers were distracting, particularly when facing them
directly on certain holes and under specific lighting conditions. (Photo from GolfTop18.com).3

Unfortunately, the General Manager declined further engagement, which could have been
insightful for future community engagement efforts.

Ivanpah is visible about 23 miles away while driving through the Mojave National Preserve.

Crescent Dunes Solar Energy Project (Tonopah, NV, Nye County)
To gauge community acceptance of CSP solar tower plants with storage, Solar Dynamics LLC
engaged with the community around the Crescent Dunes Solar Energy Project in Tonopah, NV.
Through three advance telephone interviews and in-person surveys, key insights were derived
about the community’s opinion of the solar plant..

Located about 15 miles from Tonopah, Nevada, 190 miles northwest of Las Vegas, and 235
miles from Reno, NV, Crescent Dunes features a 110 MW molten-salt concentrated solar tower
and 1.1 GWh of energy storage. Crescent Dunes is the first commercial concentrated solar
power (CSP) plant with a central receiver tower and advanced molten salt energy storage
technology at full scale (110 MW), following the experimental Solar Two and Gemasolar in
Spain at 20 MW. Developed by SolarReserve and Cobra Thermosolar Plants, Inc., and now
owned by ACS, construction started in September 2011, and the plant began operations in
September 2015. Built on public land, it received a $737 million loan guarantee from the U.S.
Department of Energy and had a long-term power purchase agreement (PPA) with NV Energy.
Over the past decade, technical and construction issues have caused several operational stops,
the loss of the PPA, and bankruptcy.

Tonopah, the unincorporated county seat of Nye County, NV, has a population of approximately
2,280, with an employment rate of 35%, a median household income of $35,947, and about
13.6% of residents holding a bachelor’s degree or higher.

Crescent Dunes has a similar presence on social media as Ivanpah, mostly positive, inquisitive
comments and photos, with occasional negative comments about being out of operation, etc.
One thing noted, however, was the occasional prideful post from construction workers. Crescent
Dunes is also on TripAdvisor and has a small visitors area with a covered space and panels
explaining the technology.

During challenging years, SolarReserve removed their website and minimized public interaction,
leaving the plant's status largely to conjecture.

The molten-salt tower is visible from town when operating.

One-on-one interviews
Three one-on-one telephone interviews were conducted with local town residents to gain insight
into the local population's opinions about the solar project. This information helped to shape the
survey interview questions. The three residents, selected based on the recommendation of
Town Manager Joe Westerlund, shared their recollections of community engagement before

and during the plant's construction, as well as general thoughts they and their neighbors had
about the plant.

A brief description of the interviewees and key takeaways from the conversations can be found
in Appendix 1.

In-person surveys collected in Tonopah, NV
Across two days, Wednesday, July 31, and Thursday, August 1, 2024, public opinion surveys
were administered in Tonopah. A "survey station" was established in front of the U.S. Post
Office in Tonopah to capture the highest foot traffic, as residents typically collect their mail there.

 Most individuals who were approached
 engaged in conversation and shared their
 views, resulting in eighty-two completed
 surveys; twenty-nine via smartphone or
 tablet, and fifty-three on paper. The
 majority of the general population either
 didn’t have a phone, use flip phones, or
 prefer not to use their phone. Those who
 declined the survey typically gave no
 specific reason or cited time constraints,
 and some perceived the survey as political.
 Participants were offered five dollars for
 completing the survey, though a few
 declined the incentive.

The survey questions, based on the Lawrence Berkeley National Lab’s Perceptions of Large-
Scale Solar Project Neighbors, were crafted to capture the most information possible about the
residents’ current attitude, their experience during the community engagement process, and
what is important to them.

Key takeaways from the surveys
The summarized responses, complete list and detailed responses are listed in Appendix 1. This
data represents the public opinion of a community that lives near a solar power tower.

Key opinion results
 ● 40% of respondents said they had a positive or very positive opinion of Crescent
 Dunes; 40% were neither positive nor negative.
 ● About 79% of respondents see Crescent Dunes on a regular basis (every day /
 occasionally); 49% every day.
 ● 10% more of respondents rated the aesthetics of the tower, receiver and mirror field
 as pleasing rather than displeasing.
 ● 56% respondents said they would support or strongly support future solar
 development in the area, while 15% opposed or strongly opposed.

 ● When asked if a similar plant were built and was operating at its full potential:
 ○ Approximately 59% of respondents felt it would improve the quality of life in
 the community
 ○ 44% thought it would improve life specifically for them
 ○ 55% feel it pays its fair share of local taxes
 ○ 61% feel it would increase American energy independence
 ○ 44% believe it would help address climate change.
 ○ At least a third of the respondents thought the plant improved or greatly
 improved the local economy in both the short and long-term, and a third
 thought it worsened or greatly worsened the beauty of the landscape.
 ● Regarding the planning process of Crescent Dunes, most of the respondents
 answered they neither agree nor disagree (24-43%), however, this was a question
 with a high level of negativity.
 ○ 26% thought they were not provided enough information about the plant.
 ○ 38% feel they do not receive enough information about the plant now that it’s
 in operation.
 ○ 29% felt the developer did not go the “extra mile” in community engagement
 and listening to the community
 ○ 32% felt the developer did not keep the promises made
 ○ 24% responded that the government officials’ decisions about the plant were
 not made in the best interest of the community
 ● Regarding public involvement during the planning of Crescent Dunes, the results
 were split:
 ○ 23% responded that the public was not involved,
 ○ 45% that the public was just kept informed,
 ○ 22% that the public provided input
 ○ almost 11% that the public input influenced the project
 ● When asked their opinion about the importance of several elements in regard to a
 future plant being sited in their area:
 ○ 72% thought it was very important or essential that it be owned by U.S.
 companies
 ○ 62% thought it was important or essential that it have no foreign investment
 (and 23% thought it was not important)
 ○ 84% thought it was very important/essential to hire local employees (within 90
 miles)
 ○ 76% thought it was important/essential to purchase materials from U.S.
 businesses.
 ○ 86% thought it was important/essential to provide workforce training
 ○ 85% agreed it was important/critical to provide more community benefit

Feedback from area airports regarding solar glare
There was negative press4 regarding pilot complaints about the solar glare from solar power
towers, so inquiries were made to the Harry Reid International Airport in Las Vegas and Nye
County airports for current feedback.

Harry Reid International Airport, Las Vegas: Conversations with Christa Schueler, the Clark
County Senior Planner of Aviation, revealed that there were numerous initial complaints about
glare from pilots. As a response, the Federal Aviation Administration (FAA) included Ivanpah on
the Visual Flight Rules (VFR) aeronautical chart to alert pilots. Although a Form 7460-1 for
Notice of Proposed Construction or Alteration was likely filed with the FAA, current feedback
indicates that complaints have subsided, leading to a redirection of further inquiries to the FAA,
from which no response has been received.

Nye County Airports (Tonopah, Beatty and Gabbs): Darrin Tuck, the Utilities Superintendent
for Airport Operations & Maintenance of Nye County, reported via email that he was not aware
of any ongoing complaints from pilots regarding solar glare.

An Army helicopter pilot based in Yermo, CA, provided anecdotal feedback about the
experience of flying past the Ivanpah facility. This input is particularly valuable as it comes from
a professional regularly navigating the airspace in the vicinity of large solar installations:
 “So for me flying, I fly generally at a lower level than like a typical aircraft or airliner and
 they (the towers) haven't really been an issue. They're definitely really bright. And we
 tend to try to not look right at them but I mean transitioning along the interstate uh it's not
 an issue for us necessarily.
 They're on charts that we use. They're marked, there's a big warning. It's like solar glare
 warming or whatever. Um, so, yeah, anyone who's flying and is doing the right thing and
 using the right charts should know that they're there and they know what to do.
 Generally speaking, it's like just don't stare at it.“

Public opinion and initial educational efforts in communities of interest
Kingman, Arizona
According to DataUSA,5 Kingman, AZ, located about 125 miles southeast of Las Vegas, NV,
has a population of 33,100. The median age is 42, and the median household income is
$56,360 as of 2022. Kingman's population is younger than that of nearby areas, and its
economy is expanding, largely driven by tourism due to its location along Historic Route 66. The
area is predominantly politically conservative.

 Interesting insights gleaned from casual conservations in Kingman included:
 ● Water is going to be the main concern and people are very territorial about water.
 ● The power produced should stay local.
 ● There has been push back when other solar companies have tried to come in,
 especially due to water.
 ● The windmills to the south “have always been there” but the new ones up north just
 “appeared” (hinted not much community engagement because there aren’t many
 residents up there).

A conversation with Bennet Bratley, Kingman Director of Economic Development and Tourism,6
gave these takeaways:
 ● The largest issue will be water and which aquifer the plant would draw from (City of
 Kingman Aquifer will draw a lot of backlash, Griffith/Laughlin not as much)
 ● There wasn’t much backlash on the wind turbines; apparently the project had been in
 the works since 2010; but it is a very political “football,” and this is a very
 conservative area
 ● There have been many plants that have been proposed and never built; at the time
 there was a moratorium on new renewable energy projects 7 because of “sites being
 snatched up and then not used.” Bennet gave the impression that there is “solar
 plant proposal fatigue;” the residents are tired of hearing that plants are being
 proposed but then not being built.
 ○ Apparently, a Chinese company had proposed a CSP tower plant near
 Laughlin and it didn’t move forward (unclear as to why)
 ● Kingman is an interesting location because there is rail, I- 40, and the future
 Interstate 118 so there are a lot of manufacturers who are looking to move in; a 700
 acre expansion to the 65 acre Industrial park near the airport has been approved
 ● I-40 Trade Corridor Project:9 with the intent to capitalize on the hydrogen/electric hub
 and CA mandates for trucking to convert to hydrogen/electric, the area is looking to
 build fueling stations, drop-off, etc., since they are so close to CA.
 ● He recommended that if there are further efforts, the community will want more
 concrete details (the location of the proposed plant, water sources, etc.).

After learning more about the community concerns in Kingman, especially that the residents
would want to know information about actual plans, and taking into consideration their
“proposed development fatigue,” it was decided that different communities, Barstow and Silver
Lakes, California, would serve as proxies for communities where development could be

targeted. This would preserve Kingman as a future area for development once the concerns
could be addressed.

Barstow, California
Barstow, CA, a desert city off Interstate 15, is situated 115 miles from Los Angeles and 156
miles from Las Vegas. According to the 2020 Census data,10 it has a population of 25,415, with
an employment rate of 49.1% and a median household income of $52,200—significantly lower
than California's median of $95,521. The median age in Barstow is 31.7 years, and about 10.4%
of its residents hold a bachelor’s degree or higher. The community stretches approximately 11
miles along Interstate 15.

In-person surveys collected in Barstow, CA
Locating a suitable place to access Barstow residents for surveys presented significant
challenges due to the city's sprawling layout and heavy traffic of tourists heading to Las Vegas.
Corporate policies prevented surveying outside grocery and big box stores, complicating the
search for communal gathering spots.

On Saturday, September 14, surveys were conducted at the Yermo Fall Fest, a suburb of
Barstow located near the Marine Logistics Base, with a population of 623. The event took place
on the economically disadvantaged side of town, approximately 11 miles from central Barstow.
Despite its modest size, the 5-year-old annual festival attracts visitors from neighboring areas,
especially Barstow.

During the 4.5-hour event, 51 surveys were collected, primarily on paper due to poor cell
coverage, reportedly affected by recent wildfire damage. Each participant received $5.00 as an
incentive, and the inclusion of other exhibitors helped achieve a broad participation base.

 By using visuals, the intent was
 not only to understand the public
 opinion but also to educate the
 public about CSP.

On Monday, September 16, 2024, another 55 surveys were gathered at Barstow Community
College within a designated public free-speech area. Participants were compensated with $5.00
or a Kind Bar for their time. The college, which serves about 3,700 students across various
degree and certificate programs, has a diverse student body, including a significant number of
ESL students. The surveys were made available in both English and Spanish to accommodate
the community's linguistic diversity, and visuals were also used.

Silver Lakes, CA
Silver Lakes was also selected as a site for surveying to enable a comparison of opinions from a
distinct demographic within the Mojave Desert. This region is recognized as one of the
promising locations in the United States for concentrated solar power (CSP) technology with
thermal storage. Notable CSP installations in California include the now-decommissioned SEGS
projects and the operational Mojave Solar project at Harper Lake using trough technology, and
the Ivanpah project employing tower technology near the Nevada state line off I-15. The nearest
CSP plant to Barstow and Silver Lakes is the Mojave Solar Project, located approximately 29
and 24 miles away, respectively.

Silver Lakes, also known as Helendale according to the United States Postal Service, is situated
about 24 miles southwest of Barstow. As per the 2020 US Census11 was 6,317, with a median
income of $106,000. The employment rate stands at about 58%, the median age is 39.5 years,
and 23.7% of residents hold at least a bachelor’s degree.

In-person surveys collected in Silver Lakes, CA
On Saturday, September 14, 2024, 38 surveys were collected over 2.5 hours at the Concert in
the Park event in Silver Lakes Community Park. Surveys were accessible via QR codes on cell
phones and on paper in both Spanish and English. Participants received Kind Bars as
incentives.

Engagement challenges were noted at this event; the gathering commemorating 9/11, had a
conservative and polite audience who were generally reluctant to share their opinions on solar
initiatives. It is speculated that offering a cash incentive perhaps might have improved
participation rates.

Residents of Silver Lakes demonstrated greater familiarity with CSP technologies compared to
those in Barstow and Yermo. Most had observed Ivanpah while traveling to Las Vegas or seen
Solar One and Two in Daggett, though they seemed less aware of trough plant technologies.

Key takeaways from the surveys in Barstow / Yermo and Silver Lakes:
The detailed responses and complete data are available in Appendix 2 , with summarized
insights and notable differences between community responses highlighted. A breakdown in the
response collection process impeded a direct comparison among the three communities, but it
was feasible to contrast Silver Lakes' responses with the combined data from Barstow, Yermo,
and Silver Lakes. Key insights are listed below.

Highlights:
 ● 65% of all respondents said they had a positive or very positive opinion of solar; 25%
 were neither positive nor negative, and about 10.5% were negative or very negative.

 When considering the Silver Lakes community specifically, the support decreased:
 53% were very positive or positive, 31% were neither positive nor negative, and
 about 17% were negative or very negative.

 ● 65% of all respondents responded about the aesthetics of the tower that they either
 liked it or “wouldn’t mind seeing it,” and about 22% wouldn’t want it within their view
 at all.

 In the Silver Lakes surveys, this opinion was lower, with 48% saying they liked the
 way the tower looked or “wouldn’t mind seeing it,” and 31% not wanting it within their
 view at all.

 ● The response regarding the suitability of solar in previously disturbed desert land is
 much higher (62%) than undisturbed desert land (34%) and other previously
 disturbed land, such as farmland (42%).

 The results from Silver Lakes mirror the combined results in the previously disturbed
 desert land, but lower on the undisturbed desert land, with only 28% saying it is
 suitable, and other previously disturbed land with 36%.

 ● 59% respondents said they would support future CSP development in the area, while
 about 13% would not; 28% had no opinion. The Silver Lakes respondents answered
 53% would support future development, 28% would not, and 19% had no opinion.
 The support of future CSP development is significantly less in this area.

 77% of respondents reported knowing what CSP plants were or having heard of
 plants using mirrors, either troughs or towers.

 ● The respondents were presented a list of positive facts regarding ( creates more
 jobs, more American-made components, proven technology, the capability of storing
 more energy than the world’s largest battery, etc.) and whether one would be more
 likely to support it after knowing those facts, and the Silver Lakes community
 consistently came in lower, and in some cases significantly lower, than the combined
 response.

 ● All respondents were asked if they agreed or not with some statements about CSP
 when thinking of the overall benefits, which were presented in the previous
 questions:
 ○ 65% agreed CSP would improve the quality of life in their community, while
 26% had no opinion and 9% disagreed. In Silver Lakes, the no opinion results

 were about the same but the agreement rates were dramatically different,
 with 53% agreeing and 22% disagreeing.
 ○ 72% agreed that CSP would bring economic development to the area,
 whereas 19% had no opinion and almost 9% disagreed. The results were
 similar in Silver Lakes.
 ○ About 70% agreed that CSP would increase American energy independence,
 whereas 22% had no opinion and 8.5% disagreed. The Silver Lakes
 community, however, reported 17% disagreement, significantly higher than
 the overall group.
 ○ 65% agreed that CSP would help limit climate change, with 27% having no
 opinion and about 8.5% disagreeing. Again, the Silver Lakes respondents
 came in significantly different with only 46% agreeing, 37% having no opinion
 and 17% disagreeing

 ● Key concerns brought up in conversations:
 ○ Dust causing major health issues, such as COPD, as well as coating the
 inside of homes
 ○ Clearing makes the land susceptible to weeds and debris brush, which
 contributes to wildfires
 ○ Water
 ○ General concern of impact on wildlife and protected species
 ○ Destruction of the creosote bush and other desert plant life that serve as
 carbon sequestration
 ○ Recycling of solar panels and consequences of companies going bankrupt
 and project end of life
 ○ Lithium battery fires: the fire departments cannot handle them; fumes are
 toxic
 ○ The residents think that they do not receive the solar energy from utility-scale
 solar plants in the area and that it is being sent to Arizona.
 ○ There is a relationship problem with SoCal Edison; there were several
 conversations about residential solar and unfair practices from the utility.

Broad takeaways from a comparison of survey data from 3 communities
(Tonopah, Barstow and Silver Lakes)
The survey used in California differed significantly from the one in Tonopah, NV, making it
challenging to compare results. The California survey was shorter and simpler, easing
participation, while the Tonopah survey gathered more comprehensive data. Despite these
differences, several key questions were consistent across both surveys, revealing that:
 ● The majority of participants view solar energy positively and support future
 development, although Silver Lakes respondents were slightly less favorable.
 ● Participants generally prefer solar development on previously disturbed desert land.
 However, in communities targeted for development, there is acceptance for using
 both disturbed and undisturbed "vacant" desert land, as indicated in Tonopah.

 ● More respondents found the aesthetics of solar tower plants unobjectionable rather
 than displeasing across the surveyed communities.
 ● It is crucial for all three communities that any local solar project be owned by a U.S.
 company, employ local workers, and source materials from U.S. businesses. There
 was a preference for hiring unionized workers in California and Tonopah, but not in
 Silver Lakes. However, these findings should be viewed with caution due to
 variations in survey design.

Lessons learned from Nevada, Arizona and California outreach
This was the first engagement with the communities of Tonopah and Barstow, providing
valuable insights for future efforts:

Conduct onsite pre-engagement activity prep
 ● Visiting the community beforehand can help understand local values, concerns, and
 logistics. Initial discussions with the Tonopah Town Manager might have given an
 overly positive impression of local views.
 ● Check mobile phone coverage before entering the community, preferably with a few
 community members to avoid assumptions made with cell phone company coverage
 maps.
 ● Research the demographics well to make sure the activity is accessible: For the
 California sites, we had the survey available on paper, as well as digital, and we also
 had a Spanish version.
 ● Determine how community members are most comfortable providing feedback,
 whether through paper surveys, online tools, or other means, taking into account
 both technological comfort and trust levels. Generally speaking, there was a sense of
 distrust for using technology in Tonopah, and/or they didn’t use their cell phones as
 much as in an urban setting.
 ● Knowing the political landscape can impact engagement strategies. For example, the
 political dynamics between Tonopah and Pahrump in Nye County or local issues with
 utility providers like SoCal Edison in California could influence community responses
 and expectations.

Be prepared for the unexpected
 ● Despite expectations of adequate mobile phone coverage in the Barstow area,
 Yermo experienced significant coverage issues, partly due to damage from the
 Bridge fire. This underscored the importance of timely information verification and
 checking for unforeseen complications.
 ● Upon arriving in Barstow, we discovered that our ideal engagement site was
 unavailable. We had the Community College as a backup, but hadn’t received
 confirmation. Similarly, in Tonopah, a last-minute location change was necessary.
 These experiences highlight the need for solid backup plans and flexibility in
 engagement efforts.

Go deeper when preparing the activity logistics
 ● Location: Choose the location wisely based on various factors, including the hours
 and who would be likely to pass at those hours, how much time would they have to
 engage, etc. The “mother with small children” population wasn’t captured fully in the
 Tonopah effort because they were unable to take the 10 minutes to participate. This
 might have influenced results, such as the input on the local schools, etc. At the
 concert in Silver Lakes, participation was lower than expected possibly due to the
 nature of the event (a 9/11 memorial).
 ● Incentive: Choose the incentive wisely, including to whom this incentive would most
 attract. Right-size the incentive; cash was definitely the preferred incentive for these
 efforts, but $5 was a little rich for the 5 minutes that it took to answer the simplified
 survey. In hindsight, $2 bills would have sufficed and perhaps more surveys could
 have been completed.
 ● Activity instrument: Surveys should be simplified to a 4-5 grade reading level with
 clear, jargon-free language. Ensure printed surveys are in large font and that
 questions translate well across different formats, such as from online to paper.
 ● Alternative response collection: Most people did not take the time to populate any
 free-form response opportunities, but many shared their thoughts verbally. For
 communities where trust might be an issue, it might be useful for surveyors to
 contemporaneously write down the high-level feedback received from each
 conversation. If the participant feels comfortable, however, their responses could be
 recorded and transcribed via a cell phone.
 ● Take advantage of the ancillary opportunities: Be well-prepared with information
 about the project to address misinformation and educate the community. For
 instance, in Tonopah, the community had unanswered questions about the current
 status of the plant, therefore it was a missed opportunity to convey that information.
 In California, there was keen interest in off-grid living and solar storage solutions,
 which could have led to education on the benefits of large-scale thermal storage.

Other
 ● Continue to do as much as possible in-person. Continuing in-person
 engagements is crucial as they help participants feel heard and appreciated, often
 leading to positive feedback and gratitude from the community.
 ● Use these efforts to build broader relationships: Inform the local agencies about
 the efforts: In Tonopah, two BLM officers shared that they greatly appreciated and
 supported the community engagement work. This helps build relationships, and
 could have been explored in the California efforts.
 ● Messaging: Tailor communication strategies to the unique demographics and
 sentiments of each community. A/B testing of messages can help determine what
 resonates best in different areas, as evidenced by the more conservative responses
 from Silver Lakes compared to other communities.

Recommendations for future community engagement
Transparency and visibility as a context
When a company embarks on the development of a new project, there are certain community
engagement elements that will support its success. The overall philosophy should be “go in
early and often, and stay late.” When companies make decisions around project development,
whether it’s the location of the project, the basic design, agreements with landowners, etc.,
before informing the public of the project, often they will encounter pushback. People do not like
to be told about decisions after the fact, even if they are not a part of the decision-making
process. People perceive decisions “behind closed doors” as having elements of bribery or
corruption, and this breeds opposition. Perhaps Tonopah residents would have been more
positive if the owners/operators of Crescent Dunes had maintained communication despite the
operational challenges. Representatives of the developer, or owner/operator, need to be visible
in the community.

Transparency is also vital when soliciting community input. Clearly communicating that the
community's feedback will inform decision-making is more effective than allowing assumptions
that their input will directly dictate outcomes.

Establishing trust
Often, development teams are not from the community. Thus, forming partnerships with
influential local organizations—such as churches, service groups, educational institutions, and
economic development offices—is essential. Survey results indicate widespread distrust in
federal and state government and national media within the surveyed areas, emphasizing the
value of local alliances.

Initial efforts:
 Pre-engagement visits and interaction: Conduct thorough desktop research followed
 by in-person visits to interact with diverse community members, assess the environment,
 and refine initial assessments.

 Communication strategy and plan development: Develop a communication plan with
 a professional to align the community's values with the developer's goals. Begin testing
 messages during initial community interactions, addressing potentially contentious
 issues like employment qualifications.

 Community needs and values assessment: Utilize various public participation
 methods—interviews, focus groups, surveys, and anonymous hotlines—to identify local
 concerns and priorities.

 Stakeholder and partnership mapping: Identify key local stakeholders and partners,
 including those potentially opposed to the project, to tailor the engagement strategy.
 Incorporate diverse groups and leverage local economic and educational institutions to
 enhance community relations. This could include: state and local economic development
 agencies, local elected officials, education and workforce partners, community

organizations and charities, the appropriate labor leaders for both construction and
operations, local tribes, and other community groups.

When considering partners, it is important to consider how to incorporate diversity,
equity, inclusion and accessibility into the process. Contacting the Minority Business
Development Agency12 Business Center or a local community business diversity
organization, nearby historically black universities or colleges, or other institutions
supporting diversity efforts, including tribal and Hispanic university programs.

Community engagement plan: Develop a comprehensive engagement plan that
encompasses the entire project lifespan that includes a community benefit agreement.13
Interaction with the community should be consistent and prioritize in person
opportunities as much as possible.

Community benefit plan: This plan should reflect local values and incorporate genuine
community input, as highlighted by the unrecognized healthcare needs in Tonopah.
Engage communities on what benefits they actually need, rather than assuming.

The community benefit plan should prioritize the involvement of local labor to ensure that
the economic benefits of the project are experienced directly by the community. This
includes guaranteeing prevailing wages to all workers, which aligns with fair labor
practices and supports the local economy. Engaging with local labor councils or unions
identified in the partnership mapping process can provide valuable insights into the local
labor market and help facilitate the integration of the workforce into the project.

If possible, incorporating apprenticeships and workforce development programs may be
important for financial incentives under the Inflation Reduction Act, and they are an
excellent way to build trust and goodwill, especially in areas where the local population
may lack the necessary skills or educational background for more technical roles. These
programs can serve as a bridge, providing locals with the training they need to take on
various roles within the project. This approach not only helps in filling positions but also
contributes to enhancing employability beyond the life of the project.

Understanding the local workforce's capabilities is important to creating appropriate job
opportunities. If higher-skilled positions cannot be filled locally, the project should
consider roles that do not require specialized skills but are still crucial for the operation.
These roles could include security personnel, grounds maintenance crews, or support
staff for shift changes, among others. This strategy helps mitigate potential backlash
from the community due to unmet expectations of employment.

Justice40, an initiative introduced by President Biden, directs that 40 percent of certain
federal investments, including some DOE funding, flow to disadvantaged communities
(DACs). Even if the project doesn’t receive federal investment, this should be taken into

 account. It is easier to promote Justice40 if the project is located in a disadvantaged
 community. Regarding the communities where outreach was conducted:
 ● Tonopah is considered a disadvantaged community 14 specifically for its low income,
 lack of indoor plumbing, the unemployment rate, and the presence of one or more
 formerly used Defense Site within the immediate area.
 ● Barstow is considered a disadvantaged community 15 because of its low income, high
 rates of unemployment and poverty, low education rate, high rates of asthma and
 diabetes, low life expectancy, housing cost, lack of indoor plumbing, and rate of toxic
 wastewater discharge.
 ● Helendale/Silver Lakes is bordered by disadvantaged communities 16 and those
 surrounding it fit the DAC criteria primarily for the low income, high wildfire risk,
 building loss rate, transportation barriers, toxic wastewater discharge, and low
 education rates.

 In order to fulfill Justice40, explore DAC communities to see where you could recruit
 your workforce and locate the projects’ supply chain. All community engagement
 activities need to be created through a DEIA lens to make sure that everyone is being
 considered and the project is a sustainable, positive partner with the community.

 Continued engagement: Maintain engagement throughout the project's lifecycle,
 planning regular activities to sustain the relationships and the trust that was established
 initially. Continuous dialogue can safeguard the project's reputation and long-term
 success, preventing the erosion of community support, like the situation seen in
 Tonopah.

Conclusion
Community acceptance is crucial for the success of renewable energy projects. Insights from
public opinion surveys on Concentrated Solar Power (CSP) in Arizona, Nevada, and California
emphasize the need for early and robust community engagement.

In Tonopah, NV, near a solar power tower, the importance of transparency—especially
regarding local hiring, consistent communication, and community involvement—was evident.
When communication ceased, residents filled the void with negative rumors and hearsay.
Continuous engagement might have revealed local healthcare needs, possibly integrating them
into an iteration of a community benefit plan, thereby strengthening community ties and
enhancing the company's reputation.

The preliminary site visit to Kingman, AZ highlighted local concerns and values that needed
addressing before further project interactions. Early engagement helps developers spot
potential issues, establishing a solid foundation for positive community relations. Additionally,
identifying deal-breaking concerns early can prevent costly development missteps.

Interaction with the Barstow and Silver Lakes communities underscored the importance of
understanding the community prior to engagement and testing various messages before

initiating participation activities. For example, a different venue or more appealing incentives at
Silver Lakes might have improved engagement rates. While initial outreach provided valuable
insights, further research would be needed before pre-development engagement.

Despite the differences, all three communities generally view solar energy positively and are
likely to support future projects.

 Title: Avian and Bat Health Concerns Page: 2 of 6 Doc. No.: DOE-SD-09809 - 4

Introduction
The effect of Central Receiver Concentrator Solar Power (CSP) plants on avian health and mortality has
been a subject of serious interest among various stakeholders including state and local governments,
environmental groups, and wildlife conservationists. There has been significant misinformation in the
media about this topic, which has led to significant concern from the public about the potential impact
of CSP plants. The wind power industry has had a long history dealing with this issue as well and in
many ways has paved a path for other renewables including CSP demonstrating 1) that the developers
of these technologies are sensitive to the issues and 2) have taken significant steps to ensure they are
not a significant threat to avian health. A similar approach is being taken with Central Receiver CSP
plants.

Avian Interactions with Central Receiver CSP Plants
Bird populations affected include both those native to the localities of these plants and migratory birds
whose migratory flight paths come within the vicinity of the plants. Regarding CSP Power Tower plants
in particular, the characteristics of the technology that have been identified as potentially having
negative impacts on bird populations include the power tower itself as an obstacle contributing to bird
collisions. The heliostat field is also considered to be a potential source of bird collisions. However, the
main concern with CSP Power Towers is the presence of concentrated solar flux levels above the
heliostat field and near the tower/receiver with potential to singe birds flying into these zones causing
harm or death. This issue has been the subject of much research and investigation. One challenge
regarding the high flux issues is understanding what level of flux birds can tolerate over a given period
of time. Limited biological research has suggested a threshold value of 4 kW/m 2 (or approximately 4
suns concentration). More rigorous research is needed to better understand the effects and limits.
When plants are “off-sun”, that is not in concentrating operational mode, there is no high flux hazard.
Collisions are the only source of possible harm to birds. It is when the plants are operational either in
power generation mode or stand-by mode that these zones of concentrated sunlight come into play. In
power generation mode, all or most of the concentrated flux is generated directly at the receiver.
There is little that can be done to mitigate these concentrated flux zones around the receiver while in
operational mode. In stand-by mode, when the heliostats are not aiming light at the receiver,
heliostats are aimed at zones around or above the receiver so that they can quickly be moved to power
generation mode when conditions warrant it. Research done by Sandia National Laboratories and the
National Renewable Energy Laboratory1 has demonstrated that certain stand-by aimpoint strategies
can reduce the extent and concentration of these zones to below the 4kW/m 2 threshold while
maintaining annual performance at satisfactory levels. It is expected that with these strategies the
danger to avian health can be significantly reduced.

1
 Evaluation of Avian Solar-Flux Hazards and Mitigation Measures at the Ivanpah Concentrating Solar Power Plant Clifford K.
Ho, Timothy Wendelin, Luke Horstman, and Cianan Sims.

 Title: Avian and Bat Health Concerns Page: 3 of 6 Doc. No.: DOE-SD-09809 - 4

As mentioned, the CSP industry has taken these issues into serious consideration expending
considerable time, effort and resources to understand the extent to which avian health and mortality is
affected by these plants. Four CSP power tower plants currently exist in the southwest United States,
the three Ivanpah Solar Electric Generating Systems (SEGS) plants in California near Primm, Nevada
and the Crescent Dunes plant near Tonopah, Nevada. The plants have implemented stand-by aimpoint
strategies to minimize the possible danger to birds. In addition, plant operations at these sites have
included extensive surveying of bird casualties over their years of operation.
Solar One Demonstration Plant
Data also exists for Solar One, a Department of Energy power tower research project that existed near
Daggett, CA from 1982 to 1983. A 40-week study performed at Solar One during its years of operation
reported 70 total bird deaths during that time. Most of these were collisions with mirrors or the tower.
A small percentage of these deaths showed any signs of singeing or burning of feathers. The
conclusion was the impact on the local bird population was minimal.
Ivanpah Commercial Plant
Ivanpah, which includes 3 separate tower plants, each over 1 square mile in size, having operated since
~2013, has much more extensive reporting. They conducted regular systematic surveys via third party
agencies to assess avian casualties and have published reports since 2017 2 detailing the purpose,
process and analytical results. These results are submitted to the U.S. Fish and Wildlife Service as well
as the California Department of Fish and Wildlife. The fatality index model, developed from survey data
of bird fatalities gathered during the first 4 years of operation, predicts 504 deaths on average would
be detected with the 95% confidence interval in the range of 276-746 incidences. 3 If fatalities remain
in this range, it was determined that there is a low potential for the mortality at the Ivanpah plants to
impact bird populations considering both local and migratory species. During the 2021-2022
monitoring year, the most recent data available, 259 fatalities were recorded involving both local and
migratory species. In general, the plant has observed a decreasing trend in bird fatalities detected in
recent years. The current value is below the 95% confidence interval, seeming to indicate a reduction
in fatalities observed at the plant. Over the years, Ivanpah has implemented more effective stand-by
aiming strategies as well as bird deterrents including acoustic methods (predatory sounds to frighten
away birds), visual methods (intense lights, decoys), tactile methods (spikes, anti-perching devices) and
chemosensory methods. The overall effects of these deterrents are hard to quantify, but ultimately the

2
  Ivanpah WEST Annual Monitoring Reports 2017-2022, California Energy Commission.
https://efiling.energy.ca.gov/Lists/DocketLog.aspx?docketnumber=07-AFC-05C.
3
  Avian & Bat Monitoring and Management Plan (ABMMP) -Ivanpah Solar Electric Generating System. 2020. Revision 14.
February 21. Available at: California Energy Commission Docket TN#235408
https://efiling.energy.ca.gov/GetDocument.aspx?tn=235408&DocumentContentId=68295

 Title: Avian and Bat Health Concerns Page: 4 of 6 Doc. No.: DOE-SD-09809 - 4

survey conclusions above suggest that the plants are successfully reducing their impact on both local
and migratory avian populations.
Crescent Dunes
Data for Crescent Dunes is limited, but one data point that can be mentioned is that because of the
SNL/NREL analysis, operators spread the aim point pattern at stand-by to reduce the peak flux below 4
kW/m2. Over several months following this change zero bird fatalities were reported. Crescent Dunes
is also required to conduct periodic site surveys to monitor avian impacts.

New Avian Mitigation Approaches
While the argument can be made based on the statistical data that with existing operational strategies
and deterrents, these plants are not adversely affecting bird populations, work is still ongoing to
improve avian detection methods at these plants. Sandia National Laboratories is developing a state-
of-the-art detection system that detects and identifies individual bird species near the tower/receiver.
This automated, deep-learning approach will provide much more data on the numbers and types of
birds that venture into potentially hazardous areas. Ultimately such a system could be combined with
advanced deterrent approaches and further reduce CSP plant impacts on avian health.
Finally, there is an open question about whether the native flora should be cleared or largely left intact
when the heliostat field is constructed. Leaving the plants in place provides habitats that could attract
avian species into the plant during operation. Clearing the native flora may help reduce avian
interactions with the plant. Additionally, it may be possible to select sites with less potential for avian
interactions.

Future Plant Avian Mitigation Plan
For future plants, the following recommendations should help significantly mitigate the impact of
Central Receiver Tower CSP plants on local and migratory avian species.
 - Appropriate site selection – screen areas with extensive avian activity.
 - Reduce onsite avian habitat – clearing of potential avian habitat.
 - Safe heliostat standby operations – implement improved operation procedures
 - Design plants to minimize perching locations and consider other features to minimize avian
 impacts. Heliostat design could consider options for mitigating bird impacts.
 - Bird deterrents: sound, lasers, scents, decoys.
 - Scanning near receiver – Monitoring of the high flux zones near receiver during operation and
 using AI avian detection, can be used to defocus heliostats if important species are detected.
 - Drone based surveys. Drone surveys using machine learning could be used to do more timely
 and geographically complete surveys of avian fatalities.

 Title: Avian and Bat Health Concerns Page: 5 of 6 Doc. No.: DOE-SD-09809 - 4

CSP Plants and Bat Mortality
Wind farms tend to have bigger impact on bats than on avian species. A typical 100 MW wind farm
might cause ~300–1,000 bat fatalities per year, depending on habitat, region, turbine operation, and
mitigation. Bats are highly mobile nocturnal mammals that use echolocation to navigate and forage.
Wind turbines—especially large modern ones—pose risks to bats primarily through collision mortality
(from rotating blades) and barotrauma (pressure-related injuries). These impacts can have population-
level consequences, particularly for migratory or long-lived species with low reproductive rates. The
best practices from U.S. Fish and Wildlife Service (USFWS) and recent peer-reviewed studies
concerning bat fatalities at wind power projects, recommend:
 • Avoidance: siting away from high-use bat habitats, migration corridors, known roosts.
 • Minimization: during operation consider curtailment, monitoring, adaptive management.
 • Monitoring & adaptive management: post-construction fatality monitoring and adjusting
 operations if impacts are high.
For Central Receiver CSP plants, the primary concern is the high flux zones between the heliostats and
the central receiver, with the flux increasing closer to the receiver. Since bats are generally nocturnal,
the high flux is not a risk. The main concern is related to the air-cooled condensers (ACC) in the power
block that have large fans on while the plant is operating. The Ivanpah project with 3 power plants
totaling 392 MW reported between 1 and 15 bat mortalities annually between 2017 and 2021. Given
the number of bat mortalities detected, the project is not considered to have a detrimental impact on
local or migratory bat populations.
